Versioning & Identity of Structured Material — A Per-Paper Research Reading

For the Softland code ingestor and object-container kernel · 2026-06-11

What this is

We started at a narrow question — what is the identity of a code file, and how should the code ingestor store it — and it opened, correctly, into a much older and larger one: what is the identity and versioning of any structured material, at multiple granularities at once. A function, a grouping of functions, a knowledge graph that must version at graph / node / relation-type / node-data level simultaneously — these are one problem, and people with names like McCarthy, Kay, and Hickey are only the visible surface of a literature that has worked it since the 1980s.

This document is the deep reading. Twenty-four sources — research papers and production systems, famous and obscure — were each read in full, section by section, then mapped back to our parts. The structure of every entry is the same:

  • The paper, section by section — what each section actually argues, builds, proves, or measures, in the authors' own words. This is the substance; we do not skip to conclusions.
  • How it maps to our parts — a zoom-out: which Softland concern (the object-container kernel, the code-identity question, versioning-as-its-own-layer, groupings, four-level graph versioning, the trail-to-code join, preserved disagreement, the Rama substrate) this paper supports, names, warns against, or hands a mechanism.
  • Takeaway — the single most important thing it contributes.

At the end of each of the five angles there is a "What we can gather" synthesis, and the document closes with an overall synthesis and the concrete amendments it implies for the slice-1 code-ingestor contract.

Calibration

The per-section content was produced by 24 parallel deep-reader agents (Opus), one per source, each instructed to read the full text — chasing an open-access copy when the publisher gated it — and to flag inference versus what it read. Twenty-three were read at full text; one (Sciore 1994) via an open secondary source; one (TerminusDB) partially. This is a reading, not a peer-reviewed survey: quotes are reproduced as the readers extracted them, and where a mapping is an analogy rather than a claim from the source, the reader was told to say so. Treat it as a high-quality literature map with citations to follow, not as settled fact.

The five angles

  1. Program-unit identity — what is a code unit across time (Unison, Molhado, MolhadoRef, Monticello).
  2. Version-model theory — what is versioning, formally (Conradi-Westfechtel, DSST persistence, Pijul, the categorical and homotopical patch theories, Darcs).
  3. Versioned databases — the two-layer pattern in production (Irmin, Dolt/Prolly, the version-controlled-database category, XTDB bitemporality).
  4. Graph & RDF versioning — the multi-level case, stress-tested (TerminusDB, OSTRICH, the BEAR benchmark, the two-track RDF-archiving framework, Wikidata history).
  5. Composite & assembly versioning — the mature, solved part (Katz, ORION, Sciore, PLM/PDM effectivity).

Angle 1 — Program-Unit Identity: What Is a Code Unit Across Time?

1. The Big Idea (Unison documentation)

Unison Computing (Paul Chiusano, Rúnar Bjarnason, et al.) · 2024 · unison-lang.org official documentation

In one line. Each Unison definition is identified by the hash of its normalized syntax tree; names are mutable metadata pointing at immutable, content-addressed code.

Source access: full. Read the full public doc page at https://www.unison-lang.org/docs/the-big-idea/ via WebFetch (two passes: full-text extraction, then per-heading verbatim quotes). This is a documentation page, not a peer-reviewed paper, so I used its real section headings rather than paper-style sections. Not gated.

The paper, section by section

§ The big idea (core claim). States the single founding axiom: every Unison definition is identified by a hash of its syntax tree, not by a file, path, or name. The hash is a 512-bit SHA3 over a NORMALIZED form of the AST: all named arguments are replaced by positionally-numbered variable references (so alpha-equivalent definitions hash identically), and every dependency reference is replaced by the hash of that dependency. This makes the hash a Merkle-style content address that recursively captures the full transitive meaning of the definition while being insensitive to naming. Names are explicitly NOT part of the hash; they are separately stored metadata that map onto hashes.

“Each Unison definition is identified by a hash of its syntax tree.”
“all named arguments are replaced by positionally-numbered variable references, and all dependencies...are replaced by their hashes”
“names are just separately stored metadata that don't affect the function's hash”

§ Benefits (overview). Frames the rest of the document as consequences of the one starting assumption. The claim is that content-addressing is not a niche optimization but a root cause whose downstream effects are broad: it simplifies distributed programming, eliminates builds and dependency conflicts, enables typed durable storage, supports structured refactorings, and unlocks richer codebase tooling. The rhetorical move is that all later sections are derivations from the single identity axiom rather than independent features.

“This starting assumption provides some surprising benefits: it simplifies distributed programming, eliminates builds and dependency conflicts, supports typed durable storage, structured refactorings, enables better tools for working with code, and lots more.”

§ Simplifying distributed programming. Argues that because a definition's identity is a content hash, computations are location-independent and self-describing. Code (as bytecode referencing hashes) can be shipped from one node to another, and any dependencies the receiving node is missing can be identified by hash and deployed on the fly, because the hash unambiguously names exactly the needed definition. There is no risk of the wrong version being present, since the hash IS the version. This turns distributed deployment into hash-resolution plus on-demand sync rather than environment matching.

“In Unison, since definitions are identified by a content hash, arbitrary computations can just be moved from one location to another, with missing dependencies deployed on the fly.”

§ No builds. Establishes immutability as the consequence of content-addressing: since a definition's hash is computed from its content, the definition behind a given hash can never change. What CAN change is the name-to-hash mapping (the basis of refactoring and code evolution), but never the content under a hash. Because of this, parsing and typechecking results can be cached permanently against the hash and never invalidated, so there is effectively no build step. The codebase ships with a perfect, permanent compilation cache.

“Because Unison definitions are identified by their hash, they never change. We may change which names are associated with which hashes (and this is used for Unison's approach to refactoring and code evolution), but the definition associated with a hash never changes.”

§ No dependency conflicts. Diagnoses dependency hell as fundamentally a naming-collision problem: conflicts arise only because two different definitions compete for the same name. Since Unison references dependencies by hash rather than name, two libraries depending on different versions of the same logical definition simply reference two different hashes that coexist without conflict. The version/name competition that produces diamond-dependency failures in conventional ecosystems is structurally dissolved; a name clash becomes a resolvable metadata question, not a hard runtime conflict.

“Dependency conflicts are, fundamentally, due to different definitions "competing" for the same names. But why do we care if two different definitions use the same name?”
“definitions are referenced by hash...so dependency conflicts...are just not a thing”

§ Typed, durable storage. Because definitions never change, any value at all (including closures and values that contain functions) can be serialized and later deserialized without the programmer hand-writing an encoder/decoder or pinning a serialization format. The runtime can persist a value by reference to the immutable hashes of all code it transitively depends on, and rehydrate it later with type information intact. This makes durable, typed storage a property of the identity model rather than a separate library, and it stays sound across time because the referenced definitions are immutable.

“In Unison, any value at all (including functions or values that contain functions) can be persisted and unpersisted at a later time, without the programmer needing to manually specify a serialization format or write an encoder and decoder.”
“Definitions never change.”

§ Richer codebase tools. Reframes the codebase itself as a structured database rather than a folder of text files. Because every definition is hashed, typed, and has fully known dependencies, the codebase can maintain a perfect compilation cache, a complete dependency graph, type-indexed search (find by type signature), and other indices that conventional text-file codebases cannot reliably build. Tooling becomes queryable over a content-addressed store with strong invariants, instead of re-parsing and guessing from filesystem layout.

“The Unison codebase is a proper database which knows the type of everything it stores, has a perfect compilation cache, perfect knowledge of dependencies, indices for type-based search, and more.”

§ Structured refactoring. Explains how evolution works given immutability: you never edit code in place, you change which names map to which hashes within structured refactoring sessions. Adding or modifying a definition produces a NEW hash; the refactoring session tracks the set of names being re-pointed and surfaces exactly what must be updated, keeping the codebase in a never-broken state throughout. This contrasts sharply with the conventional loop of mutating text files and then chasing a cascade of misleading compile errors and failing tests, which Unison characterizes as both error-prone and misleading.

“In Unison, changing what names are mapped to which hashes is done with structured refactoring sessions, rather than the typical approach of just mutating text files in place and then fixing a long list of misleading compile errors and failed tests.”
“the codebase is never in a broken state”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread; REVISIONS = immutable values inside; 'inferred structure never accepted as truth at ingest.' — GIVES A NAME + MECHANISM. Unison's hash-of-AST IS an immutable revision value; names are the mutable pointer (the container/identity thread). The split maps near-exactly onto our container-vs-revision split: hash = revision (content), name-binding = container head (current-revision). But Unison inverts our 'inferred structure never truth' rule: the hash is DERIVED (normalized AST) yet treated AS the truth. Reconcile by noting Unison's hash is a DECLARED canonical form computed deterministically at authoring, not an inference reconstructed by diffing — so it does not violate our rule, it sharpens what counts as 'declared.'
  • The code-identity question: file vs path vs name/FQN vs normalized AST vs git hash; atomic unit = file or function? — DIRECTLY ANSWERS IT. Unison's whole thesis is the strongest possible vote: identity = hash of the normalized AST of a single DEFINITION (function), with names/paths/files demoted to metadata. The atomic unit is the function, not the file; the file is not even a storage primitive (the codebase is a database). This is the maximal-purity end of our design axis — useful as the reference pole even if we adopt a weaker version.
  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history (Rama, partial persistence) vs committed/minted versions (git-style, full persistence). Two times. — SUPPORTS + WARNS. Supports the separation: Unison literally separates the immutable content layer (hashes never change) from the mutable naming/refactoring layer (which names map to which hashes) — exactly our 'content revision vs committed pointer' two-layer idea. Warns: Unison collapses 'when-it-was-true' into content-addressing and has NO arrival-time axis at all; it gets reproducibility but loses cheap time-travel over edit history. Our Rama arrival-log gives the axis Unison lacks; we should keep both.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated local world / grouping has its own version history, members pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped. — OFFERS A MECHANISM. Unison's 'namespace' is a versioned map from names to hashes — i.e. a grouping whose identity is independent of its members, and whose members are PINNED by hash. This is concretely our 'pinned member binding.' What Unison lacks is floating/effectivity-scoped binding (a name always resolves to one hash within a namespace version), so it models the pinned case well and shows the cost: refactoring becomes a structured session because pins must be re-pointed deliberately.
  • Four-level graph versioning: graph / node / relation-type / node-data, composing. — PARTIAL MECHANISM + GAP. Unison cleanly versions node-DATA (the definition body -> hash) and graph-level (namespace version) and composes them (a namespace hash is built from member hashes, dependencies replaced by their hashes -> a Merkle DAG, structural composition for free). But it has no notion of relation-TYPE versioning (edges are just 'depends-on' by hash). Lesson: Merkle composition gives us node-data + graph levels almost free, but typed-edge versioning is our own problem to solve.
  • Trail-to-code join: resolve a trail's path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; DECLARED vs INFERRED change. — WARNS / SHOWS TENSION. Unison resolves identity by hash, not path+timestamp — the opposite of our trail join key. Agent trails carry PATHS and edit ops (mutable, name-space coordinates); Unison says those coordinates are unstable metadata. Implication: if we hash-address code units, a trail's recorded path may no longer resolve, so we must keep the name/path->hash mapping AS OF the trail timestamp (Unison's namespace-version is exactly the table to snapshot). Reinforces preferring DECLARED change (the agent names the unit) over path-diff inference.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicting views representable, synthesis late-bound. — OFFERS A MECHANISM. Because two definitions can share a name without conflict (the name is just metadata, the hashes coexist), Unison structurally HOLDS competing definitions side by side — 'dependency conflicts are different definitions competing for the same name.' Content-addressing lets plural versions coexist unmerged; a name conflict is surfaced for late-bound resolution in a refactoring session rather than silently overwritten. This is a working model of preserved disagreement at the code layer.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced, immutable values, durable typed storage. — SUPPORTS / CONVERGENT. Unison's 'typed durable storage' (any value persists/unpersists with no hand-written codec because definitions never change) is the same bet as Rama's immutable event log: identity by content, no serialization drift, perfect compilation/dependency cache. Confirms that an immutable-value substrate buys reproducibility and a 'codebase as database' — validating our Rama-as-truth posture for the code layer.

Takeaway. Unison is the canonical, fully-realized answer to our code-identity question: identity = hash of the normalized AST of a single definition (function), with names, paths, and files demoted to mutable metadata pointing at immutable content. For Softland this is the reference pole on the identity axis and a direct architectural gift — it splits cleanly into our container-vs-revision model (hash = immutable revision, name-binding = container head), gives us Merkle composition for node-data and graph-level versioning nearly for free, and proves an immutable-value substrate buys reproducibility, a codebase-as-database, and structural plurality (competing definitions coexist under one name). Its key WARNING: Unison has no arrival-time axis and addresses code by hash rather than path+timestamp, so adopting it would break our trail-to-code path join unless we snapshot the name/path-to-hash table as-of each trail moment — which is exactly the second (Rama arrival-time / namespace-version) layer Unison lacks and we must keep.

2. On Product Versioning for Hypertexts

Tien N. Nguyen, Cheng Thao, Ethan V. Munson · 2005 · SCM (Software Configuration Management Workshop) — ACM / Springer LNCS

In one line. Molhado is the first hypertext-versioning system to apply the product-versioning model, using a node/slot/attribute data model (Fluid IR) and a single global version space so that documents, fine-grained logical units (AST/XML nodes), first-class links, and hypermedia structure are all versioned uniformly and resolved at a 'current version.'

Source access: full. Read the full PDF directly from the author/UCSC mirror (users.soe.ucsc.edu/~ejw/scm12/papers/Nguyen-Chapter9.pdf). WebFetch could not parse the binary, so the PDF was downloaded and text-extracted locally with pdftotext -layout (962 lines, all sections + references recovered). This is the SCM 2005 paper, a sibling of the Molhado Hypertext Versioning System work (Nguyen/Munson/Boyland/Thao, Hypertext 2004, ref [3]); the deeper node/slot/attribute and per-attribute fine-grained versioning machinery the prompt hints at lives in [3] and Nguyen's PhD thesis [32], which this paper summarizes and applies to hypertext.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States that versioned hypermedia helps manage evolving document collections but raises structural and cognitive issues. Claims Molhado as 'the first hypermedia infrastructure that applies the product versioning model to versioned hypermedia.' Molhado supports fine-grained configuration management of hypermedia structure AND version control for individual hyperlinks and document nodes, and the paper argues this addresses issues raised by prior versioned-hypermedia research (notably Osterbye) while introducing new issues.

“Molhado, the first hypermedia infrastructure that applies the product versioning model to versioned hypermedia”
“provides version control for individual hyperlinks and document nodes”

§ 1. Introduction. Defines hypertext versioning as storing/retrieving/navigating prior states of a hypertext and letting collaborators develop new states. Motivates with three domains: software engineering (capturing evolution of relationships between requirements/design/implementation artifacts, not just the artifacts themselves), legal systems (the version of a law in effect at the time of an infraction matters), and the Web (rolling a site back to a specific time). Names the structural issues (immutability of versions, version control for links, versioning for hypermedia structure) and cognitive issues (version creation, element version selection) from Osterbye. Introduces Molhado as using 'a single versioning mechanism for all software components and for the hypertext structures' that can return to a consistent prior state 'not only of a hypertext network but also of a single node or link,' supporting both embedded (HTML) and first-class (XLink) hyperlinks.

“Molhado can track changes at a very fine-grained level, allowing users to return to a consistent previous state not only of a hypertext network but also of a single node or link”
“Molhado uses a single versioning mechanism for all software components and for the hypertext structures (including anchors and links) that connect them”

§ 2. Related work. Lays out three version-control approaches and positions product versioning against the other two. (a) COMPOSITION model: atomic components (usually files) each have a version history; composites are defined by Revision Selection Rules (RSRs). Systems: Xanadu, KMS, DIF, Hyperform; HyperWeb and HyperCASE built on RCS where 'the smallest versionable information unit is a file.' RSRs make structure hard to analyze without executing the rules, prompting 'current context' notions (Neptune, CoVer, VerSE) with maintenance overhead. (b) TOTAL versioning: every item — composite and atomic — has its own version space; PCTE recursively copies the whole composition hierarchy; suffers the 'version proliferation problem' where a leaf change spawns versions of all ancestors/linked nodes. (c) PRODUCT versioning: 'establishes a single view of a software product... by arranging versions of all items in a uniform, global version space.' Popular in SCM (change-based COV, Aide-de-camp, PIE; state-based Voodoo; Wagner's infrastructure). Molhado is positioned as the only versioned-hypermedia system applying product versioning.

“product versioning establishes a single view of a software product... by arranging versions of all items in a uniform, global version space”
“the version proliferation problem, where each small change in a leaf of the hierarchy creates versions of multiple objects higher in the compositional hierarchy”
“in which the smallest versionable information unit is a file”

§ 3. Issues with versioning for hypertexts (Osterbye). Catalogs the structural and cognitive problems Molhado must answer. 3.1 IMMUTABILITY: a frozen version's contents must be immutable, but it is unclear whether new annotation/comment links to/from it should force a new version, whereas semantically substantial links should. 3.2 VERSION CONTROL FOR LINKS: not versioning individual links blocks adoption in software engineering; three sub-problems — (i) navigation ambiguity when a link has versions pointing to different destinations (v1 A→B, v2 A→C), (ii) modifying a target B→B' forces a new link version because links and resources have separate histories, (iii) whether a link is a first-class object or a relation of anchor values. 3.3 STRUCTURE: must allow returning to a consistent prior state of the WHOLE network, not just a node or link. 3.4 ELEMENT SELECTION (cognitive): determining which element in a versioned group a link points to (specific element, whole group, or current); query-language selection imposes cognitive overhead and discourages linking; debates where to store selection rules (on structure vs on containment arcs). 3.5 VERSION CREATION (cognitive): forcing users to name every node or explicitly create/maintain versions distracts from the real work.

“The fact that existing hypermedia systems for software development do not version individual links is a significant factor preventing their wider use”
“links and resources have their own version histories”
“If a selection query must be specified each time a link is created, the users are discouraged from making links”

§ 4.1 Data model (Fluid IR). Describes Molhado's underlying data model: the Fluid Internal Representation, whose core concepts are node, slot, attribute, and sequence. 'A node is the basic unit of identity' representing any abstraction; 'a slot is a location that can store a value in any data type, possibly a reference to a node'; 'an attribute is a mapping from nodes to slots,' optionally with per-node slots and a default slot. The model is an attribute table: rows = nodes, columns = attributes, cells = slots. Slots have three kinds: CONSTANT (immutable, set once), SIMPLE (reassignable), and VERSIONED (different values in different versions = slot revisions). A sequence is a same-type slot container with a unique id. Adding versioning gives 'the attribute table a third dimension: the version.' This is the paper's most load-bearing identity machinery.

“A node is the basic unit of identity and is used to represent any abstraction”
“An attribute is a mapping from nodes to slots”
“The third kind of slot is the versioned slot, which may have different values in different versions (slot revisions)”
“the attribute table gets a third dimension: the version”

§ 4.2 Product versioning. Molhado versions the whole project, not individual components: 'All system objects including (atomic and composite) components and hypertext structures are versioned in a uniform, global version space.' Properties can be declared versioned or un-versioned. 'A version is global across the whole project and is a point in a tree-structured discrete time abstraction' — the table's third dimension is tree-structured, moving discretely. Only captured versions (v1.0, v2.0...) are retrievable later; the 'current version' designates the current project state, and setting it rewinds the whole project. Edits to versioned objects create a TEMPORARY version branching off current, recorded only if explicitly captured. To record one object's history, the whole project is captured — efficient because 'the versioning system only records changes and works at a very small granularity.'

“A version is global across the whole project and is a point in a tree-structured discrete time abstraction, rather than being a particular state of an object”
“Changes made to versioned objects of the project at the current version create a temporary version, branching off the current version”
“Capturing the whole project is quite efficient because the versioning system only records changes and works at a very small granularity”

§ 4.3 Structure-oriented representation. To version at fine granularity, each document is decomposed into 'structural units or logical units' (as in SGML/XML structured-document research), represented as a document tree or graph where each node encodes one logical unit. XML is used for non-program artifacts (syntax defined by DTD/XSchema); 'for a program, an abstract syntax tree (AST) perfectly represents its logical structure.' Trees/graphs are built from nodes/slots/attributes: a 'children' attribute maps a node to a sequence of its children (directed graph), a 'parent' attribute adds tree structure. Molhado has 'a fine-grained versioning scheme for tree and directed graph data structures' recording fine-grained changes to structure AND to associated slot contents. XML example: each node carries an 'operator' (intermediate or text) defining its syntactic type and children; text nodes get a 'content' slot for CDATA; element nodes get a slot per XML attribute. The tree is versioned by the tree-based scheme.

“each document is considered to be logically structured into fine units, called structural units or logical units”
“For a program, an abstract syntax tree (AST) perfectly represents its logical structure”
“fine-grained changes to the structure of a tree or a graph and to the contents of associated slots are recorded”

§ 4.4 Hypermedia entities. Defines the hypermedia layer over the document trees/graphs. An 'href' attribute on each node holds a URL to a document node, enabling embedded HTML-style links to/from any node. The first-class model has four concepts: LINKBASE (a container for hypertext networks and/or other linkbases — like a directory to a file), HYPERTEXT NETWORK (contains links and anchors, belongs to one linkbase), LINK ('a first-class entity and is an association among a set of anchors'), and ANCHOR (denotes a region of interest, refers to a document or document node, may belong to multiple links/networks). Crucially, separating anchors from document nodes 'allows for the separation between hypertext networks and document contents.' Links and anchors can carry arbitrary attribute-value pairs.

“A link is a first-class entity and is an association among a set of anchors”
“This separation between anchors and document nodes allows for the separation between hypertext networks and document contents”

§ 4.5 Version control for hypermedia structure. Provides structure versioning at coarse and fine grain. Coarse: a composite component contains atomic and/or other composite components. Fine: an atomic component may have internal structure containing logical units. A linkbase is a composite component whose internals are a tree (versioned by the tree scheme); a hypertext network is an atomic component whose internals are a directed graph where each link or anchor is a node, a directed edge connects an anchor node to a link node it belongs to, and a 'ref' attribute on each anchor node references a component or logical unit (not a network/linkbase). Figure 6 walks two versions v1/v2: nodes added/deleted (node 5 deleted with doc3 removal, nodes 9/10 created), the 'ref' cell updated, and the 'content' slot of a section changing from 'old' to 'new' — all recorded via directed-graph versioning. Returning to a prior version restores documents, linkbases, and networks together.

“A hypertext network is implemented as a type of atomic component, whose internal structure is a directed graph. Each link or anchor is represented by a node in that graph”
“The content slot of n(section1) has changed from old to new to reflect the change in the content”
“returning to previous state of entire project is a basic versioning operation”

§ 4.6 Version control for links. Shows the directed-graph scheme inherently versions individual links (each is a graph node whose connection structure and slots are captured over time), and resolves all three of Osterbye's link problems. Navigation ambiguity (Fig 1) disappears because 'the destination of the link will be automatically chosen at the current version' — the global version disambiguates. The target-modification cascade (Fig 2) disappears because all entities live in one global version space; 'link traversal occurs only among nodes at the current version.' The first-class-vs-relation question: a link IS a first-class node; when a versioned attribute, the anchor connection, or an underlying document node changes, 'a new global version point is created in the tree-structured discrete time line, rather than a new version of resources or a new version of links.' Net: per-link and per-node version control without the problems of composition/total models.

“the destination of the link will be automatically chosen at the current version”
“link traversal occurs only among nodes at the current version”
“a new global version point is created in the tree-structured discrete time line, rather than a new version of resources or a new version of links”

§ 4.7 Revisiting other issues. Resolves the remaining Osterbye issues. IMMUTABILITY: users designate 'substantial' links (modeled as versioned slots via 'href') whose change forces a new version, vs annotation/comment links (simple slots) that change without a new version — leveraging Molhado's ability to declare aspects versioned/un-versioned and its single global space. First-class links+anchors pointing into documents do create a version (treated as annotations on top of content), but the underlying components are unchanged between the two versions. ELEMENT SELECTION: because 'an individual entity do not have its own version numbers' and the project's current version is selected implicitly (mouse) or explicitly (commands), there are NO selection rules/queries on links, anchors, or networks — easing GUI construction (used in the Software Concordance). VERSION CREATION: entities share one version space, so users commit the whole project rather than checking nodes/links in and out individually; intermediate session versions support undo. Caveat: 'a single change to a versioned entity might potentially create a new version of entire project.'

“users are allowed to specify the set of substantial links (i.e. the ones that if modified would create a new version)”
“an individual entity do not have its own version numbers”
“a single change to a versioned entity might potentially create a new version of entire project”

§ 5. The versioned hypermedia tool. Describes the implementation as library functions in two groups: (1) linkbase/network services (create, delete, re-structure, relocate, open, activate, import/export to XLink at any version) and (2) link/anchor services (create, delete, rename, view attributes, view history; add/remove/rename anchors, show the structural unit an anchor refers to). Integrated into the Software Concordance (SC) environment with CM handled by Molhado's CM infrastructure. User operations: open a project, pick a current version from a history window, browse/edit the directory and documents; any edit at the current version creates a temporary branch version held until a 'capture' or 'commit' (capture attaches a unique name, date, authors, descriptions and acts as a retrievable checkpoint). Supports branching by switching current version and 'multi-version editing' (working on several versions in one session). Commit saves chosen temporary versions storing only differences. Structured editors (Java, XML, HTML, SVG, UML) are link-aware; users can view a single document node's history (Fig 10: 'loadDelta' not created until v9.1, earlier versions disabled) and a hyperlink's history (Fig 11), and edit networks with causal vs non-causal links (Fig 12). Defining a new network does not alter document content (structure separated from content).

“This switching feature allows the user to work on many versions at the same time during one session. This capability is called multi-version editing”
“The capture command changes a temporary version into a captured one”
“Only the differences are stored. Saving complete version snapshots can improve version access time”

§ 6. Issues for future research. Identifies three problems unique to applying product versioning that composition/total systems did not face. 6.1 HYPERLINKS ACROSS VERSIONS: links exist only within a single version, so there is 'no way to say make a link to document D at version vk'; two fixes proposed — computational links whose anchors are queries against a link database ('get document D for version vk'), or adding a version identifier to all access interfaces (rejected as inconvenient in common cases). 6.2 HYPERLINKS ACROSS PROJECTS: a link lives in one project's version space; the computational-link/query approach (open another project, load a version, then a document) should suffice. 6.3 SCOPE OF THE PRODUCT VERSIONING SPACE: the whole-project version space fits software/web/legal collections, but some hypertext collections are ill-suited to one version space, and linking Molhado-managed material with material under other versioning models is a challenge. They explicitly reject treating this 'incompatibility' as grounds to abandon product versioning, calling instead for 'a generalized access and maintenance model that covers all approaches.'

“links only exist within a single version. So, there is no way to say make a link to document D at version vk”
“The anchors of a computational link are essentially queries against a database of links”
“versioning needs a generalized access and maintenance model that covers all approaches”

§ 7. Conclusions. Restates Molhado as the first system to apply product versioning to hypertexts, claims it well-addresses Osterbye's structural and cognitive issues, and notes the model eases GUI development and reduces cognitive overhead in version creation and element selection. Software components and hypertext structures are 'uniformly versioned in a fine-grained manner,' letting users return to a consistent prior state of a network, a single node, or a single hyperlink. Acknowledges the new issues (cross-version, cross-project linking) and commits to future experimental and usability studies.

“It is the first system that applies the product versioning model to hypertexts”
“Users are allowed to return to a consistent previous state not only of a hypertext network but also of a single node and of a hyperlink”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread; REVISIONS = immutable values; current-revision = state — Molhado's Fluid IR gives this its canonical name and mechanism. A 'node is the basic unit of identity' (the container), a 'slot' holds a value across versions ('slot revisions'), and 'the current version is the version designating the current state.' This is almost exactly our container/revision/current-revision triad, validated 20 years prior. Molhado's 'versioned slot, which may have different values in different versions' is our immutable-revisions-inside-a-stable-identity. The node persists; its slot values are the time-indexed revisions.
  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit committed/minted versions (full persistence); two times — Molhado makes our two-times distinction concrete and warns about a tradeoff. Editing creates a 'temporary version, branching off the current version' that is discarded unless explicitly captured ('The capture command changes a temporary version into a captured one'). This is exactly arrival-time (temporary/working) vs committed (captured, named, dated, authored). It SUPPORTS having both, and shows the commit gate ('a user has to commit changes to entire project') is the minting boundary. Warning: in their model 'individual entities do not have their own version numbers' — version is global to the whole product.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated local world has its own version history, partly independent of members — This is a sharp WARNING. Molhado's product-versioning model deliberately COLLAPSES group-vs-member independent histories: 'a version is global across the whole project... rather than being a particular state of an object.' It buys coherence (a single global timeline, no version-selection queries) at the cost of independence — 'a single change to a versioned entity might potentially create a new version of entire project.' Our goal of grouping histories PARTLY INDEPENDENT of members is precisely what they gave up. The composition/total models they reject preserved member independence but suffered 'version proliferation.' We sit between: we want both, so we must avoid both failure modes.
  • Four-level graph versioning: graph, node, relation-TYPE, node-DATA, composing — Molhado offers a working MECHANISM for fine-grained graph versioning. Hypertext networks are 'attributed directed graphs'; their 'fine-grained versioning scheme for tree and directed graph data structures' records 'fine-grained changes to the structure... and to the contents of associated slots.' This separates structure-level change (edges/nodes added/deleted — Fig 6: node 5 deleted, node 9 created) from data-level change (a slot's content 'changed from old to new'). That is two of our four levels (graph/node structure vs node-DATA) composing in one scheme via the attribute table (rows=nodes, columns=attributes, cells=slots, 3rd dim=version).
  • The trail-to-code join: resolve a path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; declared vs inferred change — Molhado's 'return to a consistent previous state' is exactly point-in-time resolution: 'When users set the current version to a recorded version, the state of entire software project will be set back to that version.' Their key invariant — 'link traversal occurs only among nodes at the current version' — is our resolve-at-timestamp guarantee: a reference resolves to the version coherent with the selected time, never a dangling cross-version pointer. Supports our join. But note their identity is global-version-indexed, not path-indexed; we resolve by path+timestamp, which they'd model as a node + current-version.
  • Code-identity question: is the atomic unit the file or the function (sub-file logical unit)? — Molhado argues strongly for SUB-FILE identity and warns against file-granularity. It explicitly critiques RCS-based systems where 'the smallest versionable information unit is a file.' Instead it takes a 'structure-oriented approach where each document is... logically structured into fine units, called structural units or logical units'; 'For a program, an abstract syntax tree (AST) perfectly represents its logical structure.' Each AST/XML node is a Molhado node = identity unit. This supports our instinct that the function/logical-unit (AST node), not the file, is the atomic identity-bearing thing — and that the AST is a legitimate identity basis.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; substrate choices (Rama event-sourced, multi-version) — Partial support, partial warning. Molhado supports MULTI-VERSION coexistence: 'while working on one version, the user can always switch to work on... any other version... multi-version editing,' and branching by 'just switching the current version.' This is plurality-as-branches. But it is single-writer / single-coherent-state per version — there is no notion of two conflicting views co-present WITHIN a version. Our preserved-disagreement requirement (synthesis late-bound, conflicts not overwritten) goes beyond what product versioning gives; their model forces one consistent state per global version point.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the node/slot/attribute identity machinery (Fluid IR) paired with the product-versioning stance: a node is a stable identity, a versioned slot holds time-indexed immutable revisions, and a global 'current version' resolves every reference coherently — which is almost exactly our object-container kernel and our resolve-at-timestamp join, demonstrated working over fine-grained AST/XML logical units rather than files. Equally important is the warning it carries: Molhado buys coherence and zero-cognitive-overhead selection by making versioning GLOBAL to the whole project, deliberately surrendering the partly-independent group-vs-member histories we want and forcing one consistent state per version (no preserved disagreement). So it validates container/revision/current-version and sub-file AST identity, while marking the exact tradeoff frontier — global coherence vs independent grouping histories vs version proliferation — that our two-times, four-level, plurality-preserving design must navigate between rather than collapse.

3. Refactoring-aware Configuration Management for Object-Oriented Programs (MolhadoRef)

Danny Dig, Kashif Manzoor, Ralph Johnson (UIUC), Tien N. Nguyen (Iowa State) · 2006 · OOPSLA 2006 (ACM) / UIUC tech report UIUCDCS-R-2006-2770; the ResearchGate 'MolhadoRef tool demo' record (221321476) is the companion tool paper

In one line. An operation-based, ID-based SCM that records refactorings and edits as first-class operations and merges them by inverting, textually merging, then replaying — preserving program-element identity and history across renames and moves where file-based SCM (CVS) loses it.

Source access: full. ResearchGate URL (221321476) returned HTTP 403. Read the FULL author PDF instead: http://dig.cs.illinois.edu/papers/MolhadoRef.pdf (the 'Refactoring-aware Configuration Management for Object-Oriented Programs' paper, same authors/content). Extracted via pdftotext locally; all sections, the merge algorithm pseudocode, and Table 1 numbers were read in full.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the core problem: text-based SCM systems handle refactorings badly because refactorings produce global, cross-cutting changes that trigger merge conflicts. A refactoring-aware SCM reduces conflicts, preserves history, and clarifies evolution. MolhadoRef's mechanism: record change operations (refactorings AND edits) used to produce a version, and replay them when merging. Because refactorings have well-defined semantics, they are treated intelligently. The empirical claim is comparative: a case study shows MolhadoRef auto-resolves more conflicts than CVS while producing fewer merge errors.

“MolhadoRef records change operations (refactorings and edits) used to produce one version, and replays them when merging versions.”
“A case-study shows that MolhadoRef solves automatically more merge conflicts than CVS while resulting in fewer merge errors.”

§ 1. Introduction. Motivates why refactoring breaks file-based SCM: refactorings cut across module boundaries, so SCM flags a conflict whenever two programmers touch the same line even for unrelated renames. Cites the authors' prior study of 5 mature Java components — Struts had 136 API changes over 14 months, and >80% of API changes were caused by refactorings — to argue the problem is real and common. Identifies a sharp failure mode unique to OO: a textually 'successful' merge can still be wrong (e.g., one user renames a virtual method while another adds a method in a subclass → accidental overriding, a silent run-time bug). Lists three advantages of MolhadoRef: (1) better merging (resolves more conflicts, no compile errors, semantics preserved), (2) better history preservation (tracks elements even when renamed/moved across files), (3) better evolution understanding (shows high-level refactoring ops instead of thousands of scattered low-level changes). Names the technical crux: edits and refactorings are interleaved, so to merge you must INVERT refactorings to commute them past edits, and refactorings have inter-dependencies. Built on Eclipse (front-end) + Molhado (ID-based SCM backend). Claims the first algorithm to merge refactorings and edits.

“Struts had 136 API changes over a period of 14 months. In each system, more than 80% of the API changes were caused by refactorings.”
“even a successful merge might result in an incorrect program... textual merging could result in accidental method overriding, thus leading to unexpected runtime behavior.”
“it converts refactorings from being the weakest link in an SCM system to being the strongest”
“it presents the first algorithm to effectively merge refactorings and edit operations”

§ 2. Motivating Example. A concrete LAN-simulation program (a known refactoring teaching example) with 5 classes (Packet, LANNode superclass, PrintServer/NetworkTester/Workstation subclasses). Two users branch from V0: Alice (V1) moves getPacketInfo from PrintServer to Packet (τ1), adds methods (τ2, τ3, τ4), renames originate→generatePacket (τ5). Bob (V2) renames getPacketInfo→getPacketInformation (τ6), renames polymorphic send→sendPacket (τ7), renames class WorkStation→Workstation (τ8). The example concretely shows three failure classes of textual merge: (a) an UNNECESSARY conflict — Alice moved a method's declaration while Bob renamed it, same lines touched, but semantically mergeable; (b) a COMPILE error — testLosePacket calls send whose declaration was renamed to sendPacket; (c) a silent RUN-TIME error — Alice's new NetworkTester.sendPacket plus Bob's send→sendPacket rename causes accidental overriding, so packets are silently lost. Also shows LOST HISTORY: once getPacketInfo moves PrintServer→Packet, a file-based repo treats it as a brand-new method and the history is gone.

“a textual merge results in accidental method overriding... the merged program contains bugs that require many hours to find.”
“once Alice moved method getPacketInfo... from class PrintServer to Packet, the history of the getPacketInfo is effectively lost since a file-based SCM repository maintains the method as if it is a newly defined method”

§ 3. Background and Terminology. Defines the operation-based paradigm: an SCM records the operations transforming one version into another and replays them; a version IS the sequence of operations used to create it. Distinguishes three operation classes by semantic richness: API refactorings (richest — rename package/class/method, move class/method, change method signature), API edits (add/delete package/class/method-decl/field-decl), and code edits (everything else, weakest semantics, merged textually). Formalizes operations as program transformations τ: Program→Program, with preconditions; an inappropriate application yields ⊥ (invalid program). Composition τi;τj means apply τi then τj. Gives three formal definitions: (1) two operations COMMUTE if any order yields the same valid program; (2) they CONFLICT if any order yields ⊥ (compile conflicts; MolhadoRef also catches run-time conflicts involving method overriding); (3) τj DEPENDS on τi (τi≺τj) if they don't commute, a strict partial order. Crucial insight: dependences exist only because refactoring engines are NAME-based — e.g., τ5≺τ8 because once the class is renamed (τ8) the FQN the method-rename (τ5) needs no longer exists. The fix would be ID-based engines, but rewriting them is infeasible, so MolhadoRef EMULATES ID-based behavior via two tactics: reorder refactorings, and modify subsequent refactorings' descriptors during replay.

“An operation-based system treats a version as the sequence of operations used to create it.”
“refactorings are the operations with the most well defined semantics, so the ones that can benefit the most”
“If the refactoring engine used the IDs of the program elements, scenarios in which the names of program entities change would never pose a problem... the next best solution is to emulate ID-based engines.”

§ 4. Merging Algorithm. The technical core, a 5-step pipeline over inputs {V0 base, V1, V2, refLog1, refLog2}. Step #1: detect API/code edits via 3-way differencing (3-way, not 2-way, because 2-way can't distinguish additions from deletions). Step #2 (loop): search for compile and run-time conflicts among edits+refactorings using a predicate matrix; build a refactoring dependence DAG; detect circular dependences; user resolves conflicts (e.g., picks losePacket instead of the colliding sendPacket) and breaks cycles by deleting an operation; repeat to a fixed point (guaranteed to converge because each manual resolution removes operations). Step #3: INVERT every refactoring in V1 and V2 by applying an inverse refactoring (not the engine's location-based undo) — this rewrites edits that referenced refactored entities back to the V0 names, yielding V1−Ref and V2−Ref. Step #4: standard 3-way textual merge of the now refactoring-free versions → V_merged−Ref; same-line conflicts caused by refactorings have vanished. Step #5: topologically sort the refactoring DAG and REPLAY all refactorings onto V_merged−Ref, modifying descriptors so a name-based engine emulates an ID-based one. Sub-sections detail: conflict detection via the predicate matrix (e.g., two RenameMethod ops conflict if same source or same destination, with extra cases for polymorphic overriding); inverting refactorings with preconditions and heuristics (saving call-site parameter values like the '7' default-arg case so replay doesn't lose them; tagging name-collision cases for late inversion; falling back to textual treatment when no inversion heuristic exists, degrading gracefully to plain text merge); and replay ordering (topological sort so method refactorings precede their class renames; descriptor rewriting when one version renames and the other moves the same entity).

“Version V_1_minusRef= invertRefactorings(V_1, refs)... 3-wayTextualMerge... orderedRefs= topologicalSort(refsDAG)... replayRefactorings(V_merged_minusRef, orderedRefs)”
“The only way to undo a refactoring is to first undo all the operations that come after it. MolhadoRef must be able to undo a refactoring without undoing later operations.”
“When no heuristic for inverting a refactoring is found, the algorithm treats the refactoring as a textual edit... in the worst case it is as good as classic textual merging.”
“MolhadoRef uses a topological sort algorithm to reorder the nodes in the refactorings DAG.”

§ 5. Implementation. MolhadoRef is an Eclipse plugin: Eclipse Java editor as front-end, Molhado (an OO SCM infrastructure / 'a database that keeps track of history') as backend. At check-in it parses Java (all 1.4 syntax) down to method/field declarations, creates a Molhado counterpart for each program element with a UNIQUE IDENTIFIER, and stores method/field bodies as attributes of declarations. Refactorings update Molhado properties (names, args) but element identity stays intact across refactoring. Discusses the operation-based vs state-based tradeoff: pure operation-based is accurate but slow (many operations to record/replay); state-based deltas at commit are efficient but lose change semantics. MolhadoRef uses a HYBRID — Eclipse compare engine for body/element deltas plus captured Eclipse refactoring operations for semantics. Built on Eclipse's processor-participant refactoring architecture; their participants rewrite the FQNs in subsequent refactoring descriptors (the descriptor-rewriting emulation of ID-based engines). Notably argues these qualities could be RETROFITTED onto CVS: store identifier-to-name maps in metadata files in the repo, load at checkout, update during refactoring, write back at check-in. Current scope is a subset of Fowler's catalog; the key extensibility requirement is the ability to INVERT a refactoring.

“For each entity, Molhado gives a unique identifier... the identity of program entities remains intact even after refactoring operations.”
“MolhadoRef uses a mixture of both paradigms to maximize efficiency and accuracy.”
“Identifier-to-name maps can be saved in metadata files and stored in the repository... The key property we require of refactorings is the ability to invert refactorings.”

§ 6. Case Study. Evaluated on MolhadoRef's own three weeks of parallel development by two programmers (who stopped pair-programming and merged real edits+refactorings). The authors note such datasets are rare because developers avoid the problem (notes asking teammates to check in before refactoring are common), so they dogfooded. Many CVS same-line conflicts traced to two refactorings: renaming a central class LightRefactoring→Operation and moving it to another package. Table 1 quantifies: on the MolhadoRef codebase, CVS produced 36 conflicts, 41 compile errors, 7 run-time errors; MolhadoRef produced 1 conflict, 0 compile errors, 0 run-time errors. MolhadoRef auto-merged all 36 CVS same-line conflicts and asked for help only once (both devs added getID() to the same class). Time: 105 minutes for the two developers to reach a correct merge with CVS vs under 1 minute with MolhadoRef. Two further wins: granularity reduction — over 12 weeks, 67 refactorings corresponded to 1267 changed lines, a 1:19 compression for program understanding; and history — over the 3 weeks CVS LOST the history of two core files containing 73 API methods, which MolhadoRef preserved.

“MolhadoRef was able to automatically merge all 36 same-line conflicts reported by CVS... MolhadoRef did not introduce any compile-time or run-time errors while CVS had 48 such errors”
“it took 105 minutes for the two developers to produce the final, correct version using CVS, while it takes less than one minute for MolhadoRef.”
“there were 67 refactorings which correspond to 1267 changed lines... a reduction of 1 : 19.”
“CVS lost the history of two core files containing 73 API methods.”

§ 7. Related Work. Positions MolhadoRef across SCM lineages. Traditional file/directory systems (CVS); Subversion adds metadata/rename/copy versioning but still file-focused. Fine-grained SCM systems (COOP/Orm, Coven, POEM, Westfechtel, Ohst) version sub-file artifacts but none handle refactorings. Software merging taxonomy (per Mens's survey): text-based (RCS/CVS, lines as indivisible units, can't combine two edits to one line); syntactic (parse-tree/AST/graph-based, ignores comment/whitespace conflicts but misses semantic invalidity); semantic (dependency graphs, program slicing, denotational semantics, e.g., Yang-Horwitz-Reps). Operation-based merging lineage (Lippe & van Oosterom gave a theoretical framework but no refactoring application; Edwards; GINA's redo mechanism, which can't handle long histories/fine granularity). Closest prior work: Ekman & Asklund's refactoring-aware versioning in Eclipse — but they keep element IDs only in volatile memory (short-lived history) and present no merge algorithm, whereas MolhadoRef persists IDs across the whole project lifecycle and contributes the merge algorithm. Claims novelty as the combination of operation-based + fine-grained + ID-based SCM for refactorings, and the first algorithm to merge refactorings and edits.

“none of them handle refactorings as MolhadoRef does.”
“Ekman and Asklund... keeps the program elements and their IDs in volatile memory, thus allowing for a short-lived history... In our approach, program elements and their IDs are stored throughout the lifecycle of the project”
“the novelty of this work is the combination of operation-based, fine-grained, ID-based SCM to handle refactorings and high-level edit operations.”

§ 8. Conclusions and Future Work. Restates that file-level SCM fails under refactoring three ways (more conflicts, lost history, harder evolution understanding) and that MolhadoRef, being aware of program entities and the refactoring operations that change them, records/detects and replays changes to merge edits and refactorings effectively without compile/run-time errors. Persistent IDs track history; explicit refactoring representation reduces the cognitive load of understanding evolution. Future work: empirical productivity study of teams using it; the larger research goal is upgrading component-based applications to new component versions by replaying the component's refactorings on both component and application sides — framed as a special case of the same merge algorithm. Closes with the social claim that refactoring-tolerant SCM will make developers bolder about refactoring.

“Storing the IDs of program entities across versions tracks the history better, while explicit representation of refactorings reduces the load of understanding the program evolution.”
“This research is part of our larger goal to upgrade component-based applications... by replaying the component's refactorings”
“software developers will have the freedom to make their designs easier to understand and reuse.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread; REVISIONS = immutable values; 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — Directly NAMES and validates the core mechanism. Molhado gives every program element (class, method, field) a unique persistent ID at check-in; refactorings update properties (name, args) but 'the identity of program entities remains intact.' This is exactly our container/revision split: ID = container, source-at-a-version = revision. It also SUPPORTS our hard rule via a sharp DECLARED-vs-INFERRED contrast: MolhadoRef records refactorings as declared operations from Eclipse's engine; only as a fallback does it INFER edits via 3-way diff. The paper's whole thesis is that declared semantics beat reconstructed diffs.
  • The code-identity question: is the unit the file or the function? Is identity file/path/name/FQN/AST/hash? — Gives a strong, evidenced ANSWER and a failure mode. Identity is NOT file, path, name, or FQN. File-based identity loses history when a method moves class; name/FQN-based identity breaks under rename (their τ5≺τ8 dependence: renaming a class invalidates a pending method-rename because the FQN no longer exists). Their answer: identity is an opaque persistent ID, atomic unit is the program element (method/field/class), not the file. 'If the refactoring engine used the IDs of the program elements, scenarios in which the names change would never pose a problem.'
  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions; declared change vs inferred change — Offers a concrete MECHANISM for the two-times distinction and a build-vs-buy lesson. MolhadoRef mixes paradigms: operation-based (record each declared change when it happens = our committed/minted, semantic time) vs state-based deltas computed at commit (= arrival-time diffing). It explicitly weighs them: operation-based is 'accurate' but slow; state-based is 'efficient' but 'cannot recover the semantics.' Their hybrid (capture refactorings as operations, diff bodies) is a template for our 'declared change vs inferred change' join.
  • The trail-to-code join (first consumer): resolve trail path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; DECLARED vs INFERRED change — This is the paper's strongest gift. An agent reasoning trail that DECLARES 'I renamed method X to Y, moved Z to class W' is exactly MolhadoRef's refactoring log — first-class operations with well-defined semantics. The paper proves that resolving such declared ops gives correct history tracking across moves/renames where path+diff alone fails (CVS lost history of 2 core files / 73 API methods). Our trail should carry declared edit-operations, and we resolve them as operations, falling back to diff-inference (their RefactoringCrawler path) only when undeclared.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated grouping/local-world with its own history, members pinned/floating — PARTIAL support + a gap to fill. Molhado is described as 'an object-oriented SCM infrastructure' that versions structure, not just files (parses to method/field declaration, bodies as attributes). It versions a tree of elements with IDs — closer to our grouping-with-members than a flat file repo. But the paper does NOT give groupings independent version history or pinned/floating member binding; it tracks individual element identity. So it warrants the element-identity foundation but leaves the grouping-version layer to us.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicting views representable, synthesis late-bound — WARNS against eager auto-resolution and offers a structured arbitration model. MolhadoRef does NOT silently overwrite: it builds a dependency DAG of operations, detects conflicts (compile + run-time, incl. accidental method overriding) via a predicate matrix, and escalates to user-assisted resolution — late-bound human synthesis exactly where automation can't safely decide. Their commute/conflict/dependence formalism (Defs 1-3) is a usable vocabulary for our preserved-disagreement arbitration: two ops commute (auto-merge), conflict (surface), or depend (order them).
  • Four-level graph versioning: graph / node / relation-type / node-data must compose — PARTIAL precedent. MolhadoRef versions node identity (element IDs), node-data (method bodies as attributes), and a kind of relation-type change (override relationships, which it specifically guards — accidental override is a run-time conflict). Replay + topological sort is a working example of composing changes across these levels in one merge. It does not formalize a 4-level model, but demonstrates that node-identity, node-data, and relation (override/containment) changes must be merged together with ordering, validating that the levels must COMPOSE.
  • Substrate choices: Rama (event-sourced) as append-only operation log — SUPPORTS the event-sourced substrate bet at the granularity of code. MolhadoRef IS an operation-based (event-sourced) SCM: 'an SCM tool records the operations that were performed... and replays them.' A version = the sequence of operations used to create it. This is precisely Rama's append-only log + replay/projection model applied to code edits. The paper's cost note (pure operation-based 'can be slow') is a real warning for a Rama-backed code kernel: don't replay everything; checkpoint state (their hybrid).

Takeaway. The single most important contribution: code-unit identity should be a persistent, opaque ID attached to the program element (method/field/class), NOT its file, path, name, or FQN — and the durable way to track that identity across renames/moves is to record changes as DECLARED, first-class operations (refactorings) with well-defined semantics, replaying them rather than reconstructing intent from text diffs. This is direct, dogfooded validation (1 conflict and 0 merge errors vs CVS's 36 conflicts + 48 errors; 73 API methods' history preserved where CVS lost it) of our container/revision kernel and our DECLARED-vs-INFERRED rule. The transferable mechanism for our trail-to-code join is their invert→textual-merge→replay pipeline plus the commute/conflict/dependence formalism (Defs 1-3) for deciding what auto-merges, what must be ordered, and what is escalated to late-bound human synthesis — with the explicit caveat that pure operation replay is slow, so a Rama-backed kernel must checkpoint state (their operation/state hybrid) rather than replay everything.

4. Versioning Your Code with Monticello (Chapter 7, Deep into Pharo / Pharo by Example)

Co-written with Oscar Nierstrasz (Software Composition Group, University of Bern); part of the Pharo book series by Nierstrasz, Ducasse, et al. · 2013 · Book chapter, Deep into Pharo / Pharo by Example (Square Bracket Associates); hosted at scg.unibe.ch

In one line. A detailed how-to and conceptual exposition of Monticello, Pharo's package-granular versioning system, where classes/methods (not text files) are the units of change, packages are intensional containers defined by category/protocol rules, versions are immutable UUID-stamped snapshots with DAG ancestry, and umbrella packages with pinned dependencies act as configurations.

Source access: full. Read the FULL chapter. The WebFetch of the PDF returned only raw binary, so I downloaded the PDF locally and ran pdftotext -layout on it, yielding the complete 1012-line chapter (scg.unibe.ch/archive/papers/Nier13bMonticello.pdf). This is a book CHAPTER, not a conference paper — no Abstract/Related Work; I walk its real section headings (7.1 Basic usage through 7.7 Chapter summary).

The paper, section by section

§ Preamble / Chapter intro (why a code-granular versioning system). The chapter opens by motivating versioning in general (store multiple versions, manage concurrent access, track changes, enable collaboration 'as soon as the size of your software increases beyond a few classes'). Its core argument is that general-purpose text VCS (CVS, Subversion, Git) are the WRONG granularity for Smalltalk: 'CVS-like tools only version plain text files and not individual packages, classes or methods. We would therefore lack the ability to track changes at the appropriate level of granularity.' Because Monticello knows it stores classes and methods rather than lines of text, 'they can do a better job of supporting the development process.' It positions Monticello as the CVS-equivalent and SmalltalkHub/Squeaksource as the SourceForge-equivalent online repositories — establishing the program-unit-as-unit-of-change thesis the whole research angle rests on.

“CVS-like tools only version plain text files and not individual packages, classes or methods.”
“If the versioning tools know that you store classes and methods instead of plain text, they can do a better job of supporting the development process.”
“SmalltalkHub is the equivalent of SourceForge, and Monticello the equivalent of CVS.”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Creating a package (the container as an intensional object). Defines the central container abstraction. A package is not a folder or file list but a live object: 'a package is an object — an instance of PackageInfo — that knows how to identify the classes and methods that belong to it.' Membership is INTENSIONAL, defined by naming rules rather than enumeration: a package Perfect contains (1) all classes in category Perfect or categories starting with Perfect-, (2) all methods in protocols named perfect / Perfect or starting with perfect- (these cross-cutting members are 'class extensions'), and (3) all methods of those classes EXCEPT ones in -prefixed protocols (which belong to other packages). Monticello auto-maps Smalltalk categories to packages. This is the key identity mechanism: the container's contents are computed from a declared rule, so the same identity thread can gain/lose members without the container itself changing identity.

“a package is an object — an instance of PackageInfo — that knows how to identify the classes and methods that belong to it.”
“All methods belonging to any class (in any category) that are defined in a protocol named perfect or Perfect... Such methods are known as extensions.”
“Monticello will map categories to packages for us.”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Committing changes & versions (immutable snapshots, non-global numbering). Introduces the immutable-revision concept and the repository. A repository is 'a package container' (local or remote); package-cache is a default local directory mirror of everything saved/loaded. The crucial definition: 'A version is an immutable snapshot of a package that has been written to a repository.' Each version gets a number that is LOCAL to a repository, NOT globally unique: 'this number is not globally unique — in another repository you might have the same file identifier for a different snapshot.' Saving auto-assigns the next number but it is editable, and critically 'version branches do not interfere with the numbering scheme (as with CVS or Subversion)' — numbering is a flat per-repo counter, identity/ancestry is tracked separately. Files are named like Perfect-XX.1.mcz where XX is author initials/name.

“A version is an immutable snapshot of a package that has been written to a repository.”
“this number is not globally unique — in another repository you might have the same file identifier for a different snapshot.”
“version branches do not interfere with the numbering scheme (as with CVS or Subversion).”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Class extensions & clean/dirty state. Develops the cross-cutting-membership idea concretely: methods isPerfect and divisors are added to the system class Integer but placed in a *perfect protocol, so 'they do belong to the Perfect package since they are in a protocol whose name starts with * and matches the package name. Such methods are known as class extensions.' These extension methods 'will be available only to someone who loads the Perfect package' — membership, not file location, governs what travels with a version. The clean/dirty mechanism defines the relationship between the immutable saved revision and the mutable working state: 'Modifying the code in a package... makes that package dirty. This means that the version of the package in the image is different from the version that has been saved or loaded.' A dirty package shows an asterisk; saving cleans it. This is precisely a container with an immutable current-revision plus a divergence flag.

“they do belong to the Perfect package since they are in a protocol whose name starts with * and matches the package name.”
“This means that the version of the package in the image is different from the version that has been saved or loaded.”
“Saving a dirty package cleans it.”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Repository inspector, load/unload/update. Describes how installed-vs-repository state is surfaced through typeface conventions in the inspector, which encode the ancestry relationship between the image's current revision and stored revisions: underlined = installed; bold = 'not an ancestor of the installed version' (newer OR a different branch); normal = older. At the package level: underlined name = installed; bold underlined = installed but a newer version exists; normal = not installed. The exercise loads version 1 (reverting), then version 2 (updating), demonstrating that you can move the current-revision pointer freely across immutable snapshots. This operationalizes 'current-revision = state': loading is just repointing the image at a chosen immutable snapshot, and the UI computes the ancestry relation to tell you whether that snapshot is ahead, behind, or on a sibling branch.

“a bold version name means that this version is not an ancestor of the installed version. This may mean that it is a newer version, or that it belongs to a different branch.”
“an underlined version name means that this version is installed in the image.”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Branching. Defines branching as divergent lineage over a shared ancestor: 'A branch is a line of development versions that exists independently of another line, yet still shares a common ancestor version if you look far enough back in time.' You branch by basing a new save on an EARLIER version than the latest. The worked example loads v1, makes a different isPerfect implementation, and saving produces v3 as a sibling of v2 (both children of v1). The system detects divergence and warns on conflicting loads ('warning that you have unsaved changes'). This establishes the DAG (not linear) version model and grounds preserved-plurality: two independent valid implementations of the same methods coexist as separate branches, neither overwriting the other.

“A branch is a line of development versions that exists independently of another line, yet still shares a common ancestor version if you look far enough back in time.”
“versions 2 and 3 constitute independent branches.”

§ 7.1 Basic usage — Merging (fine-grained, per-element conflict resolution). Describes merge as re-integration of branches with method/class-level granularity — the heart of the program-unit-as-unit-of-change claim. 'The merge tool... allows for fine-grained package version merging'; differences are shown per element (a new method, two changed methods), with source-level diff coloring (new=red, removed=struck blue, unchanged=black). Conflict is defined precisely: 'A method or a class is in conflict if its definition has been altered.' Resolution is per-element and human-decided via typeface/arrow conventions: right-arrow = repository replaces current, left-arrow = repository version rejected, red = conflicting, requiring 'Keep current version or Use incoming version.' The merged version records BOTH source versions as ancestors. This shows late-bound, granular synthesis where disagreement is preserved up to the merge decision and resolved per unit, not per file.

“The merge tool is a tool that allows for fine-grained package version merging.”
“A method or a class is in conflict if its definition has been altered.”
“The merged version will have as its ancestor the two versions it was merged from.”

§ 7.2 Exploring Monticello repositories — Browse, Changes & History. Covers three inspection tools (folding in the 7.3 History item, which is conceptually identical). The Browse button opens a 'snapshot browser' whose advantage over the normal browser 'is its ability to display class extensions' — it shows the package's full intensional contents including cross-cutting extension methods on foreign classes, which the ordinary class browser hides; best practice is to 'always browse the code of your package before publishing it, to ensure that it really contains what you think it does' (an admission that intensional membership can surprise you). The Changes button 'computes the difference between the code in the image and the most recent version of the package in the repository,' opening a patch browser of changed/new methods — the inferred-diff tool (vs the declared snapshot). The History item opens a version-history viewer listing every version of a package with its commit comments, and lets you per-version explore changes relative to the working copy or 'spawn a new history browser relative to the selected version' — each container carries its own ordered, annotated revision log.

“The advantage of the snapshot browser over the browser is its ability to display class extensions.”
“The Changes button computes the difference between the code in the image and the most recent version of the package in the repository.”
“It opens a version history viewer that displays the comments committed along with each version of the selected package.”
“always browse the code of your package before publishing it, to ensure that it really contains what you think it does.”

§ 7.3 Advanced topics — Dependencies & umbrella/configuration packages. The most important section for our groupings-as-versioned-objects question. Monticello declares required packages so they load in dependency order, recursively, leaves-first. Critically, version pinning auto-tracks: 'Whenever new versions of required packages are checked in, then new versions of the packages that depend on them will automatically depend on the new versions.' Hard limit: 'Dependencies cannot be expressed across repositories.' The umbrella pattern (Pier-All) is named explicitly: 'an empty package that acts as a kind of umbrella' that requires all real packages, so installing it installs everything and 'the only package that needs to be saved is Pier-All; all dependent dirty packages are saved automatically.' A worked refactor splits Perfect into NewPerfect-Tests + NewPerfect-Extensions under umbrella NewPerfect-All. The failure mode is stressed: committing a member directly (NewPerfect-Tests) instead of through the umbrella 'effectively break[s] the dependency... Don't do it!' — plus a naming caveat against suffix-overlapping package names. The chapter recommends graduating to a Metacello configuration (the +Config button, Chapter 9) for 'more flexible dependencies.'

“Package Pier-All is an empty package that acts as a kind of umbrella. It requires Pier-Blog, Pier-Caching and all the other Pier packages.”
“Whenever new versions of required packages are checked in, then new versions of the packages that depend on them will automatically depend on the new versions.”
“Dependencies cannot be expressed across repositories. All requiring and required packages must live in the same repository.”
“If instead you save NewPerfect-Tests, this will not cause NewPerfect-All to be saved. This is bad because you effectively break the dependency.”

§ 7.3 Advanced topics — Class initialization. Explains a load-time hook: 'any class that defines an initialize method on the class side will be sent the initialize message' when the package loads — only to classes that define it themselves (not inherited), and 'not invoked when you merely reload a package.' Its flagship use is working around a limitation of class extensions: extensions can only ADD METHODS, not instance variables, so a class-side initialize is used to add an instance variable at load time (e.g. adding lastRedRun to TestCase). A side-effect noted: extending/altering a class you don't own dirties ITS package ('After installing TestCaseExtension, the package SUnit will become dirty'). This reveals a subtlety relevant to our membership model — an extension lives in one package but mutating a foreign class can mark the foreign owner's package dirty, i.e. cross-container side effects on shared nodes.

“any class that defines an initialize method on the class side will be sent the initialize message.”
“Class extensions are strictly limited to adding new methods to a class.”
“if you change a class that is not in your package, the other package will become dirty.”

§ 7.4 Getting a change set from two versions (snapshot vs patch). Draws the explicit distinction between a full snapshot and a derived patch — directly the declared-vs-inferred axis. 'A Monticello version is the snapshot of one or more packages. A version contains the complete set of class and method definitions.' Against this, 'A patch is the set of all necessary side effect in the system to go from one version A to another version B.' Pharo change sets are the patch mechanism (global side effects on the system). The technique to capture a diff is procedural and inference-based: create an empty change set, then load v2 while v1 is loaded, and the change set records the delta, which can be filed out to a DiffPerfect.X.cs file. This confirms Monticello's stance: store complete declared snapshots as truth; derive patches by replaying loads and recording side effects.

“A version contains the complete set of class and method definitions that constitute the underlying packages.”
