“A patch is the set of all necessary side effect in the system to go from one version A to another version B.”

§ 7.5 Kinds of repositories. Enumerates repository backends, each a different physical store for the same immutable-snapshot abstraction, with access modes (read-only/write-only/read-write) and optional per-user rights. HTTP (most popular, used by SmalltalkHub, web-browsable/WebDAV-able, runnable on plain Apache); FTP (similar over FTP); GOODS (stores versions in a distributed OO database with transactions/journaling/replication, 'suitable for large repositories used by many clients'); Directory (local filesystem dir, the package-cache kind, only option for disconnected development, versions later copyable to shared repos via SmalltalkHub's Import Versions); Directory-with-Subdirectories (hierarchical variant); SMTP (write-only, emails versions to an address/mailing-list). It also shows programmatically adding repositories via MCDirectoryRepository / MCRepositoryGroup. Takeaway for us: version/snapshot identity is independent of the storage substrate — the same .mcz can live across many backend types.

“Repositories can be read-only, write-only or read-write. Access rights may be defined globally or can be tied to a particular user.”
“A directory repository stores versions in a directory in the local file system... it's the only option for disconnected development.”

§ 7.6 The .mcz file format (what an immutable version physically is). Reveals the on-disk structure of a version — concretely grounding the identity model. An .mcz is just a ZIP ('Monticello zip') containing four conceptual things: (1) package — name info; (2) VersionInfo — metadata including 'the author initials, date and time the snapshot was taken, and the ancestry of the snapshot'; (3) Snapshot — 'a record of the state of the package at a particular time' under a snapshot/ dir (currently a single source.st fileout); (4) Dependencies — a dependencies/ dir with one member per required package. The pivotal statement on identity/ancestry: 'A version doesn't contain a full history of the source code. It's a snapshot of the code at a single point in time, with a UUID identifying that snapshot, and a record of the UUIDs of all the previous snapshots it's descended from.' Metadata is S-expression key/value (nestable dicts); an example version file shows name/message/date/time/author/ancestors. This is exactly a content-snapshot + UUID identity + UUID ancestry-DAG — our immutable-revision-inside-a-container made physical.

“A version doesn't contain a full history of the source code. It's a snapshot of the code at a single point in time, with a UUID identifying that snapshot, and a record of the UUIDs of all the previous snapshots it's descended from.”
“VersionInfo... contains the author initials, date and time the snapshot was taken, and the ancestry of the snapshot.”
“an mcz file is simply a zipped file containing the source code and other meta-data.”

§ 7.7 Chapter summary. Recaps the model: packages map to Smalltalk categories + method protocols (class extensions via *foo protocols anywhere in the system); editing marks a package dirty/saveable; many repository kinds exist (HTTP via SmalltalkHub most common); saved packages cache to package-cache; the repository inspector browses and selectively loads/unloads versions; branches are created by basing a new version on an earlier-than-latest one and the inspector tracks ancestry to identify separate branches; branches merge with fine-grained per-conflict control and the merged version records both parents; dependencies are tracked so saving a depending package mints a new version pinned to latest required versions; class-side initialize runs at load (notably to add instance variables for extensions); and versions are stored as .mcz zips holding a complete source snapshot plus metadata/dependencies. The summary reaffirms the program-unit (class/method/package) — not the text file — as the unit of change throughout.

“You can create a new branch of a package by basing a new version on another version which is earlier than the latest version.”
“The merged version will have as its ancestor the two versions it was merged from.”
“The mcz file contains a snapshot of the complete source code of that version of your package, as well as files containing other important metadata.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (stable container = identity thread; revisions = immutable values; current-revision = state) — GIVES IT A NAME + supporting precedent. Monticello's PackageInfo IS exactly our stable container: 'a package is an object — an instance of PackageInfo — that knows how to identify the classes and methods that belong to it.' A version is 'an immutable snapshot of a package' — our immutable revision. The image's loaded copy (clean/dirty) is the current-revision/state. The container is intensional (a membership rule over categories/protocols), not a frozen list — directly models a container whose identity persists while its contents change. CAVEAT for our hard rule 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest': Monticello's membership is a declared rule (category prefix + *protocol pattern), so what belongs is computed deterministically from declared metadata, not inferred — aligns with our rule only if the rule itself is treated as declared truth.
  • The code-identity question (is the unit a file or a function? identity = path/name/AST/hash?) — OFFERS A DIRECT ANSWER and a warning. Monticello's whole thesis is that the file is the WRONG unit: 'CVS-like tools only version plain text files and not individual packages, classes or methods. We would therefore lack the ability to track changes at the appropriate level of granularity.' Identity here is the NAME/FQN: package = name + membership rule; method identity = Class>>selector in a named protocol; class identity = its name. NOT file, NOT path, NOT AST, NOT content-hash. Supports our instinct that the function/method (not the file) is the atomic unit, but warns that name-based identity breaks under rename (Monticello sees a renamed method as delete+add — pure INFERRED-by-diff, no declared identity continuity).
  • Versioning as its own layer; two times (arrival-time vs committed/minted-time) — SUPPORTS the two-times split and shows a pure version-2 of it. Monticello has NO arrival-time substrate — every version is an explicit, human-MINTED commit ('the next available number is automatically assigned'). VersionInfo records 'date and time the snapshot was taken, and the ancestry' — committed-time + DAG ancestry. This is full-persistence (branch from any node) with zero partial-persistence. Validates our committed/minted layer design but shows the gap our Rama substrate fills: Monticello cannot answer 'what was the code when this trail-event arrived' because it has no arrival log — only discrete human commits. Our two-times model is strictly more expressive.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with their own history, member binding pinned/floating — OFFERS A CONCRETE MECHANISM (umbrella package) AND a failure mode. Pier-All / NewPerfect-All is 'an empty package that acts as a kind of umbrella' — a grouping with its OWN version history, partly independent of members. Member binding is explicitly version-PINNED: 'a new version of NewPerfect-All which then requires the new version of NewPerfect-Tests... will also depend on the existing, unmodified version of NewPerfect-Extensions.' That is per-member effectivity (some pinned-updated, some pinned-frozen) — exactly our pinned-vs-floating distinction. FAILURE MODE is loud: 'If instead you save NewPerfect-Tests, this will not cause NewPerfect-All to be saved... you effectively break the dependency. Don't do it!' — a grouping whose member pins silently desync when you commit a member directly. Warns our grouping layer needs commit-through-the-container discipline.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data must compose) — PARTIAL precedent + shows the missing levels. Monticello composes exactly TWO of our four levels: node-DATA (method/class source defs inside a snapshot) and GROUPING/graph (package version aggregating member versions, umbrella aggregating packages). It has NO relation-TYPE versioning — the only 'relation' is the untyped 'requires' dependency, and dependencies 'cannot be expressed across repositories,' a hard composition boundary. So Monticello demonstrates that data-level + container-level versioning DO compose (umbrella depends on pinned member versions), but its lack of typed-relation versioning is precisely the gap our four-level model must fill — a worked example of the two easy levels and a blank where the relation-type level should be.
  • Trail-to-code join: declared change vs inferred change — GIVES US THE EXACT VOCABULARY. Monticello cleanly separates the two: the SNAPSHOT (declared full state, 'the complete set of class and method definitions') vs the PATCH/change-set (inferred diff, 'A patch is the set of all necessary side effect in the system to go from one version A to another'). Section 7.4 shows the diff is RECONSTRUCTED by loading v1, recording, loading v2 — pure inference. The merge tool's red/blue/struck typefaces ARE diff-inference made visible. For our trail-join: store the DECLARED snapshot as truth and compute the INFERRED patch on demand — never the reverse. Directly supports our rule that the agent's declared edit-op is primary and diff-reconstruction is secondary.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; late-bound synthesis — OFFERS A MECHANISM (branch + fine-grained merge) for late-bound synthesis. Branches are first-class independent lines ('a line of development versions that exists independently of another line, yet still shares a common ancestor'). Conflict is preserved, not auto-resolved: 'A method or a class is in conflict if its definition has been altered'; resolution is per-element, deferred to a human ('Keep current version or Use incoming version'), and the merged version records BOTH parents as ancestors. Exactly our preserved-disagreement-then-late-synthesis pattern at method granularity. The limit: Monticello forces resolution AT merge time (you must pick); it cannot hold an unresolved conflict as a durable representable object the way our plurality layer wants.
  • Substrate choices (Rama event-sourced log vs discrete commits) — CONTRASTS / clarifies our substrate bet. Monticello is a pure commit-graph substrate: discrete human-minted snapshots, DAG ancestry by UUID, no event log between commits, no time-travel except to committed nodes. 'A version doesn't contain a full history of the source code. It's a snapshot of the code at a single point in time, with a UUID... and a record of the UUIDs of all the previous snapshots it's descended from.' This is the full-persistence half of our design WITHOUT the arrival-time half. Confirms that a UUID-keyed immutable-snapshot + ancestry model is sound and battle-tested, and that our addition (Rama arrival log underneath the commit graph) is the genuinely new contribution, not a reinvention.

Takeaway. Monticello is a working, decades-proven existence proof of our object-container kernel at code granularity: a package is a stable INTENSIONAL container (a PackageInfo membership rule over names/protocols, not a file list) whose identity persists while its immutable UUID-stamped snapshot revisions accumulate a DAG ancestry — and it validates two of our hardest design moves, (a) name/protocol-based unit identity that lets cross-cutting 'class extensions' travel with the right container regardless of file location, and (b) the umbrella/configuration package as a separately-versioned grouping whose members are pinned per-version. But it equally maps our frontier: it has ONLY committed/minted time (no arrival-time log — the gap our Rama substrate fills), only untyped same-repo 'requires' relations (no relation-TYPE versioning level), and name-based identity that silently degrades to delete+add under rename — and it broadcasts the exact failure mode to avoid (commit a member directly instead of through its umbrella and you silently desync the pins: 'Don't do it!')."

What we can gather — Angle 1

The four systems triangulate a single answer with three variants. The atomic unit of code identity is the program element — the function, method, or definition — not the file, and the identity must be a stable handle decoupled from the mutable name and path. Every one of them rejects file-granularity explicitly (Monticello: "we would lack the ability to track changes at the appropriate level"; Molhado critiques RCS's "smallest unit is a file"; Dolt later re-bases git from files to rows for the same reason). They differ only in how the stable handle is minted, and that difference is the whole design axis:

  • Unison derives it from content — the hash of the normalized AST. Rename becomes a metadata edit by definition; the diamond-dependency problem evaporates; plural definitions coexist under one name. Maximal purity. The cost: it has no arrival-time axis and addresses code by hash, not path+timestamp — which is the exact key our trail-to-code join needs.
  • Molhado/MolhadoRef mint an opaque persistent ID and demote the name to a versioned attribute. This is the closest system to our kernel, and it carries the strongest evidence in the whole angle: persistent IDs preserved the history of 73 renamed/moved API methods that CVS lost (1 merge conflict vs CVS's 36 + 48 errors). And it proves the principle our kernel already holds — declared operations beat inferred diffs: refactorings are recorded as first-class operations by the engine that performed them, never reconstructed from text.
  • Monticello uses an intensional container (a membership rule over names/protocols) with name-based identity, battle-tested for two decades — and it shows the failure mode of name-based identity in the open: a rename silently degrades to delete+add, because nothing carries the identity across the name change.

So the convergence is sharp. The container/revision split is validated four independent times. The function is the unit; the file is an assembly that pins function versions — which is already the bridge to Angle 5, before we even get there. And the decisive practical guidance: adopt opaque-ID identity (Molhado), not content-hash identity (Unison) — because we need the path+timestamp trail resolution and the arrival-time history that content-addressing actively destroys — while keeping a content hash as a derived attribute for dedup and for the exactness flag. The one piece Unison has that we would otherwise lack is the namespace-version: the name/path→identity table, snapshotted as-of a moment — which is exactly what we must keep so a trail's recorded path still resolves later.

Angle 2 — Version-Model Theory: What Is Versioning, Formally?

5. Version Models for Software Configuration Management

Reidar Conradi (NTNU Trondheim) and Bernhard Westfechtel (Aachen University of Technology) · 1998 · ACM Computing Surveys, Vol. 30, No. 2

In one line. The canonical taxonomy of software versioning: it separates product space from version space, extensional from intensional, state-based from change-based, and binds them together through AND/OR graphs and configuration rules.

Source access: full. ACM (dl.acm.org) returned HTTP 403. Read the FULL original PDF (51 pages, Acrobat Distiller 1998) from an open university mirror: https://www.kiv.zcu.cz/~brada/files/aswi/.../conradi98version-models.pdf — extracted locally with pdftotext -layout and read line-by-line. All quotes below are verbatim from that authoritative PDF (pages 232-282), not from the lossy WebFetch summary.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract + 1. Introduction. After 20+ years of SCM, building consistent configurations of versioned products remains hard; the survey classifies the VERSION MODELS underlying commercial systems and research prototypes, defines the four fundamental concepts (revisions, variants, configurations, changes), and foregrounds INTENSIONAL versioning (construction from configuration rules) as the hard center. The Introduction installs the paper's organizing dichotomy: a product lives in a PRODUCT SPACE (objects + relationships, ignoring versions) and a VERSION SPACE (how versions are organized); a VERSIONED OBJECT BASE combines them. A version model is defined by how the version space is structured, which objects are versioned externally vs internally, how different objects' version spaces relate, and how old/new versions are reconstructed/constructed. Notes the field's curse — every system reinvents terminology over near-identical concepts — so the contribution is a UNIFIED terminology plus a canonical graph formalism (nodes/edges/attributes). Surveys representational families: file-based, language-based (module interconnection languages), and database-founded (EER, OO, deductive).

“we focus on intensional versioning, that is, construction of versions based on configuration rules”
“Software objects and their relationships constitute the product space, their versions are organized in the version space. A versioned object base combines product and version space.”
“each system comes with its own terminology. On the other hand, the underlying concepts are often very similar.”
“A version model defines the objects to be versioned, version identification and organization, as well as operations for retrieving existing versions and constructing new versions.”

§ 2. Product Space (Objects, Relationships, Representations). Describes product structure WITHOUT versioning. The product space is a product graph; nodes = software objects, edges = relationships. Every object carries an OBJECT IDENTIFIER (OID): an external OID is a user-assigned name; a system-generated unique OID may be used internally — the identity primitive. Objects are coarse-grained but internally structured (a module = declarations + statements; fine-grained content held as 'long attributes'). Two relationship kinds: COMPOSITION (subsystem→module→leaf; composite objects = configurations, leaves = atomic objects) and DEPENDENCY (orthogonal: import/include, lifecycle, build), each with a dependent that must be kept consistent with its master. Objects split into SOURCE (human-authored) vs DERIVED (tool-produced). Representation choice (text files vs syntax trees/graphs) changes what 'diff' even means — differing text lines vs differing syntactic units. Distinguishes domain-independent vs domain-specific product models.

“each software object carries an object identifier (OID) that serves to identify it uniquely within a certain context. An external OID is a name assigned by the user, whereas a system-generated, unique OID may be used internally.”
“Objects that are decomposed are called composite objects or configurations. Objects residing at the leaves of the composition hierarchy are denoted atomic objects.”
“a diff command... returns differing text lines in the case of text files and differing syntactic units in the case of syntax trees”

§ 3.1 Versions, Versioned Items, and Deltas. The core identity machinery. A version v is a state of an evolving item i, formally v=(ps,vs): a point in product space AND a point in version space. The ITEM is anything put under version control at ANY granularity. A VERSIONED ITEM is 'a container for a set V of versions' — exactly Softland's object-container split. Versioning requires a SAMENESS CRITERION (decide two versions belong to the same item), provided by a unique identifier (OID). Within an item each version has a VERSION IDENTIFIER (VID): auto-number, symbolic name, or an expression (the intensional scheme). All versions share INVARIANTS; they differ in specific properties, and the difference is a DELTA — either SYMMETRIC (set-difference of properties) or DIRECTED / CHANGE (a sequence of operations op1...opm transforming v1 into v2, explicitly likened to database transaction logs). Warns deltas are not always small; commonality erodes as items evolve, with the escape being MULTILEVEL / RECURSIVE versioning (a version may itself have versions — Adele interfaces realized by body versions; DAMOKLES generalizes to fully recursive).

“v is characterized by a pair v = (ps, vs), where ps and vs denote a state in the product space and a point in the version space, respectively.”
“A versioned item is a container for a set V of versions.”
“Versioning requires a sameness criterion; that is, there must be some way to decide whether two versions belong to the same item.”
“a directed delta, also called a change, is a sequence of (elementary) change operations op1...opm which, when applied to one version v1, yields another version v2 (note the correspondence to transaction logs in databases).”

§ 3.2 Extensional and Intensional Versioning. The pivotal distinction for Softland's two-times problem. EXTENSIONAL versioning enumerates the set V = {v1,...,vn}: every version was explicitly checked in once, is retrievable, and is typically made IMMUTABLE (at check-in or via explicit freeze), guaranteeing safe reconstruction. INTENSIONAL versioning defines the set by a predicate V = {v | c(v)}: versions are implicit, constructed ON DEMAND, identified by an expression/query (a tuple of attribute values mapped by q: A1×...×An → V). Illustrative contrast: SCCS/RCS store-and-reconstruct text files (extensional) vs C conditional compilation building any source from preprocessor variable values (intensional). The paper insists these are NOT mutually exclusive and should be combined in one system.

“Extensional versioning means that V is defined by enumerating its members: V = {v1,...,vn}.”
“To ensure safe retrieval of previously constructed versions, versions can be made immutable.”
“Instead of enumerating its members, the version set is defined by a predicate: V = {v | c(v)}.”
“extensional and intensional versioning are by no means mutually exclusive, but can (and should) be combined into a single SCM system.”

§ 3.3 Intents of Evolution: Revisions, Variants, Cooperation. Defines the three orthogonal INTENTS behind making a version. A REVISION is intended to SUPERSEDE its predecessor, evolving along the TIME dimension (bug fixes, enhancements, library adaptation); old revisions are preserved rather than overwritten. A VARIANT is intended to COEXIST with siblings — parallel alternatives (OS, window system, storage/runtime tradeoffs). A COOPERATION version supports parallel developers, each in a WORKSPACE governed by import/export policies. This revision/variant separation is the conceptual spine of the version space.

“A version intended to supersede its predecessor is called a revision. Revisions evolve along the time dimension”
“Versions intended to coexist are called variants.”
“Instead of performing modifications by overwriting, old revisions are preserved”

§ 3.4 Representations of the Version Space: Version Graphs and Grids. How extensional version sets are drawn. A VERSION GRAPH has nodes (groups of versions) and edges (relationships); it presupposes an explicit set, so it pairs with EXTENSIONAL versioning. One-level uses a single SUCCESSOR relation in three shapes: sequence, tree, acyclic graph (multiple predecessors = merge). Two-level (RCS) adds BRANCHES with successor + offspring; ClearCase RECORDS MERGES, yielding a DAG where branches keep existing rather than joining. The key failure mode: version graphs handle variants only weakly — multidimensional variation causes COMBINATORIAL BRANCH EXPLOSION bounded by the product of domain cardinalities (foo: OS×DB×window = up to 27 branches). Escapes: classification-hierarchy clusters, or an n-dimensional GRID (Sciore) with an added orthogonal time dimension (Reichenberger).

“Since a version graph assumes an explicitly given set of versions, it is applied in conjunction with extensional versioning.”
“In the case of multidimensional variation, this approach breaks down because the number of branches explodes combinatorially.”
“ClearCase goes beyond the RCS organization by recording merges in the version graph.”

§ 3.5 State-Based and Change-Based Versioning. The SECOND great axis, declared explicitly ORTHOGONAL to extensional/intensional. STATE-BASED models describe a version by the STATE it represents (revisions + variants); changes are anonymous and must be DEDUCED from version-graph topology. CHANGE-BASED models describe a version by the CHANGES applied to a baseline; changes get Change Identifiers (CIDs) and attributes, giving a clean link to change requests. Change-based comes in extensional (change packages — documentation only) and intensional (the change-set model, Aide-de-Camp; changes freely composed as partial functions c: V→V; v = c1∘...∘cn(b)). The change space is drawn as an option grid (COV), a matrix (Aide-de-Camp), or a version graph with explicit change-labeled edges — which 'provides more information' than anonymous state-based graphs by recording WHEN each change applied across versions.

“Version models that focus on the states of versioned items are called state-based.”
“In change-based models, a version is described in terms of changes applied to some baseline... changes are assigned change identifiers (CID)”
“State- versus change-based is orthogonal to extensional versus intensional.”
“state-based versioning does not name the changes; that is, the changes are anonymous. As a consequence, the changes applied to a version must be deduced from the topology of the version graph.”

§ 4.1 AND/OR Graphs + Selection Order + Version Proliferation. The unifying mechanism JOINING product space and version space (Tichy 1982). Two node types: AND nodes (an object's specific versions/structure) and OR nodes (the versioned object = choice among versions); an unversioned product graph is all-AND, versioning adds OR nodes. AND edges carry both composition and dependency; an edge is BOUND if it ends at an AND node (specific version) and GENERIC if it ends at an OR node (unresolved choice). A CONFIGURATION is the subgraph reachable from a root; BOUND if all AND edges are bound, GENERIC otherwise (partially/totally generic). Binding = eliminating OR nodes by selecting one successor each — the precise vocabulary for pinned-vs-floating member binding. Models are classified by SELECTION ORDER: PRODUCT FIRST (SCCS/RCS, cannot express structural versioning), VERSION FIRST (PCTE/POEM, product version determines structure), INTERTWINED (ClearCase/Adele, supports structural versioning); the latter two version RELATIONSHIPS too. Cardinal warning: version-first triggers VERSION PROLIFERATION — a leaf bug-fix forces new versions of every ancestor — cured only by distinguishing versions of modules from versions of configurations.

“AND/OR graphs provide a general model for integrating product space and version space.”
“A relationship is bound to a specific version if the corresponding AND edge ends at an AND node; otherwise it is called generic.”
“in addition to objects, relationships are versioned as well.”
“in (b) new versions of all modules above c have to be created... This effect is called version proliferation... we have to distinguish between versions of modules and versions of configurations.”

§ 4.2 Granularity of Versioning (Component / Total / Product). The four-level / multilevel versioning core. Distinguishes EXTERNAL granularity (what the user sees versioned) from INTERNAL (much finer). Three external schemes: COMPONENT VERSIONING (only atomic leaves versioned; each has its own version graph; composition model; component graphs often only WEAKLY related, creating a selection problem); TOTAL VERSIONING (ALL composition levels versioned; composite objects are explicit first-class versioned entities; atomic+composite versioned UNIFORMLY; supports structural versioning — PCTE 'version first', ClearCase 'intertwined'); PRODUCT VERSIONING (all object versions in ONE global version space, a transparent single-version view — VOODOO, all change-based systems). Product versioning's global-view strength is also its weakness: NO MODULARITY — every change/revision/variant is global. Also separates VERSION granularity (size of a version) from DELTA granularity (unit deltas are recorded in) — RCS versions files but deltas at text-line level.

“Component versioning means that only atomic objects are put under version control. Each object has its own version space”
“Total versioning applies to all levels of the composition hierarchy.”
“Product versioning differs from total versioning by arranging versions of all objects in a uniform, global version space.”
“A potential problem consists of lacking modularity of the version space. All changes, revisions, and variants are global.”

§ 4.3 Deltas (Physical/Logical, Directed/Symmetric, Interleaved). Implementation of deltas, kept ORTHOGONAL to the external version model. Separates PHYSICAL deltas (storage/runtime efficiency) from LOGICAL deltas (user-facing difference). DIRECTED deltas (Tichy/RCS) reconstruct by applying a change sequence to a base (RCS: backward on trunk, forward on branches). EMBEDDED deltas store all versions overlapping with shared fragments — either each version points to its fragments, or fragments carry CONTROL EXPRESSIONS marking which versions they appear in (INTERLEAVED deltas — SCCS, DSEE; lines tagged with their version set). Explicit claim: internal delta representation is orthogonal to external model — interleaved deltas can realize extensional OR intensional, state OR change-based. AND/OR graphs are themselves classifiable as embedded deltas; adding control expressions to constrain combinability turns them into an interleaved scheme.

“the internal delta representation is orthogonal to the external version model.”
“the fragments are decorated with control expressions for determining the versions in which they are visible (interleaved deltas)”
“Since versions are stored in an overlapping manner, AND/OR graphs can be classified as embedded deltas.”

§ 4.4 Relations Between Version Model and Data Model. Three architectural placements of versioning relative to the data model — directly relevant to whether Rama owns versioning or layers it. (1) VERSION MODEL ON TOP OF DATA MODEL: versioning is an ordinary DB application, data model unaware (PCTE, CoMa); simple but the DB can't store versions efficiently and the transaction manager ignores versioning, forcing awkward separate types for versioned-objects vs versions. (2) VERSION MODEL BUILT INTO DATA MODEL: data-definition language has versioned-object constructs, queries are version-aware (DAMOKLES, Adele); Adele uses ONE object type with common/modifiable/immutable attribute classes (updating an immutable attr mints a new revision). (3) DATA MODEL ON TOP OF VERSION MODEL: an instrumentable version ENGINE provides base delta storage + config rules, fully ORTHOGONAL to the data model so any data model plugs in (ICE, COV) — the most decoupled.

“the version model is completely orthogonal to the data model. This may be achieved through an instrumentable version engine that provides a basic delta storage and configuration rules by means of which specific version models may be expressed”
“Adele distinguishes between common attributes shared by all revisions, modifiable attributes whose values are revision-specific, and immutable attributes, where each update triggers the creation of a new revision.”

§ 5. Intensional Versioning (Combinability, Framework, Rules, Configurators, Merge). The combinatorial heart. Intensional versioning must reconcile COMBINABILITY (any version on demand) with CONSISTENCY CONTROL. The blowup is quantified: m modules in v versions → v^m configs; unconstrained change combination → 2^c configs. Change-based reduces the product-space problem but MOVES selection into the version space. Framework: a CONFIGURATION RULE has a PRODUCT PART (scope) + VERSION PART (selection), written p:v; a QUERY is a set of submitted rules; a CONFIGURATOR evaluates query against versioned-object-base + rule-base, satisfying version AND product constraints. Binding has a TIME axis: STATIC, DYNAMIC, or deferred to RUNTIME (Java applets). Rules are scoped (local/global/transitive-closure) and ordered by STRICTNESS: CONSTRAINT (mandatory) > PREFERENCE (optional if satisfiable) > DEFAULT (weakest). Configurators span paradigm×model into four cells, with state/rule-based using depth-first BACKTRACKING. MERGE tools combine versions/changes (raw / two-way / three-way-with-baseline), by semantic level (textual / syntactic / semantic / operation-based), distinguishing INCONSISTENCY (an op fails) from CONFLICT (contradictory non-commuting changes); conflicts can be pre-empted in version space via mutual-exclusion constraints c1∇c2 (COV).

“a configuration rule consists of a product part, which determines its scope in the product space, and a version part, which performs a selection in the version space”
“A constraint is a mandatory rule that must be satisfied... A preference is an optional rule... a default is also an optional rule, but is weaker than a preference”
“the configurator has to backtrack from the wrong selection.”
“conflict detection can be performed in the version space... Conflicting merges can be excluded by constraints of the form c1 ∇ c2 (mutual exclusion).”

§ 6. Version Models in SCM Systems (Taxonomy + 24 systems). Surveys 20+ influential systems via an EVOLUTION GRAPH and two orthogonal classification tables, insisting the taxonomy's value is SEPARATING aspects systems conflate (change-based ≠ intensional ≠ product-level). Concrete anchors: conditional compilation (fine-grained intensional, interleaved deltas, functional), SCCS (tree, hidden interleaved deltas), RCS (directed deltas, trunk+branches, symbolic names = threads through version graphs), PIE (1980 change-based layers/contexts — change-based is OLD), Gandalf/Adele (separate immutable interface from realization variants/revisions; constraints/preferences/defaults), DSEE→ClearCase (config thread, derived-object cache, virtual file system, dynamic binding, merge recording), COV/EPOS (change-oriented, option grid, ambition/choice = write/read filters), ICE (feature logic; configuration = unification of feature terms, inconsistency = failed unification), VOODOO (orthogonal revision/variant/object dimensions), DAMOKLES (recursive versioning built into the data model). Repeatedly shows the SAME duality realized both ways: change-based on top of version graphs (Asgard) AND version graphs on top of change-based rules (COV/ICE).

“an important contribution of this article is to distinguish among different aspects that used to be mingled together.”
“symbolic names define threads through the version graphs and make reconstruction of configurations easier (RCS)”
“A configuration is inconsistent if unification fails (empty intersection of value sets...) (ICE)”

§ 7. Related Work & Disciplines + 8. Conclusion. Reviews prior surveys (Tichy 1988; Feiler 1991's four models — checkout/checkin, composition, long-transaction, change-set — critiqued as non-orthogonal; Katz 1990, limited to version-graphs + extensional). Maps SCM to neighbors load-bearing for Softland: TEMPORAL DATABASES record evolution but cover only time (no variants/changes) and distinguish VALID TIME vs TRANSACTION TIME — a distinction the authors call irrelevant for SCM because software objects aren't real-world referents (Softland disagrees: arrival-vs-commit IS that distinction). SCHEMA VERSIONING (versioning the rule-base/metadata, rarely taken seriously). DEDUCTIVE DATABASES 'urgently needed' for intensional versioning. LONG TRANSACTIONS (ACID doesn't survive them; versions crucial to cooperation). Current systems handle only coarse dependencies and lack FINE-GRAINED traceability. The Conclusion argues real systems are CONVERGING (change-based built on version graphs and vice versa), forecasts a UNIFORM CUSTOMIZABLE model integrating both axes plus revisions+variants+source+derived, and predicts versioning will be PULLED OUT of SCM systems and MOVED INTO database systems, powered by a deductive DB.

“Temporal databases focus solely on the time dimension and cover neither variants nor change-based versioning. Furthermore, they often distinguish between valid time... and transaction time”
“Deductive capabilities are urgently needed for intensional versioning.”
“their underlying version models are converging... change-based versioning has been realized on top of version graphs and vice versa.”
“versioning can be pulled out of SCM systems and moved into database systems... This framework... must be customizable to suit the needs of a specific application.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (stable CONTAINER = identity; REVISIONS = immutable values; current-revision = state) — Gives our kernel its exact academic name and formalism. Section 3.1: 'A versioned item is a CONTAINER for a set V of versions' — our container is their versioned item. Their three-part identity machinery validates ours: identity needs a SAMENESS CRITERION (our container thread) provided by an OID (Section 2.1), each value needs a VID, and immutability is enforced at check-in or via explicit freeze (Section 3.2 = our immutable revisions). Adele's Figure-13 pattern is the sharpest mechanism: ONE object type with common/modifiable/IMMUTABLE attribute classes where 'each update triggers the creation of a new revision' — precisely current-revision-as-state with copy-on-write minting. SUPPORTS our kernel and supplies tested vocabulary.
  • The code-identity question (file vs path vs FQN vs AST vs git-hash; atomic unit = file or function?) — Directly addresses it and refuses a single answer — itself the lesson. Section 3.1: 'Versioning can be applied at ANY level of granularity, ranging from a software product down to text lines,' and the ITEM is 'anything that may be put under version control' (file, directory, entity, relationship, attribute). Section 2.1 separates external OID (name/path) from internal system OID, warning the name is not the identity. Section 4.2 splits EXTERNAL granularity (file/function) from INTERNAL (much finer). And representation changes identity comparison: text-file diff = differing lines, syntax-tree diff = differing syntactic units (Section 2). So 'file or function' resolves as: choose external granularity per workflow, keep internal finer, and make the OID/sameness-criterion explicit rather than inferring it from the path. WARNS against conflating name with identity — reinforcing our 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' rule.
  • Versioning as its own layer; two distinct times (arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions) — The paper's spine and our strongest borrow. Extensional vs intensional (3.2) maps onto our two times: Rama's arrival-time log = EXTENSIONAL (enumerated, immutable, reconstructable — partial persistence / 'when it arrived'), git-style minted branchable versions = the COMMITTED layer on top. The emphatic ruling — 'extensional and intensional are by no means mutually exclusive, but can (and SHOULD) be combined' — green-lights both times in one system. Section 4.4 gives the architecture decision: version-model-on-top vs built-into vs DATA-MODEL-ON-TOP-OF-version-engine (ICE/COV's orthogonal instrumentable engine = the decoupled layer we want). CAUTION (7.2): they dismiss valid-time/transaction-time as 'not relevant for SCM' — but Softland's arrival-vs-commit IS exactly that temporal-DB distinction, so we deliberately revive a separation they set aside. SUPPORTS the layer + NAMES both times.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (own history; member binding pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped) — Section 4.1 gives the exact mechanism. A configuration (= our grouping/local world) is a versioned object; member references are BOUND (pinned to a revision = AND edge to AND node) or GENERIC (floating = AND edge to OR node), with partially/totally-generic covering mixed binding. Effectivity/condition-scoped binding = configuration rules with product part + version part (5.3), scoped local/global/transitive-closure, ordered constraint>preference>default. The VERSION PROLIFERATION warning is a critical failure mode: naive version-first forces a new version of every enclosing grouping per member edit — fixed only by their rule to 'distinguish between versions of modules and versions of CONFIGURATIONS,' i.e. give the grouping its OWN history partly independent of members, exactly our requirement. GIVES MECHANISM + warns of a concrete failure mode.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-TYPE / node-DATA, must compose) — The AND/OR graph (4.1) is the composition engine: AND edges carry BOTH composition and dependency, and 'in addition to objects, RELATIONSHIPS are versioned as well' — our relation-type level versioned, not just nodes. Section 4.2's component/total/product versioning is the level-composition spectrum: TOTAL VERSIONING ('all levels of the composition hierarchy,' composite objects as first-class versioned entities, atomic+composite versioned UNIFORMLY) is 'version at every level and compose them.' Node-DATA versioning maps to their fine-grained internal granularity (deltas to text lines / long attributes). DAMOKLES' RECURSIVE versioning ('any version may be versioned in turn') formally guarantees the levels nest. SUPPORTS the four-level design with total/recursive versioning as realization — while warning (4.2) that a single GLOBAL version space (product versioning) sacrifices modularity, so our levels should stay distinct, not collapse.
  • Trail-to-code join (resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; DECLARED vs INFERRED change) — Section 3.5's state-based vs change-based axis IS the declared-vs-inferred distinction, formalized. CHANGE-BASED = DECLARED: changes carry CIDs and 'a version may be described in terms of the change REQUESTS it implements' — an agent saying what it did is a first-class change package. STATE-BASED = INFERRED: 'changes are anonymous... must be DEDUCED from the topology of the version graph' (reconstructed by diffing). The verdict favors us: the explicit-change graph 'provides MORE information' than the anonymous state graph because it records WHEN each change applied. For resolving path+timestamp-to-code, RCS symbolic names that 'define threads through the version graphs' are the join primitive. CAUTION (7.2): current systems lack FINE-GRAINED dependency traceability — our trail join must operate below file granularity to deliver what they couldn't. SUPPORTS declared-change as superior and names both sides.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (conflicting views representable, late-bound synthesis) — Section 3.3 VARIANTS are exactly preserved, coexisting disagreement: 'versions intended to COEXIST,' and ClearCase records merges so 'the branches are not joined; rather, each of them continues to exist' (3.4) — divergence kept, not overwritten. The merge taxonomy (5.5) is our late-bound synthesis vocabulary: INCONSISTENCY (an op fails) vs CONFLICT (contradictory non-commuting changes), with three-way merge consulting a baseline and synthesis deferred to a human when automatic resolution isn't safe. COV's mutual-exclusion constraints c1∇c2 mark known-conflicting views WITHOUT silently picking a winner — modeling plurality structurally. SUPPORTS preserved disagreement; offers conflict/inconsistency as synthesis primitives.
  • Substrate choices (Rama event-sourced; whether the substrate owns versioning) — Section 4.4 + Conclusion speak straight to Rama. Their 3.1 directed-delta = change explicitly 'corresponds to transaction logs in databases' — Rama's append-only log IS a change/directed-delta substrate, giving us extensional change-based versioning for free. But 4.4 warns that putting the version model purely ON TOP of an unaware data model means 'no support for storing versions efficiently' and 'the transaction manager does not take versioning into account' — Rama-as-substrate must be deliberately made version-aware, not assumed. The Conclusion's prophecy — 'versioning can be pulled out of SCM systems and MOVED INTO database systems... customizable to suit the needs of a specific application' — directly endorses building versioning as a reusable substrate layer (our intent), ideally with deductive/rule evaluation for the intensional (committed/branch) side. SUPPORTS substrate-owned versioning, with an efficiency/awareness caveat.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the pair of ORTHOGONAL axes — extensional-vs-intensional and state-vs-change — sitting atop a clean product-space/version-space split, all unified by AND/OR graphs and configuration rules. For Softland this directly licenses our two-times design: Rama's arrival-time append log is extensional change-based versioning (their directed-delta = transaction log), and our git-style minted/branchable layer is the intensional side, and the paper's emphatic ruling is that these 'are by no means mutually exclusive, but can (and SHOULD) be combined.' It also hands us the exact kernel vocabulary (versioned item = container; OID = sameness criterion; VID; immutable revisions; bound vs generic member binding) and one concrete failure mode to engineer around — VERSION PROLIFERATION, where naive version-first grouping forces a new version of every ancestor, fixed only by giving groupings their own configuration-level version history distinct from their members.

6. Making Data Structures Persistent

James R. Driscoll, Neil Sarnak, Daniel D. Sleator, Robert E. Tarjan · 1989 · Journal of Computer and System Sciences (JCSS), Vol. 38, No. 1 (condensed preliminary version appeared at the 18th ACM STOC, 1986)

In one line. Defines the partial/full persistence taxonomy and gives three general techniques — fat nodes, node copying, node splitting — that make any bounded-in-degree linked structure persistent at O(1) amortized space and time overhead per update, then applies them to balanced search trees.

Source access: full. ACM (dl.acm.org/citation.cfm?id=64317) returned HTTP 403. Read the full open-access author copy hosted on Dan Sleator's CMU page: https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~sleator/papers/another-persistence.pdf — the complete 39-page JCSS reprint (pp. 86-124, all sections, all figures, references). Every section summary and quote below is from that PDF read directly, not from a secondary summary.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the dichotomy that organizes the whole paper: ordinary structures are 'ephemeral' — a change destroys the old version — whereas a persistent structure preserves access to old versions. The paper promises 'simple, systematic, and efficient techniques for making linked data structures persistent' and a headline application: persistent binary search trees with logarithmic access/insert/delete time and O(1) space per insertion or deletion.

“Ordinary data structures are ephemeral in the sense that making a change to the structure destroys the old version, leaving only the new one.”
“We use our techniques to devise persistent forms of binary search trees with logarithmic access, insertion, and deletion times and O(1) space bounds for insertion and deletion.”

§ 1. Introduction — definitions and model. Defines the two persistence levels precisely: partially persistent = all versions readable but only the newest is modifiable; fully persistent = every version both readable and modifiable. It formalizes a 'linked data structure' as nodes with a fixed set of named fields (information fields or pointer fields) and constant out-degree, accessed via named 'access pointers' to entry nodes — modeled as a labeled directed graph. It distinguishes access operations (read-only, building an 'accessed set' of nodes) from update operations (intermixed access + update steps that create nodes or change one field/pointer). Versions are indexed by the update that produced them (version 0 = empty). The paper notes that, prior to Overmars, persistence had been handled only by ad hoc constructions, and that a systematic study (growing out of Sarnak's PhD thesis) is the goal. Bounds are stated as amortized (worst-case averaged over a sequence).

“The structure is partially persistent if all versions can be accessed but only the newest version can be modified, and fully persistent if every version can be both accessed and modified.”
“We define a linked data structure to be a finite collection of nodes, each containing a fixed number of named fields.”
“By amortized cost we mean the cost of an operation averaged over a worst-case sequence of operations.”

§ 2.1 Known Methods (Overmars). Reviews three prior partial-persistence schemes and why each is too costly, motivating the paper's contribution. (a) Store every version explicitly by copying the whole structure each update: Θ(n) time and space per update. (b) Store no versions, only the update log, and rebuild on access: O(m) space but Ω(i) rebuild time for version i. (c) A hybrid storing every k-th version plus the log, which gives a time-space tradeoff but, for any k, blows up either space or access time by a √m factor. Bentley-Saxe dynamization adds a logarithmic blowup. The diagnosis is sharp: these are poor precisely because they assume almost nothing about the underlying structure.

“One reason that the results of Overmars are so poor is that he assumes very little about the underlying ephemeral structure.”
“By restricting our attention to linked structures and focusing on the details of the update steps, we are able to obtain much better results.”

§ 2.2 The Fat Node Method (partial). The first technique: never erase a field value — instead let each node grow 'fat' by holding an arbitrary number of stamped values per field. Each node and each extra value carries a version stamp; at most one value per field per version is kept (a same-version rewrite overwrites). Reads navigate by selecting, for field f in version i, the value with the maximum stamp ≤ i. An auxiliary access array stores the access-pointer values per version so any version's entry point is reached in O(1). Cost: O(1) worst-case space per update step, but O(log m) per access/update step because choosing among many stamped values in a node requires a search tree on stamps — a logarithmic-factor slowdown. Two drawbacks: fat nodes need linked fixed-size representations, and the per-step search costs the log factor.

“Our first idea is to record all changes made to node fields in the nodes themselves, without erasing old values of the fields.”
“Each extra field value has an associated field name and a version stamp. The version stamp indicates the version in which the named field was changed to have the specified value.”
“If the values of a field within a fat node are ordered by version stamp and stored in a binary search tree, simulating an access or update step takes O(log m) time.”

§ 2.3 The Node-Copying Method (partial). The technique that removes the fat-node log factor: persistent nodes hold only a FIXED number of field values (d original pointers + e extra slots + 1 copy pointer). Extra slots absorb a bounded number of stamped changes; when a node overflows, a fresh copy holding only the newest values is made, and the copy must be threaded into all predecessors in the newest version (using maintained inverse pointers), which can cascade. Each ephemeral node maps to a 'family' — a copy-pointer-linked list of persistent nodes, the last being 'live.' A potential-function (amortized) argument proves O(1) amortized space AND time per update step, with O(1) WORST-CASE access — the best of both. This is the paper's main partial-persistence result for bounded in-degree.

“We eliminate the drawbacks of fat nodes with our second idea, node copying. We allow nodes in the persistent structure to hold only a fixed number of field values.”
“Each ephemeral node corresponds to a set of persistent nodes, called a family.”
“Hence the amortized space cost of the entire update operation is O(t) = O(u), i.e., O(1) per update step. The same analysis shows that the amortized time per update step is also O(1).”

§ 3.1 Version Tree and Version List (full persistence). Full persistence is harder because, when you can update ANY version, versions form a partially-ordered 'version tree' (version i is the parent of j if j is obtained by updating i), not a line — so simple numeric stamps no longer order navigation. The fix: impose a total order consistent with the tree, recorded as a 'version list' (a preorder of the tree) with the crucial property that all descendants of i appear consecutively starting at i. Navigation then needs an order query — 'does version i precede j in the list?' — solved by the Dietz-Sleator list-order structure in O(1) query and O(1) amortized insert (or O(1) worst-case with a more complex variant).

“The versions of a fully persistent structure are only partially ordered. This partial ordering is defined by a rooted version tree.”
“We impose a total ordering on the versions consistent with the partial ordering defined by the version tree. We represent this total ordering by a list... the version list.”
“For any version i, the descendants of i in the version tree occur consecutively in the version list, starting with i.”

§ 3.2 Fat Node Method, full (valid intervals). Fat nodes extend to full persistence by comparing version stamps by position in the version LIST rather than numerically. Inserting a new version mid-list means an update may have to store TWO new field values, not one, because it splits an existing value's range. The key new concept is the 'valid interval': each stored value is valid over the span of version-list positions until the next stamp for that field — and these intervals partition the node's version range. Gives full persistence at O(1) worst-case space per update and O(log m) (more precisely O(log h), h = max changes to a node) per access/update — same asymptotics as the partial fat-node case.

“Updating differs slightly from what happens in the partially persistent case, because the insertion of new versions in the middle of the version list makes it... necessary to store two updated field values per update step, rather than one.”
“The valid intervals of the values of a field f in a fat node x partition the interval of versions from the version stamp of x to the last version.”

§ 3.3 The Node-Splitting Method (full, O(1)). The paper's hardest construction: a B-tree-style variant of node copying achieving full persistence at O(1) amortized space/time per update and O(1) worst-case access. To support modifying old versions, the ephemeral structure is first 'augmented' with explicit inverse (predecessor) pointers, giving symmetry invariants (x→y iff y→x). When a persistent node overflows, instead of full copy it SPLITS, moving roughly half the extra pointers to a new node so both retain room — like B-tree splits. Updates run in two phases: phase one records changes (possibly making pointers 'improper' or 'overlapping'); phase two is a cascading repair of alternating pointer-copying and node-splitting until a 'pointer invariant' (all pointers proper and non-overlapping) is restored. A potential argument over vacant extra slots proves O(1) amortized. The authors note kinship to fractional cascading.

“The major difference between node splitting and node copying is that in the former, when a node overflows, a new copy is created and roughly half the extra pointers are moved from the old copy to the new one, thereby leaving space in both... for later updates.”
“The second phase repairs this damage by judiciously copying pointers... a cascading process of alternating pointer copying and node splitting, which ends when the pointer invariant is entirely restored.”
“We note in conclusion that our node-splitting process is very similar to the fractional cascading method of Chazelle and Guibas.”

§ 4. Persistent Balanced Search Trees (red-black, partial). Applies the techniques to a concrete structure: red-black trees, whose color constraints bound depth at 2 log₂ n so access is O(log n), with rebalancing by O(1)-time rotations and recolorings. A bottom-up strategy gives O(1) AMORTIZED recolorings per update. Making them partially persistent via node copying with e=1 extra pointer per node yields O(m) space for m insertions/deletions, O(log n) access to any version, O(log n) worst-case insert/delete. The method generalizes to weight-balanced trees, weak B-trees, and finger search trees, always at only a constant-factor blowup over the ephemeral structure.

“The color constraints imply that the depth of an n-node red-black tree is at most 2 log₂ n.”
“A partially persistent red-black tree representing a history of m insertions and deletions occupies O(m) space and allows O(log n)-time access to any version.”

§ 4.2 Full Persistence via Lazy Recoloring. To make red-black trees FULLY persistent with node splitting, two obstacles arise: amortized (not worst-case) recoloring, and the inability to overwrite old colors. The solution is 'lazy recoloring' (refining Tsakalidis): postpone all recoloring except at path ends, encoding deferred changes as vertex-disjoint 'recoloring intervals' (insertion/deletion intervals) that store header info — a flag and the bottom item — in their top node. Maintaining these intervals disjoint, and shrinking/splitting them with O(1) recolorings, stops color-change propagation and converts the O(1)-amortized recoloring bound into O(1) WORST-CASE update time. This beats the only competitive prior method (full path copying, due to Myers, Krijnen-Meertens, Reps-Teitelbaum-Demers, Swart) by a logarithmic factor in space.

“We exploit this observation by postponing all the recoloring except that at the path ends.”
“The crucial property of recoloring intervals is that they stop the propagation of color changes during an insertion or deletion.”
“Their method... requires O(log n) space and time in the worst case per insertion or deletion. Thus our method saves a logarithmic factor in space.”

§ 5. Fully Persistent Balanced Trees Revisited (displaced storage). An alternative giving O(1) WORST-CASE space and O(log n) worst-case time (not amortized). The new idea is 'displaced storage of changes': instead of recording a change in the node it applies to, store it in a possibly different node on the access path in the new version, keeping 'displacement paths' sufficiently disjoint. Navigation switches from a version list to a 'traversal list' (each version twice — preorder + postorder) for O(1) ancestor queries. The structure maintains three displacement-path invariants and supports shrinking/splitting of displacement paths. The authors leave the most elegant variant of displaced storage as future research.

“Instead of indicating a change to an ephemeral node x by storing the change in the corresponding persistent node x̄, we store information about the change in some possibly different node that lies on the access path to x̄ in the new version.”
“We obtain a fully persistent form of red-black trees with the same resource bounds as in Section 4, but the O(1) space bound and the O(log n) time bound per insertion or deletion are worst-case rather than amortized.”
“We leave as a topic for future research the discovery of the most elegant and practical variant of the method.”

§ 6. Applications, Extensions, Open Problems. Catalogs what becomes persistent: O(1)-per-op persistent stacks/queues (linked lists) and deques (doubly-linked lists); partially persistent red-black trees with planar point-location applications (companion Sarnak-Tarjan paper, O(log n) query, O(n) space); fully persistent trees for text editing (Myers), program editing (Reps et al.), and high-level languages (Krijnen-Meertens). A theoretical highlight: node copying can be made write-once except for access pointers, implying any bounded-in-degree augmented-LISP structure (with replaca/replacd) is simulable in linear time in pure LISP (cons/car/cdr only) — illuminating the power of purely applicative languages. Four open problems are flagged: (i) make node copying/splitting O(1) WORST-CASE per step; (ii) CONFLUENT updates combining two old versions (the version tree becomes a DAG — i.e. merge); (iii) updates that change many versions at once; (iv) a better-than-fat-node method for UNBOUNDED in-degree, where copying/splitting break down.

“Any data structure built using augmented LISP (in which replaca and replacd are allowed) can be simulated in linear time in pure LISP (in which only cons, car, and cdr are allowed), provided that each node in the structure has constant bounded in-degree.”
“Find a way to allow update operations that combine two or more old versions of the structure. This would allow concatenation of lists... the version tree of Section 3 becomes a directed acyclic graph.”
“The node-copying and node-splitting methods completely break down in this [unbounded in-degree] case; the fat node method needs only O(1) space per update step but increases access times by a logarithmic factor.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer — Rama arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit git-style committed/minted versions (full persistence) — GIVES IT A NAME and a precise formal distinction. The paper's central taxonomy IS our two-times split: partially persistent = 'all versions can be accessed but only the newest version can be modified' (a linear log you read but only append to the head — Rama arrival-time history); fully persistent = 'every version can be both accessed and modified' (branch from any committed point — git). The paper proves both are achievable at O(1) amortized overhead, and crucially shows full persistence is strictly harder because its versions form a TREE not a line ('the versions of a fully persistent structure are only partially ordered... defined by a rooted version tree'). This validates that our two times are genuinely different layers, not the same mechanism viewed twice — and warns that the committed/branchable layer needs extra machinery (a version tree + a total-order 'version list') that the arrival-log layer does not.
  • Object-container kernel — stable CONTAINER = identity thread, REVISIONS = immutable values inside, current-revision = state — OFFERS A MECHANISM. The 'fat node' is the canonical implementation of a container: one stable node identity that 'hold[s] an arbitrary number of values of each field,' every value tagged with a version stamp, navigation = 'choosing among several such values the one with maximum version stamp no greater than i.' This is exactly container-with-revisions: the node is the identity thread, each stamped field-value is an immutable revision, current = newest stamp. The paper hands us the read rule (rightmost-stamp-not-after-i) and the write rule (never erase, only append a stamped value) for free.
  • Hard rule: 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' / DECLARED vs INFERRED change — SUPPORTS IT with a sharp engineering reason. The whole paper assumes changes are DECLARED — update operations explicitly name the node and field they mutate, and persistence records exactly that declared delta with a version stamp. It never reconstructs history by diffing two states; it stores the change as it happens. The Section 6 LISP result is the payoff: an augmented-LISP program using declared mutations (replaca/replacd) 'can be simulated in linear time in pure LISP.' Declared mutation is cheap and exact; the paper's amortized O(1) bounds depend on knowing the change rather than inferring it. Our INFERRED path (diff-reconstruct) is the lossy fallback the paper deliberately avoids.
  • Four-level graph versioning — graph / node / relation-TYPE / node-DATA must version AND compose — OFFERS A MECHANISM and a partial WARNING. The fat-node method versions a node's identity, its DATA (information fields), and its RELATIONS (pointer fields) independently, each with its own stamp — that is node-level + node-data-level + relation-instance-level versioning composing under one stamp ordering. So three of our four levels have a worked, composing implementation. WARNING: the paper's clean O(1) bounds require 'constant bounded in-degree'; for unbounded in-degree only the costlier fat-node (log-factor) survives — graph-level edges with high fan-in (relation TYPES touching many nodes) are exactly the open problem (vi) the authors flag as unsolved. Our graph-level and relation-TYPE-level versioning live in that harder regime.
  • Groupings as versioned objects — a curated grouping / local world has its OWN version history, partly independent of members; binding pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped — OFFERS A MECHANISM — the 'valid interval' / effectivity primitive. Each stored value has a 'valid interval... the interval of versions in the version list from the version stamp of v up to but not including the next version stamp' — i.e. a field value is bound to a node only over an explicit version range. This is precisely effectivity/condition-scoped membership: a grouping's pointer to a member can be PINNED (single stamp) or FLOATING/scoped (a valid interval that auto-extends until overwritten). The valid-interval partition gives groupings a self-owned timeline whose member bindings each carry their own validity window.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality — conflicting views representable, synthesis late-bound — PARTIAL SUPPORT + a clean WARNING about its limit. Full persistence (the version tree with sibling branches) is exactly preserved divergence: two updates off the same parent produce two co-existing versions, neither overwriting the other — branches ARE preserved disagreement. But the paper STOPS at branching: it explicitly lists CONFLUENT merge as unsolved — open problem (ii), 'allow update operations that combine two or more old versions... the version tree of Section 3 becomes a directed acyclic graph.' So the paper supports our plural-branches but warns that late-bound SYNTHESIS (merging branches back) is genuinely hard and was left open in 1989 (later named 'confluent persistence').
  • The trail-to-code join — resolve a trail's path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment — OFFERS THE CORE MECHANISM. Resolving 'code at version i' is exactly the paper's access primitive: navigate to the node, then pick the field value whose stamp is 'rightmost in the version list but not to the right of i.' A trail's timestamp maps to a version stamp; the path maps to the access pointers / entry nodes (the paper keeps 'an access array, one per access pointer name, to store the various versions of the access pointers' so 'initiating access into any version takes O(1) time'). That access-array-of-roots is the index our join needs: given (path, t) → root-at-t → walk → value-at-t.
  • Substrate choices — Rama event-sourced, multi-LLM, code-identity (file vs function) — SUPPORTS Rama's arrival-log model and FRAMES the atomic-unit choice. 'The event log is primary, rendered state is projection' is the paper's own stance inverted into truth: it stores the sequence of stamped update steps and treats every version as a projection navigable from that log — partial persistence is literally 'rebuild the version from the change log.' On code-identity: the paper defines the atomic versioned unit as the NODE (fixed named fields, constant bounded in-degree for the good bounds). The lesson for file-vs-function: the cheaper your atomic unit's fan-in, the cheaper versioning — a function (low in-degree, stable field set) behaves like the paper's well-behaved node; a whole file (high churn, many referrers) drifts toward the unbounded-in-degree open problem. This is an argument that the function, not the file, is the natural container.

Takeaway. This paper is the formal backbone for Softland's 'versioning as its own layer': it names and separates exactly our two times — partial persistence (read-any-version, modify-only-newest = Rama's arrival-time log) versus full persistence (modify-any-version = git-style branchable committed versions) — and proves both are achievable at O(1) amortized overhead, while showing full persistence is fundamentally harder because its versions form a version TREE (with a total-ordered version-list for navigation), not a line. Its fat-node mechanism is a ready-made object-container kernel (stable node identity + immutable stamped revisions + a 'rightmost-stamp-≤-i' read rule), and its 'valid interval' primitive directly models effectivity/floating member bindings for groupings. Two warnings are load-bearing for us: cheap O(1) versioning requires constant bounded in-degree (favoring the function, not the high-fan-in file, as the atomic container), and confluent MERGE of branches — our late-bound synthesis of preserved disagreement — was explicitly left open here (the version tree becoming a DAG), confirming it is the genuinely hard part of our four-level graph-versioning ambition.

7. Pijul model — the patch-based model ('How Pijul works' essay + FAQ on pijul.org), grounded in 'A Categorical Theory of Patches' (Mimram & Di Giusto, 2013)

Pierre-Étienne Meunier & Florent Becker (Pijul); formal theory by Samuel Mimram & Cinzia Di Giusto (CEA, LIST) · 2013 (theory) / 2017+ (Pijul site) · pijul.org documentation/blog; theory in Electronic Notes in Theoretical Computer Science (MFPS), arXiv:1311.3903

In one line. A file is a graph of identity-bearing line-vertices; a patch is a morphism that adds vertices or remaps edge-status; merge is a categorical pushout, and conflicts are first-class objects in the pushout-completed category rather than errors.

Source access: full. pijul.org/model is a SvelteKit SPA serving no static prose; I extracted the actual page content (FAQ + 'How Pijul works' model essay + 2017 blog posts) from the JS chunk /_app/immutable/chunks/fTENTFLE.js by stripping KaTeX/SVG. The site states the model 'merges concepts from category theory' and links the formal paper. To honestly ground the pushout/free-monoid/conflict-completion claims (which the site only summarizes informally), I fetched and read the full text of the underlying paper, Mimram & Di Giusto 'A Categorical Theory of Patches' (arXiv:1311.3903, PDF via pdftotext, 1869 lines). Verbatim quotes below are sourced from whichever of the two I actually read.

The paper, section by section

§ FAQ: Why a new VCS? (pijul.org). Frames the whole project as resolving a two-camp split in version control: snapshot-based systems (git, mercurial, svn) are fast but hard to use beyond simple operations, while patch-based systems (darcs) are easy and have nice algebraic properties but historically slow. Pijul's pitch is that it COMBINES the two by 'representing data in a more general way that allows efficient patch operations,' with merges and conflicts as the place this generality matters most. The FAQ explicitly indicts three-way merge as algorithmically wrong, not merely buggy, citing real cases where git/mercurial/svn 'just do the wrong thing.'

“There are basically two approaches to version control: snapshot-based (git, mercurial, svn) or patch-based (darcs).”
“Pijul combines these approaches, representing data in a more general way that allows efficient patch operations.”
“there are real-world examples where Git, Mercurial and SVN just do the wrong thing”

§ How repositories used to work — the graph-of-lines model (essay). Defines the core data structure: a single file is a directed graph G=(V,E) of lines, each vertex a line, each edge labelled by a change number meaning 'according to change c, line u comes before line v.' The decisive design choice is that vertices are UNIQUELY IDENTIFIED by the hash of the change that introduced them plus a position within that change — so two textually identical lines from different changes are different objects, and a line keeps its identity even when its change is applied in a wholly different context. This is content-independent, provenance-based identity.

“a single file [is represented] by a directed graph G=(V,E) of lines, where each vertex represented a line”
“vertices are uniquely identified, by the hash of the change that introduced them, along with a position in that change”
“a line keeps its identity, even if the change is applied in a totally different context”

§ Append-only deletion and the two action kinds (essay). Deletion never removes data: the system is append-only, and a deletion is a change that REMAPS the edge introduced by an earlier change from 'alive' to a 'dead' status, labelled by the deleting change's name. Live edges are 'alive', removed ones 'dead' (drawn dashed). From this the essay claims a minimal action vocabulary: there are exactly TWO kinds of action — one adds vertices with surrounding alive edges, the other maps an existing edge label onto a different one. Edge labels carry two parameters: a status (alive/deleted/others) and the introducing change.

“this system is append-only, in the sense that deletions are handled by a more sophisticated labelling of the edges”
“We have just described the two basic kinds of actions in Pijul. There are no other.”
“the edge labels are given by two parameters: their status ... and the change that introduced them”

§ Dependencies (essay). Changes form a dependency relation derived from the graph: a change that ADDS a vertex depends on the changes that introduced its context (the lines immediately before and after the insertion point); a change that DELETES a vertex (remaps an edge introduced by change c) depends on c. The essay stresses this is the MINIMAL dependency set needed for the text edits to make sense, and that higher-level semantic dependencies can be layered on via hooks. This dependency closure is what makes patches partially (not totally) ordered and independently transferable.

“If a change adds a vertex, we must have its 'context', i.e. the lines before and after it”
“this is just the minimal set of dependencies needed to make sense of the text edits”
“Hooks and scripts may add extra language-dependent dependencies based on semantics”

§ Why keep change-labels on edges? (essay). A minimality probe: could edges store only status (alive/dead) and drop the introducing-change label? The essay shows two failure modes that force keeping the label. (1) Change inverses: if two authors delete the same line in parallel and one reverts, label-free statuses would merge the two deletions into one and a single revert would wrongly resurrect the line; keeping per-change labels distinguishes the two deletions so revert only undoes its own. (2) Missing contexts: when Alice edits inside a paragraph that Bob deletes in parallel, the situation is asymmetric, and only the change-labels let one side detect that Alice's edit lands in context Bob marked deleted. So the change-id on the edge is not redundant; it is what makes revert and conflict-detection correct.

“why even keep the change number on the edges?”
“the two deletions get merged into one, and the inverse applies to both ... this is not what we expect”
“the context of Alice's edits is labelled as deleted in his repository”

§ Patches commute / associativity vs three-way merge (2017 blog). Argues the practical payoff of the patch algebra: patches COMMUTE, enabling patches that touch only one part of the repo and stay compatible with concurrent work elsewhere; and in the darcs lineage patches are ASSOCIATIVE, 'a really useful property to have in a VCS.' The post is emphatic that three-way merge's misbehavior 'is not a bug/implementation mistake ... It's a problem in the algorithm itself' — i.e. snapshot-diff merging is structurally, not incidentally, wrong, which is the motivation for an algebraic model.

“patches are associative, which is a really useful property to have in a VCS”
“This is not a bug/implementation mistake in 3-way merge/git ... It's a problem in the algorithm itself.”
“patch commutation [enables] patches touching just one part of the repository, compatible with the rest of the work”

§ Theory — Category L of files and patches (Mimram & Di Giusto, §2). The formal underpinning the site cites. A file is a function A:[n]→L assigning lines (from a fixed set L) to positions 0..n-1. A patch (morphism) between files A:[m]→L and B:[n]→L is an INJECTIVE INCREASING PARTIAL function f:[m]→[n] that preserves line content where defined. Category L has files as objects and patches as morphisms; it is strictly monoidal (concatenation of files), and Proposition 2 shows every patch is generated by two primitives — insertion η_a:I→a and deletion ε_a:a→I — i.e. the same two-action vocabulary the Pijul essay describes, here proven to be a free monoidal category. This is the exact formal version of 'there are only two kinds of action.'

“A morphism between two files ... is an injective increasing partial function ... Such a morphism is called a patch.”
“The category L has files as objects and patches as morphisms.”
“patches are generated by the operations of inserting and deleting a line”

§ Theory — Merge as pushout; conflicts as non-existent pushouts (§3). Merging the effects of two coinitial patches (the diverging g,h from a shared file) is DEFINED as the pushout of the diagram — the universal way to combine two independent edits. The key theorem: not every pair of coinitial patches has a pushout in L, and exactly those that don't are the CONFLICTING ones. The fix is to embed L into a larger category P obtained by the FREE COMPLETION under finite colimits (initial object + all pushouts), so that conflicting files become first-class objects 'in the most general way possible.' These generalized files are finite sets of lines with only a PARTIAL (transitive) order rather than a linear one — the conflict is literally an unresolved partial order, and the paper even draws it as git's <<<<<<< HEAD / ======= / >>>>>>> markers to contrast snapshot tools' textual conflict with Pijul's structural one.

“the operation of merging the effect of two coinitial patches is defined by pushout”
“not every diagram in L has a pushout ... the two patches f1 and f2 are said to be conflicting”
“its objects are finite sets labeled by lines equipped with a transitive relation and morphisms are partial functions”
“patches are only partially ordered ... there is no global notion of 'time'”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest.' — STRONG SUPPORT + a mechanism. Pijul gives each line a stable identity = hash(introducing-change) + position, independent of content or context. That IS a container: the identity thread is the vertex, its successive alive/dead status are the revisions. Critically, identity is DECLARED by the change that introduced it, never INFERRED by diffing content — 'two lines with the same content, introduced by different changes, will be different.' This is exactly our 'inferred structure is never truth at ingest' rule, validated at line granularity. The kernel should mint a container id at the declaring event, not reconstruct it.
  • The code-identity question: is identity the file, path, name/FQN, AST, or git hash? Is the atomic unit the file or the function? — OFFERS A NAMED ANSWER at a finer grain than we'd considered. Pijul's atomic unit is the LINE, and its identity is neither content nor position but provenance: the change-hash that introduced it plus an offset. This warns against our snapshot instincts (path/hash) and AST-normalization: identity should be the introducing-event id, so a unit 'keeps its identity even if the change is applied in a totally different context.'
