Translated up: a function's identity could be the kernel event that first declared it, surviving moves/renames — closer to git-hash-of-introducing-commit than to FQN or AST.
  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time (partial persistence) vs committed/minted (full persistence); two times. — WARNS + clarifies. Pijul's deepest claim is that patches are only PARTIALLY ordered — 'there is no global notion of time' — and that linear snapshot history (git) imposes a false total order that produces silently-wrong merges. This validates our two-times split but pushes harder: the committed/true-time layer is a DAG/partial order, not a line, and commutation (independent patches can be reordered) is the property that makes branching-from-any-point clean. Our 'minted version you can branch from' should be a partially-ordered patch set, not a linear commit chain.
  • Groupings as versioned objects; member binding pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped. — OFFERS A MECHANISM via dependencies. In Pijul a change carries a minimal DEPENDENCY set: an insertion depends on the changes that introduced its surrounding context; a deletion depends on the change that introduced the deleted edge. A grouping-version is then a closed set of changes under dependency. This is a model for 'pinned vs floating' membership: pinning = include a specific change-id; floating = include whatever satisfies the dependency closure. 'Hooks and scripts may add extra language-dependent dependencies based on semantics' maps directly to effectivity/condition-scoped binding.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-TYPE / node-DATA) that must COMPOSE. — GIVES A COMPOSITIONAL SUBSTRATE. Pijul versions a file as a graph G=(V,E) where vertices (nodes) and edges (relations) are BOTH versioned by the same primitive — a change either adds vertices+edges or remaps an edge's label (status). Node identity, edge existence (alive/dead), and edge type (status enum) are all expressed in one append-only edge-relabeling calculus, so the levels compose by construction. This is direct evidence that node-level and relation-type-level versioning can share one event vocabulary rather than four bolted-on schemes — and the categorical paper proves the whole thing composes (associativity of patch composition).
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing). — STRONG SUPPORT — this is Pijul's central thesis transposed. Pijul exists because three-way merge INFERS structure by diffing snapshots and 'just does the wrong thing'; Pijul instead records the change as a first-class declared object with explicit context/dependencies. Our agent trails are declared edit operations — so we are already on Pijul's side of the line. The mapping: store the trail's declared ops as the truth and treat diff-reconstruction only as fallback/verification, exactly as Pijul stores patches not snapshots.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicting views representable, not overwritten; synthesis late-bound. — GIVES IT A FORMAL NAME AND A FAILURE MODE TO AVOID. This is the paper's headline result: a conflict is the case where the pushout (merge) does NOT exist in the base category, and the fix is to WORK IN THE COMPLETED CATEGORY P where conflicting files exist as first-class objects — 'finite sets labeled by lines equipped with a transitive (partial, not linear) relation.' A conflict is a value, not an error; it persists until resolved by a later patch ('resolve a conflict once — pulling never brings it back'). This is precisely late-bound synthesis over preserved plurality, with a proof that it's the universal (most general) way to represent disagreement.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced, back-arrow rule, append-only log as primary. — SUPPORT + design discipline. Pijul's model is append-only at its core — 'this system is append-only; deletions are handled by a more sophisticated labelling of the edges' (a dead edge, never a removed one). That is event-sourcing for code structure: state is a projection of an append-only set of change-events, identity is the event id. It validates Rama-as-truth: never mutate a node, append a status-remap event. The warning: keep the change-id ON the relation (edge label), because dropping it breaks inverse/revert and missing-context detection — analogous to keeping provenance on every Rama fact.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution to our versioning/identity question is the principle that IDENTITY AND CHANGE ARE DECLARED, NEVER INFERRED FROM CONTENT: every line carries a provenance-based identity (hash of the introducing change + position) that survives moving, copying, and re-application, and every edit is a first-class patch object — never a snapshot to be diffed. From this one move Pijul gets the two things we want most: (1) conflicts/disagreement become first-class VALUES (objects of the pushout-completed category, a preserved partial order) instead of errors silently resolved by a lossy three-way merge, and (2) versions are partially-ordered, commuting patch-sets you can branch and synthesize late from — exactly our committed/minted layer over an append-only log. It is direct evidence that 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' is not just a safety rule but the foundation a correct version model is built on.

8. A Categorical Theory of Patches

Samuel Mimram, Cinzia Di Giusto · 2013 · arXiv (cs.LO), CEA LIST; later in ENTCS (MFPS 2013)

In one line. Models files as objects and patches as identity-preserving partial functions in a category L; defines merge as pushout, and since conflicting patches have no pushout, builds the free finite cocompletion P whose objects are finite label-sets with a transitive relation — giving conflicts a first-class mathematical representation.

Source access: full. Read the full PDF (including Appendix A) via pdftotext on the arXiv source arXiv:1311.3903v1 saved locally by WebFetch. WebFetch itself could not parse the FlateDecode binary; pdftotext extracted the complete 14-page text including all sections, examples, references, and the geometric-interpretation appendix.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the complementary-to-implementation strategy: rather than verify a vcs implementation, introduce a theoretical model 'defined abstractly by the universal property that it should satisfy' and work out a concrete description. Defines a category of files and patches where merging two coinitial patches is a PUSHOUT. Observes the pushout need not exist (incompatible patches), raising the question of the correct category for files in conflicting state. Answers via the free completion of the file category under finite colimits, with an explicit description: objects are finite sets labeled by lines with a transitive relation; morphisms are partial functions respecting labeling and relations.

“the operation of merging the effect of two coinitial patches is defined by pushout”
“objects are finite sets labeled by lines equipped with a transitive relation and morphisms are partial functions”

§ 1. Introduction. Motivates via git/Darcs: a vcs records changes as patches and imports others' patches; 'there is no global notion of time: patches are only partially ordered.' Walks the canonical diamond: file A, patch f:A->B imported by both users, then concurrent g:B->C and h:B->D; the merge E must be the same regardless of import order, computed via residuals h/g and g/h. Formally residuals are 'the arrows of pushout cocones' so the merge square is a pushout. Notes not every coinitial pair has a pushout in L — this is exactly conflict (e.g. two users editing the same line). To represent ALL conflicting files, build the free completion of L under all pushouts (plus initial object = finite colimits), called P, 'adding all conflicting files in the most general way.' P exists abstractly; the paper's main contribution is an explicit description via presheaf theory, paving the way to a provably correct vcs.

“there is no global notion of time: patches are only partially ordered”
“there should be a patch h/g, the residual of h after g, which transforms C into E and has the same effect as h once g has been applied”
“not every pair of coinitial morphisms have a pushout in the category L: this reflects the fact that two patches can be conflicting”
“we investigate the free completion of the category L under all pushouts ... which corresponds to adding all conflicting files”

§ Related work (within Intro). Positions against prior patch formalizations. Darcs formalizes patches via commutation properties; operational transformations axiomatize residual patches (Ressel et al.). The paper's distinguishing claim: in both, 'the fact that residual should form a pushout cocone is never explicitly stated,' only said informally. Cites Jacobson's inverse-semigroup formalization of Darcs, and most importantly Houston's category-with-pushouts for conflicting files (discussed in Section 6) as the closest prior work — but Houston's morphisms are reversed, so it is not the free cocompletion (not the most general model).

“the fact that residual should form a pushout cocone is never explicitly stated, excepting in informal sentences”
“Houston has proposed a category with pushouts, similar to ours, in order to model conflicting files”

§ 2. Categories of files and patches. Defines the base category L. Fix a set L of lines. A file is a finite sequence A:[n]->L where [n]={0,...,n-1} indexes lines. A patch / morphism f:A->B between files A:[m]->L, B:[n]->L is an INJECTIVE INCREASING PARTIAL function f:[m]->[n] such that B(f(i))=A(i) wherever f(i) is defined — i.e. the patch 'keeps track of the unchanged lines.' Deleted lines = those undefined under f; inserted lines = those not in the image. Prop. 2: L is the free monoidal category generated by line insertions eta_a:I->a and deletions eps_a:a->I with eps_a . eta_a = id (deleting an inserted line = nothing). Prop. 4: in the unlabeled (singleton L) case, L is the free category on generators s^n_i (insert at position i) and d^n_i (delete) subject to standard simplicial-like relations. Defines subcategory L+ (same objects, TOTAL injective increasing morphisms = insertion-only patches), the simpler case for Section 4.

“A morphism between two files ... is an injective increasing partial function f:[m]->[n] such that B(f(i))=A(i) whenever f(i) is defined. Such a morphism is called a patch”
“f keeps track of the unchanged lines”
“The category L is the free monoidal category containing ... morphisms eta_a:I->a (insertion) and eps_a:a->I (deletion) such that eps_a . eta_a = id_I”

§ 3. Towards a category of conflicting files. Shows by example that merge = pushout: two non-conflicting insertions have a pushout (the smallest file containing both edits, e.g. ab merging to accbcd), but diagram (3) — both users inserting different lines at the same position — has NO pushout in L: these patches are 'conflicting.' Defines P as the free completion of L under finite colimits (initial object + pushouts), intuitively adding files whose lines are only PARTIALLY ordered, explicitly drawn to mirror git's conflict markers (a, then c-or-d, then b). Def. 5: P is the free finite conservative cocompletion — the unique finitely cocomplete category with colimit-preserving embedding y:L->P satisfying the universal factorization property; 'conservative' = preserves colimits already in L. Theorem 6 (Kelly, folklore): this cocompletion is the full subcategory of presheaves Lop->Set preserving finite limits, restricted to finite colimits of representables. Example 7: FinSet is the cocompletion of the terminal category 1. Notes the surprising consequence that the construction forces 'cycles' into objects of P.

“it should be a pushout of the diagram”
“the two patches f1 and f2 are said to be conflicting”
“P is the free finite conservative cocompletion of L”
“perhaps a bit surprisingly, it turns out that we have to allow cycles in the objects of the category P”

§ 4. A cocompletion of files and insertions of lines. Works out P concretely for the simplified case (L+, insertions only, singleton labels). Uses the subcategory G of objects {1,2}: presheaves on G ARE graphs (P(1)=vertices, P(2)=edges). The inclusion I:G->L is DENSE (Def. 9), so every file n is canonically a colimit of 1's and 2's; the nerve N_I(n) is the graph on [n] with an edge i->j iff iy, and P(n+1) = paths of length n. Lemma 11 — it suffices to check preservation of colimits of diagrams 'generated by a graph.' Lemmas 12-13 — such a generated diagram has a colimit iff the graph is 'tree-shaped': ACYCLIC and CONNECTED (these become a total order). Prop. 14 / Theorem 15: P is equivalent to the full subcategory of graphs where every non-empty path has exactly one edge — equivalently, FINITE SETS WITH A TRANSITIVE RELATION and relation-respecting functions. Prop. 16: pushout of B<-A->C is (B union C)/~ (gluing along shared images) with the TRANSITIVE CLOSURE of the inherited relation. Extends to labels via a slice category L/!L.

“a presheaf P in G-hat is a graph”
“A finite graph G induces a diagram which admits a colimit if and only if it is tree-shaped, i.e. acyclic and connected”
“objects are finite sets equipped with a transitive relation <, and functions respecting relations”
“the pushout ... is B union C / ~ ... equipped with the transitive closure of the relation inherited by <B and <C”

§ 5. Examples. Concrete merges as pushouts. Example 17: starting from ab, one user inserts a' at the start and c in the middle, another inserts d in the middle; the pushout is the merged file with a partial order branching at the c/d position — a non-conflicting concurrent edit. Example 18: defines a SEQUENTIALIZATION morphism seq:G1+G1->G2 (turn two conflicting alternative lines into two consecutive lines, a user's resolution choice); the pushout of seq against seq' (opposite order) produces a CYCLIC graph, showing concretely why cycles appear in P. Example 19: by coproduct of two id:G1->G1 there is a universal MERGE morphism G1+G1->G1 collapsing two independent/conflicting lines into one — the formal act of conflict resolution.

“After merging the two patches, the resulting file is the pushout”
“the sequentialization morphism ... given two possibilities for a line, a user can decide to turn them into two consecutive lines”
“which illustrates how cyclic graphs appear in P”
“two independent lines can be merged by a patch (in order to resolve conflicts)”

§ 6. Handling deletions of lines. Generalizes from L+ (insertions only) to the full L (insertions AND deletions). Theorem 20: the free finite cocompletion P of L is the category whose objects are TRIPLES (A, <, l): A a finite set of lines, < a transitive relation, l:A->L a labeling; morphisms are PARTIAL functions f:A->B respecting labels (l(f(a))=l(a)) and relations (a<a' implies f(a)<f(a')). Pushouts computed as in Prop. 16, generalized to partial functions. Example 21: from abc, one user inserts d after a, another deletes b; the pushout is adc, with the deletion realized as a PARTIAL morphism (b has no image). Compares to Houston's independently-proposed similar category: NOT equivalent because Houston's morphisms are reversed partial functions, so it is not the free cocompletion / most general model — illustrated by FinSet being the cocompletion of 1 while FinSet-op is not, and Houston's category lacking the merge morphism (it has a dual duplicating morphism instead).

“objects are triples (A,<,l) ... and morphisms ... are partial functions f:A->B such that ... l(f(a))=l(a), and a<A a' implies f(a)<B f(a')”
“Notice that the morphism f2 is partial: b has no image”
“it is thus not the most general model (in the sense of being the free finite cocompletion)”

§ 7. Concluding remarks and future works. Claims success: a finitely cocomplete category where merging any modifications is always well-defined, derived from universal constructions, giving correctness from universal categorical properties. Outlines extensions: the methodology 'adapts easily to other base categories' — multiple files (move/delete), structured file types (text, XML trees); repositories modeled as EVENT STRUCTURES labeled by morphisms in P; modeling cherry-picking, branches, patch removal; adding patch inverses. Plans to study algorithmic representation/manipulation of conflicting files (objects of P) toward a 'theoretically sound and complete distributed version control system (no unhandled corner-cases as in most current implementations).'

“It is finitely cocomplete, thus the merging of any modifications of the file is well-defined”
“the structure of repositories (partially ordered sets of patches) is naturally modeled by event structures labeled by morphisms in P”
“no unhandled corner-cases as in most current implementations of vcs”

§ Appendix A. Geometric interpretation of presheaves on L+. Supplementary intuition: recalls how presheaves over L+ (forgetting labels, empty file disallowed) can be read geometrically/simplicially, viewing files and their higher path-structure topologically. Provides the simplicial/geometric reading that underlies the graph-as-presheaf identification used in Section 4, helping the reader grasp why P(n) corresponds to length-n paths and why the construction behaves like gluing simplices.

“Since presheaf categories are sometimes a bit difficult to grasp, we recall here the geometric interpretation”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, current-revision = state; 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest.' — Gives a NAME and a clean mathematical separation for our split: in this paper objects (files) ARE the states/values and morphisms (patches) ARE the identity-preserving transitions between them. A file at a given moment is a finite sequence A:[n]->L (an immutable value, our revision); a patch is the structure-carrying thread between values (our container's history of transitions). Crucially, the patch is an INJECTIVE PARTIAL FUNCTION on line-indices that 'keeps track of the unchanged lines' — declared identity of which lines survive — NOT a diff inferred after the fact. This supports our hard rule: identity (which line is which) is carried explicitly by the morphism at edit time, not reconstructed by diffing two revisions. The warning: their model only has identity where a morphism is recorded; if you only store snapshots (objects) and try to recover the patch, you must INFER it, and inference is lossy (many morphisms can connect the same two files).
  • The code-identity question: is the atomic unit the file or the function? Is identity path/name/AST/hash? — Offers a concrete stance and a generalization hook. Their base unit is the LINE (an element of [n]), not the file: a file is just a finite ordered set of lines, and identity is tracked PER LINE through the partial function. The file is merely the current container; the line carries the surviving-identity. This is a direct analogue to 'the function, not the file, is the atomic unit' — pick the granularity at which a morphism preserves identity. The conclusion explicitly says the method 'adapts easily to other more complicated base categories L' — 'multiple files (which can be moved, deleted, etc.), different file types (containing text, or more structured data such as xml trees).' So an AST-node identity model is the same construction over a richer base category, warning us the choice of base category L (line / function / AST node) is THE design decision and everything else (merge, conflict) is derived.
  • Versioning as its own layer; two distinct times (arrival vs committed/true); branch-and-merge. — Gives the mechanism for the COMMITTED/MINTED layer. The repository is 'partially ordered sets of patches' — 'there is no global notion of time: patches are only partially ordered.' This is exactly our committed-time-as-DAG (not a line). Merge of two concurrent commits is the PUSHOUT of the two coinitial patches; the residual h/g is defined as the pushout arrow, formalizing git's three-way merge as a universal construction. This validates separating arrival-time (Rama log order) from committed causality (the partial order of patches), and tells us merge should be a derived universal operation over the committed layer, not over arrival order.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicts representable, not silently overwritten; synthesis late-bound. — This is the PAPER'S CENTRAL CONTRIBUTION and our strongest mechanism match. Not every pair of patches has a pushout in L — 'two patches can be incompatible, such a pushout does not necessarily exist.' Rather than erroring or auto-resolving, they FREELY COMPLETE the category (P = free finite cocompletion of L) so that EVERY conflict has a representable object: a file whose lines are only PARTIALLY ordered (a transitive relation, possibly with cycles), literally mirroring git's '<<<<<<< / ======= / >>>>>>>' markers as a first-class mathematical object. Conflict is a stored state, not a failure. Synthesis is late-bound: a 'merge' morphism (Example 19) or 'sequentialization' morphism (Example 18) resolves the conflict LATER, by an explicit user-chosen morphism — exactly our 'synthesis is a transformation of preserved plurality, not summary.'
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data) that must COMPOSE. — Offers a composition discipline and a partial caution. Their final object (Theorem 20) is a triple (A, <, l): a finite SET of lines A (nodes), a transitive RELATION < (relations/edges), and a LABELING l:A->L (node-data). Morphisms must SIMULTANEOUSLY respect set membership (partial function), relation (a<a' implies f(a)<f(a')), AND labels (l(f(a))=l(a)). This is a worked example of node-level + relation-level + data-level versioning composing through a SINGLE morphism condition, and merge (pushout) is computed by union-quotient + transitive closure across all three at once. Caution: they have no separate 'relation-TYPE' level (only one relation <) and only one node label set, so our 4th level (typed edges) is a genuine extension — the construction suggests it is another coordinate in the labeling/relation structure, but they do not prove it composes.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing). — Sharpens the declared-vs-inferred distinction with a formal reason to prefer DECLARED. Their patch f:[m]->[n] is the DECLARED operation: it explicitly records which lines are kept, inserted (not in image), or deleted (undefined). The Related Work notes Darcs/operational-transforms axiomatize residuals but 'the fact that residual should form a pushout cocone is never explicitly stated.' The lesson for our trail join: if the agent DECLARES its edit as a morphism (kept/inserted/deleted line-identities), residuals and merges are computable by universal property; if we only have before/after snapshots and must INFER the morphism by diffing, we lose the identity information the pushout needs, and multiple incompatible patches could explain the same snapshot pair. Declared edits make the path+timestamp -> code-at-that-moment resolution exact rather than heuristic.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced, multi-LLM, the 'correctness from universal properties' goal. — Supports the event-sourced + derived-state stance philosophically. The paper's thesis is that the model is 'defined abstractly by the universal property that it should satisfy' and then given a concrete description — so behavior (merge, conflict representation) is DERIVED from a specification, giving 'a vcs whose correctness is deduced from universal categorical properties' with 'no unhandled corner-cases.' This is the formal-method analogue of our Rama-as-truth/projection-as-derived split: define the truth-layer's universal operations (merge=pushout) once, and all projections follow. Warning for substrate: they note the cocompletion FORCES cyclic / partially-ordered objects to even exist ('we have to allow cycles in the objects') — the truth representation for conflicts is necessarily richer (a relation with cycles) than the clean linear file, so our PState schema for a conflicted code-unit cannot be just an ordered list; it must store a transitive relation.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution for our versioning/identity question is the move from 'merge as procedure' to 'merge as universal property, conflict as a first-class object.' Mimram and Di Giusto show that if your edits are DECLARED as identity-preserving morphisms (which lines/units survive, not a diffed snapshot), then merge is exactly the pushout, residuals fall out for free, and — critically — when two edits conflict (no pushout exists), you do NOT fail or auto-resolve: you freely complete the category so the conflicted state is a genuine, storable object, namely a finite labeled set with a TRANSITIVE RELATION (a partial, possibly cyclic order) directly mirroring git's conflict markers. This is the precise mathematical license for our preserved-disagreement + late-bound-synthesis stance, and it tells us our truth-layer schema for a versioned/conflicted code-unit must store a relation (not a linear list), with synthesis modeled as an explicit later resolution morphism. The standing caution: the whole edifice depends on identity being declared at edit time — inferred diffs lose the morphism, and with it the guarantees."

9. Homotopical Patch Theory (Expanded Version)

Carlo Angiuli, Edward Morehouse, Daniel R. Licata, Robert Harper · 2014 · ICFP 2014 (expanded version, author homepage PDF)

In one line. Models version-control patch theory inside homotopy type theory: repository contexts are points, patches are paths, patch laws are paths-between-paths, and a model is a structure-preserving functor -- with identity defined as edit-history modulo commutation and an explicitly incomplete merge.

Source access: full. Read the full text from the author-homepage PDF (carloangiuli.com/papers/hpt-expanded.pdf). WebFetch returned only raw compressed PDF streams, so I downloaded the file (saved by the harness) and extracted text locally with pdftotext (1261 lines), reading all sections including the Appendix A addendum that was cut from the published ICFP version.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the thesis: homotopy type theory (HoTT) extends Martin-Lof type theory so that propositional equality is proof-relevant and corresponds to paths in a space, enabling higher inductive types (HITs) -- datatypes with constructors for points AND paths. The paper applies HITs to patch theory (the formalism behind Darcs), where a patch is a syntactic representation of an edit to a repository. The key architectural separation is between a patch theory (a formal language of patches, presented as a HIT) and its models (maps out of that HIT, which being functors automatically preserve patch structure). Standard homotopy-theory tools (encode/decode, calculating fundamental groups) become practical programming techniques.

“Version control systems such as Darcs are based on the notion of patches—syntactic representations of edits to a repository.”
“A patch theory is presented as a higher inductive type. Models of a patch theory are given by maps out of that type, which, being functors, automatically preserve the structure of patches.”

§ 1. Introduction. Motivates the work: in HoTT, proofs of equality have computational content and can influence how a program runs, so the authors hunt for programming applications of computationally-relevant equality proofs. Patch theory is the chosen example. The central design decision: contexts act as TYPES for patches -- a patch 'delete file f' applies only where f exists (domain context) and yields a context where f is gone (codomain). Patches are closed under identity (no-op), composition (sequencing), and inverse (undo). They satisfy general laws (composition associative, inverses cancel) AND domain-specific laws ('the order of edits to independent lines of a file can be swapped'). The paper represents patches as paths and laws as paths-between-paths, and insists models depend crucially on proof-relevance: a model assigns each equality-proof a computational meaning as a function acting on repositories. Functoriality forces every model to respect identity/inverse/composition and validate the laws. Honest caveats: no implemented examples (would need HIT simulation in Agda/Coq), and no formal operational semantics yet, so 'how programs run' is given as sequences of propositional equalities.

“the contexts act as types for patches”
“a model must respect identity, inverses, and composition (e.g. sending composition of patches to composition of functions) and validate the patch laws”
“several of the examples in the paper are functions into contractible types ... up to paths/propositional equality, such a function can return any element of the type”

§ 2. Basics of Homotopy Type Theory. Reviews the machinery. 2.1 Paths: distinguishes definitional equality (proof-irrelevant, unmarked, includes beta-reduction) from propositional equality / the identity type (proof-relevant, marked: coe p e coerces e along p). Path operations: refl, inverse !p, composition q o p, plus group(oid) laws (refl o p = p, associativity, !p o p = refl) that hold as homotopies. ap f lifts any function to an action on paths (functions are functors); apd is the dependent version producing a PathOver (heterogeneous equality across a type family). 2.2 n-types: a set/0-type has any two parallel paths equal; contractible = a mere proposition with an element. 2.3 Univalence: for sets, paths A = B are given by bijections A <-> B; ua turns a bijection into a path, and coe (ua b) x = applying the bijection -- so paths carry non-trivial computational content, and two different bijections give two differently-behaving paths. 2.4 Higher Inductive Types: generalize inductive types with path constructors; the circle = one point base + one path loop : base = base; circle recursion maps the generators (a point and a loop) into a target type, with computation rule ap f loop = l' (taken as propositional, not definitional, equality in current models).

“In homotopy type theory, elements of the identity type are used to model a notion of paths in a space or morphisms in a groupoid.”
“two different bijections (f,g,p,q) and (f',g',p',q') determine two paths ua(f,...) and ua(f',...) that behave differently when coerced along.”
“Higher-dimensional inductive types ... generalize inductive types by allowing generators not only for points (terms), but also for paths.”

§ 3. General Patch Theory. The framework. A patch theory characterizes a VCS algebraically; a model is an implementation obeying the laws. Each patch has specified domain/codomain contexts (states it applies to / results in). 3.1 Patch Theories as HITs: contexts = points, patches = paths between context-points, patch laws = 2-dimensional paths (paths between paths). The big payoff: identity/inverse/composition AND the groupoid laws (associativity, unit, inverse-cancellation) 'come for free' from the HIT's path structure -- no boilerplate constructors needed. A subtlety flagged early: encoding patches as paths FORCES every patch to have an inverse (p o !p = refl as well as !p o p = refl), which the authors note 'forces us to use some care' and sometimes 'gets in the way.' 3.2 Merging: merge takes a span (f1,f2) (diverging patches from a common state) to a cospan (g1,g2) reconciling them, required to satisfy g1 o f1 = g2 o f2 and to be SYMMETRIC (merge(f1,f2)=(g1,g2) => merge(f2,f1)=(g2,g1)) so independently-computed merges agree -- essential for distributed collaboration. Darcs additionally requires merge to respect inverses, equivalent to 'pseudo-commutation,' Darcs' primitive operation. A total merge ALWAYS exists: merge(f1,f2)=(!f1,!f2) undoes both changes, which is how a merge CONFLICT is signaled (human intervention needed). Critically, merge need NOT respect the groupoid structure -- a recursive 'tiling' merge can give different results depending on whether you compose first.

“merge(f1, f2) = (g1, g2) ==> g1 o f1 = g2 o f2”
“It is always possible to define a total merge function, since for any span we may give merge(f1, f2) = (!f1, !f2), the reconciliation that undoes both changes. This can be used to signal a merge conflict”
“a merge function that is a symmetric reconciliation need not respect the groupoid structure of a higher inductive type”

§ 4. An Elementary Patch Theory. Simplest example: repository = a single integer; patches = add/subtract n. One context num suffices (every patch applies anywhere), patches are paths num = num, generated by add1. This HIT is 'just a renaming of the circle.' Remark 4.1 contrasts with the non-HIT alternative: a plain datatype with id/compose/inv constructors would need a quotient type imposing 5 group laws (assoc, invr, invl, unitr, unitl) as boilerplate -- the HIT provides all this for free. 4.1 Interpreter: I : R -> Type with I num = Int, ap I add1 = ua(successor); interp p = coe-biject (ap I p). Up to propositional equality interp add1 = successor, and interp is functorial (takes composition to bijection-composition) because it is built from ap. Topologically I is the universal cover of the circle and interp computes the WINDING NUMBER -- a normal form counting net turns after detours like loop o !loop cancel. Multiple sound models exist: I' interprets in Bool with add1 = not (add mod 2), satisfying extra equations (add1 o add1 = refl) the Int model does not. The equational theory of R is complete for Int -- 'the fundamental group of the circle is Z.' 4.2 Merge: since addition commutes, merge(f1,f2)=(f2,f1); symmetric holds by definition; reconcile (f2 o f1 = f1 o f2) requires a derived induction principle proving the loop group is abelian, via encode/repeat establishing num=num is in bijection with Int -- 'our first example of a software correctness proof being a corollary of a theorem in homotopy theory.' merge' shows the technique of choosing canonical representatives (integers), computing on them with a function that need not respect group laws (mergeI swaps), then mapping back -- the composite still respects the laws.

“This is, of course, just a renaming of the circle!”
“the integers give canonical representatives ("add x, for x in Z") for equivalence classes of patches ... considered modulo the group laws”
“the correctness of merge follows from the fact that the fundamental group of the circle is Z—our first example of a software correctness proof being a corollary of a theorem in homotopy theory!”

§ 5. A Patch Theory with Laws. Adds domain-specific patch laws. Repository = one document of fixed n lines; one basic patch s1 <-> s2 @ i = 'permute strings s1 and s2 at line i' (chosen because it induces a TOTAL bijection on Vec String n -- self-inverse, fits the symmetric-paths requirement). The HIT R has point doc, path constructor <->@_, and two 2-dimensional path constructors encoding laws: indep (edits at independent lines i != j commute) and noop (s <-> s @ i = refl). This is the paper's first type with both paths and paths-between-paths, so it details the recursion/induction principles: a function out of R must provide doc', swap', and proofs indep'/noop', with computation rules beta1 (definitional) and beta21/beta22 (PathOvers, only propositional) -- the well-typedness of higher computation rules depends on lower ones. 5.2 Interpreter: I doc = Vec String n, ap I (s1<->s2@i) = ua(swapat...); the law images become proof GOALs (GOAL0/GOAL1) that must validate the patch laws for the interpretation, solved via univalence rules (ua reflb = refl; ua b2 o ua b1 = ua(b2 o b1)) plus the corresponding fact swapat-independent. 5.3 Optimizer: optimize : (p:doc=doc) -> Sigma(q:doc=doc). p=q -- returns an equivalent patch (optimizing s<->s@i to refl, saving two string comparisons). Two styles shown: 'program then prove' (write opt1, prove opt1-correct by induction, with the wrinkle that faces/2-paths are NOT automatically identified -- 'there is nothing that actually forces any two such faces to be identified' -- one can have proof-relevant patch laws, or use TRUNCATION to make them trivial); and 'program and prove' simultaneously by mapping into a Sigma(y:R). y=x, which is CONTRACTIBLE (a singleton type S(x)), making the remaining coherence goals trivial. Key insight on singletons: even though optimize into a contractible type is homotopic to the identity function, it still has meaningful COMPUTATIONAL content -- 'computation does not respect extensional equality of functions.'

“indep ... (s <-> t @ i) o (u <-> v @ j) = (u <-> v @ j) o (s <-> t @ i)”
“there is nothing that actually forces any two such faces to be identified ... we could potentially consider proof-relevant patch laws—proof-relevant patch laws”
“even though the optimize function ... is equal (i.e., homotopic) to the function that simply returns p itself ... we expect ... it will in fact compute appropriately”

§ 6. A Patch Theory With Richer Contexts (the core contribution for versioning). The substantial example: patches ADD s@l (insert string s as line l) and RM l (remove line l) that are NOT total and NOT all composable -- removal from an empty file must be inadmissible. This needs MULTIPLE contexts. A naive indexing doc n by line-count fails because doc n = doc n+1 as a path, yet Vec n String and Vec n+1 String are not bijective, so univalence can't equate them. The decisive move (6.1): index contexts by PATCH HISTORIES -- 'contexts need only determine the contents of the repository, not literally contain them.' History m n is a quotient HIT of composable patches from m-line to n-line files, quotiented by commutation laws (e.g. ADD-ADD-< / ADD-ADD->= commute two additions with shifted line numbers; ADD-RM, RM-ADD, RM-RM omitted but analogous). Complete histories (History 0 n) applicable to the empty file UNIQUELY determine the file by 'replay' and serve as the patch contexts. R has point doc : History 0 n -> R and path constructors addP/rmP; because differing sequences are never parallel, the patch laws become PathOvers over the History quotient equations (addP-addP-< etc.). 6.2 Interpreter: I (doc h) = S(replay h) -- each context interpreted as the SINGLETON type of the exact file its history replays to; replay steps a complete history to its Vec String n; every function between singletons is automatically a bijection (single-biject). 6.3 Logs: an alternate model I'(doc h)=S(h) interprets contexts as singleton HISTORIES, giving interpH that updates the history; this shows histories can be RECOVERED from patches and ERASED at runtime ('it would be sensible to discard the histories at runtime'). 6.4 Merge: restricted to divergent paths with shared domain doc [] (so patches are forward sequences of ADD/RM with NO inverses -- 'a merge involving inverse paths is not sensible in this patch theory'). merge converts inputs to complete histories via interpH, calls mergeH (defined via Extension: h2 extends h1 if h1 is a prefix), and converts back via toPath/extToPath. mergeH itself is reduced to a merge on plain text files (e.g. via replay + existing diff algorithms), which 'would necessarily respect the patch laws because replay does.' This is the section the conclusion admits is INCOMPLETE.

“This is achieved by indexing contexts by patch histories, which are sequences of composable patches applicable to files of a given length. ... a history applicable to the empty file uniquely determines the resulting file's contents.”
“We can avoid these undesirable inverses by restricting merge to divergent paths with shared domain doc []. ... Users of a version control system will always encounter compositions of generating patches starting from the empty file”
“it would be sensible to discard the histories at runtime, through some erasure mechanism”

§ 7. Related Work. Positions against prior category-theoretic VCS analyses. Jacobson formalizes Darcs patches as inverse semigroups (partial bijections). Mimram & Di Giusto and Houston analyze merging as a PUSHOUT, giving a canonical merge for every pair including a primitive representation of merge conflicts; Houston also notes a duality with exceptions. The authors' distinguishing contribution: presenting patch theory in a categorical setting that is ALSO a programming formalism, so it leads directly to an implementation. They note these prior works allow non-invertible maps, whereas in HoTT all identity types are symmetric -- forcing the authors to either use naturally-total bijective patches (Sections 4-5) or restrict contexts via histories (Section 6) to fit the symmetric setting. Dagit uses advanced Haskell types to prove VCS invariants; the Camp (Commute And Merge Patches) project aims to prove Darcs patch theory correct in Coq. Swierstra & Loh use separation logic for patch behavior, which the authors suggest could describe histories with small-footprint specs.

“Mimram and Di Giusto analyze merging as a pushout, which provides a canonical merge for every pair of patches, including a primitive representation of merge conflicts.”
“In homotopy type theory all identity types are symmetric, and to fit patch theories into this symmetric setting, we either considered a language where all patches were naturally total bijections ... or used types to restrict patches to repositories where they are bijections”

§ 8. Conclusion. Summarizes: patch theories are HITs with generators for points (contexts), 1-paths (patches), 2-paths (patch laws); groupoid laws come free; a model is a functor into a univalent universe of sets and bijections (sets = concrete repositories, bijections = patch actions), intrinsically functorial so it must respect the structure. Homotopy-theoretic 'encode/decode' identity-type characterizations become an implementation technique for merge: pass to a concrete representation, define merge there, pass back. Honest limitations stated plainly: the Section 6 merge is INCOMPLETE -- 'we have not proved the required properties of it,' because proving merge laws via mergeH needs a more precise inductive characterization of R's identity type (an inductive type equivalent to it, as Z is for the circle), left to future work. The computational interpretation of HoTT and the meaning of type erasure need further development. The most pointed reflection: requiring paths to be SYMMETRIC (have inverses) is 'both conceptually questionable and practically problematic'; they worked around it with histories (forward-only patches) but could NOT formulate pseudo-commutation, which seems to need a complete characterization of the space of spans of patches. Proposed future direction: a type theory with NON-symmetric paths grounded in directed homotopy theory, to express partial patches without refining contexts.

“the development of the merge operation in Section 6 is incomplete, because we have not proved the required properties of it.”
“demanding inverses, rather than just retractions representing "undo" operations, is both conceptually questionable and practically problematic”
“a more precise characterization is needed, namely an inductive type which is equivalent to the identity type, as Z is to the identity type of the circle”

§ Appendix A. Addendum (interval, open interval, binary trees, quotiented trees, merge laws). Cut from the published version; clarifies the role of histories. A.1 Interval: the HIT I (zero, one, seg:zero=one) is CONTRACTIBLE (center zero), so zero=one has no more elements than seg -- inverses add nothing because any path zero=one must have one more seg than !seg, and groupoid laws collapse seg o !seg o seg to seg. This is WHY restricting to forward paths eliminates inverses. A.2 Open Interval I (doc:Nat->I, add1:doc n=doc n+1) is the half-line [0,inf); proving it contractible (center doc 0) shows there is a UNIQUE path doc 0 = doc n, so the codomain context determines exactly which patch sequence occurred -- 'patches applicable to doc 0 are characterized precisely by the index n of their codomain.' A.3 Binary Trees: two edits per context (add true/add false), contexts indexed by Bool List histories -- 'the patch theory looks like a tree, where the nodes are histories and the paths label the edges.' Contractibility again makes merge laws hold: reconcile follows because the type of merge forces a square and contractibility makes all squares commute; symmetric reduces to symmetry of mergeH. A.4 Quotiented Binary Trees: adds a commutation law (any two patches commute), so Bool Lists must be quotiented by permutation into MULTISETS (MS, lists quotiented by Exchange of adjacent elements). The ex constructor equates add true o add false with add false o add true as PathOvers (since their endpoints differ as lists but are equal as multisets). Naively R seems to have non-trivial loops doc[]=doc[], but the patch law ex 'trivializes these loops by equating the two sides' -- proving toPath respects the MS quotient 'verifies that ex fills in the loops.' A.5 Merge Laws for Richer Contexts: sketches that with a machine-checked proof (linked Agda: PatchWithHistories.agda / PatchWithHistories2.agda) R from Section 6 is contractible, so paths are uniquely determined by endpoints, the type of paths from doc[] is equivalent to Sigma(n).History 0 n (the path 'provides no additional information'), and merge laws on histories suffice for merge laws on paths.

“the codomain context doc n determines exactly which sequence of patches has occurred.”
“Now the patch theory looks like a tree, where the nodes are histories and the paths label the edges.”
“The reconcile law follows from the contractibility of R: the type of merge specifies that p1, p2, q1, and q2 form a square, and by contractibility, all squares in R commute.”

How it maps to our parts

  • The code-identity question (identity = file vs path vs name vs AST vs git hash; declared vs inferred change) — GIVES IT A NAME + MECHANISM. The paper's central move is that a repository context is NOT the file's contents but a patch history (a sequence of composable edits modulo commutation laws). Identity of a state = the canonical history that produced it, not the bytes. This is the 'identity = history modulo commutation' thesis: two edit sequences that commute denote the SAME context. Mapped to code identity, this argues identity is best modeled as a DECLARED operation-history (what the agent says it did), not an INFERRED snapshot/AST-diff. The history 'uniquely determines the resulting file' but the file does not determine the history -- exactly our declared-over-inferred bias.
  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time log vs committed/minted versions; partial vs full persistence) — SUPPORTS + WARNS. The paper separates a patch theory (formal language of edits, a higher inductive type) from a model/interpreter (how edits act on concrete repositories). This is precisely our split between the version layer and the materialized state it projects onto. Patches-as-paths give a free groupoid (identity/compose/inverse + associativity/unit laws) -- the algebra a commit/branch layer needs. WARNING: the paper found that requiring every patch to have an inverse (symmetric paths) was 'conceptually questionable and practically problematic'; an append-only arrival log (their History type contains 'only compositions of generators, and not their inverses') is the cleaner substrate. Validates Rama's append-only log as primary and committed versions as a derived layer.
  • Incomplete merge / preserved disagreement / plurality (synthesis is late-bound) — SHOWS A FAILURE MODE + a name for our 'merge conflict as first-class'. The paper proves merge can ALWAYS be made total by 'undoing both changes' (merge(f1,f2)=(!f1,!f2)) -- a valid reconciliation that signals a conflict needing human intervention. Crucially it admits its own merge development is INCOMPLETE: 'we have not proved the required properties of it' because proving merge laws needs a complete inductive characterization of the span of patches, which they couldn't construct. Direct warning: a merge/synthesis operator that looks total can hide unproven correctness; conflicts (preserved disagreement) are the honest default, and forcing premature synthesis is where the theory breaks.
  • Object-container kernel: container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — SUPPORTS strongly. Contexts (points) are the stable identity threads; patches (paths between points) are immutable transitions; the current file is a projection (replay of the history), never stored as truth-at-ingest. The paper's I' interpreter even shows you can 'recover a history from a patch' and erase the concrete file at runtime -- the history (declared structure) is truth, the materialized vector-of-strings is a derived view. This is the container/revision split with the hard rule built in: inferred file contents are computed, never the source of identity.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with their own history, partly independent of members — OFFERS A MECHANISM (functorial semantics / quotient HITs). A patch theory is a free structure; a model is a functor out of it that 'automatically preserves the structure of patches.' A grouping/local-world can be modeled the same way: define its own higher-inductive theory of admissible transitions, and bind members via a model (functor) that may differ (I vs I' -- one tracks file contents, another tracks logs/metadata). The quotient HIT (History modulo commutation, MS = multisets modulo Exchange) shows how to give a grouping a version history that is independent of member ordering -- pinned vs floating binding maps onto whether you quotient by commutation.
  • Four-level graph versioning that must COMPOSE (graph/node/relation-type/node-data) — OFFERS A COMPOSITION DISCIPLINE. The paper's whole apparatus is layered paths-between-paths-between-paths: points (0-dim), patches (1-dim paths), patch-laws (2-dim paths/faces), and truncation/3-dim coherence. Each level's operations are forced to respect the level below by functoriality ('these come for free, from functoriality'). This is a worked example of multi-level versioning where higher levels COMPOSE coherently with lower ones by construction -- a model for making our 4 graph-versioning levels compose rather than drift, at the cost of coherence obligations (their GOAL0/GOAL1 face proofs) at every level.
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve a reasoning trail's path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment) — OFFERS THE replay MECHANISM. Their 'replay : History -> Vec String' steps a complete history from the empty file to reconstruct the exact file at that point in time; 'histories also reify sequences of patches in a way which facilitates ... moving forward or backward in time.' This is exactly our path+timestamp -> code-at-that-moment resolution: an agent trail IS a declared history; replaying it to the relevant prefix yields the code state the trail was reasoning about, with no diffing/inference required.
  • Substrate choices (Rama event-sourced log) and the arrival-vs-commit two-times distinction — SUPPORTS the event-sourced bet. The paper's most practical conclusion is that a VCS can be implemented by 'storing sequences of patches as paths, and repositories as vectors of strings,' updating by running interp on each new patch -- an event-sourced design where the patch log is primary and state is a fold/projection. It also separates the forward History (arrival/append order) from reconstructed paths involving inverses (which it deliberately excludes), echoing our two distinct times: append-order is the safe substrate, committed/branchable structure is derived on top.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution to our versioning/identity question is the thesis that a state's IDENTITY is its edit-history modulo commutation, not its contents -- and that the clean substrate for this is a forward-only, append-style history (no inverses), with the concrete file/state computed by REPLAY as a derived projection that can even be erased. This directly validates our object-container kernel (history = truth, materialized state = projection, inferred contents never the source of identity) and our two-times model (the forward arrival-log is the safe primary; branchable structure is derived on top). The paper's most actionable warning is about merge: a total merge always exists by 'undo both' (the honest signal for an unresolvable conflict / preserved disagreement), but the authors could NOT prove their richer merge correct because that requires a complete characterization of the space of spans -- so we should treat merge/synthesis as late-bound and conflict-as-first-class rather than assume a clean total reconciliation.

10. Understanding Darcs/Patch Theory

Wikibooks contributors (community manual; draws on David Roundy's Darcs patch theory and FOSDEM 2006 conventions) · n.d. (living wiki page; content reflects mid-2000s Darcs patch theory) · Wikibooks (open community textbook)

In one line. An intuition-first, illustrated introduction to Darcs's patch algebra: patches are context-dependent transformations with inverses, commutation reorders them, and merge is defined purely from inverse+commutation — with conflicts explicitly deferred.

Source access: full. Read the full live Wikibooks page (en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Understanding_Darcs/Patch_theory) via two WebFetch passes — one for full section text, one to verify heading order and the (absent) conflict content. The page is open-access, no gating. Note: the deeper conflictor/conflict-resolution material is on a separate linked page ('More patch theory') NOT covered here; this breakdown covers only the Patch_theory page as requested.

The paper, section by section

§ 1. Math and Computer Science Nerds Only. A framing warning that the chapter is for 'math and computer science nerds (and the occasional physicist),' not casual users. It establishes that day-to-day Darcs use does not require this material, but that understanding how Darcs 'really works' demands engaging with the patch formalism. Sets expectation that the content is intuition-driven but mathematically grounded.

“Casual users be warned, the stuff you're about to read is not for the faint of heart!”

§ 2. What is the Theory of Patches?. Defines the patch formalism as the mathematical foundation for how Darcs exchanges patches between repositories, implemented concretely as Haskell data structures and functions over patch sequences. States the chapter's aim: convey the intuitions behind the math so readers can follow patch theory and the later 'conflictors' research. Frames a repository's behavior as operations over sequences of patches rather than over file snapshots.

“The patch formalism provides mathematical foundations for understanding how Darcs exchanges patches between repositories.”

§ 3. Why All This Math?. Argues that distributed VCS perform merging constantly, so correctness is critical, and that mathematical formalization serves two purposes: abstracting away implementation detail and guaranteeing the techniques stay 'fundamentally sound' as complexity grows. Crucially separates clean merging from conflict cleverness — the chapter begins with successful non-conflicting merges and defers conflicts. Also fixes notation: composition is left-to-right (AB = B after A), per FOSDEM 2006 convention rather than the Darcs appendix.

“getting merging right does not necessarily consist of having clever behaviour with respect to conflicts”
“We will begin by focusing on successful, non-conflicting merges and move on to the darcs approach to handling conflicts”

§ 4. Context, Patches and Changes. Introduces the central concept: a CONTEXT names a repository state. Using a shopping-list file (apples/bananas/cookies/rice) labeled context o, it shows that when a user adds 'beer' at line 3, that act is a CHANGE, and Darcs records it as a PATCH capturing BOTH content ('beer') AND location (line 3). The notation °A^a denotes a patch A that takes the repository FROM context o TO context a. This is the load-bearing idea: a patch is inseparable from the contexts it connects — it is a typed arrow between states, not a free-floating diff.

“Darcs produces a patch recording both the content ('beer') and location (line 3)”
“The notation °A^a denotes moving from context o to context a via patch A.”

§ 5. Sequential Patches. Defines SEQUENTIAL patches as patches that apply successively, with shorthand collapsing the context annotations (°A^a B^b → AB; … → ABC). Establishes that a Darcs repository simply IS a sequence of patches. Motivates the rest of the theory: complex operations (like undo) require 'rearranging patches and putting them in different orders' while preserving repository coherence — which the following sections give precise definitions for.

“Darcs repositories are patch sequences”
“rearranging patches and putting them in different orders”

§ 6. Inverses. Defines the INVERSE: the inverse of patch P is P⁻¹ such that composition PP⁻¹ makes no net change to the context, and (P⁻¹)⁻¹ = P. Illustrated with the beer example: A adds beer (°A^a), A⁻¹ removes it and returns to context o (°A^a A⁻¹^o). States a hard requirement of the formalism: EVERY patch must have an inverse. This invertibility is what makes undo and (later) merge mechanically definable.

“The Inverse of patch P is P⁻¹, which is the patch for which the composition PP⁻¹ makes no changes to the context and for which the inverse of the inverse is the original patch.”
“Every patch must have an inverse”

§ 7. Commutation. Defines COMMUTATION as reordering two adjacent patches while preserving correctness: XY ↔ Y₁X₁, where X₁ and Y₁ perform 'the same change' as X and Y but adjusted for the swapped context. Motivated by selective undo: if A (beer) then B (pasta), you cannot just delete A because B sits on top of it; you commute AB into B₁A₁ to surface A. Three sub-points: (a) 'Why Not Keep Our Old Patches?' — naive reordering breaks because line numbers/contexts shift (pasta at line 5 vs beer at line 3 must be recomputed), so commutation must produce contextually corrected variants; (b) 'The Complex Undo Revisited' — to undo a buried patch, commute it to the surface then apply its inverse (commute AB→B₁A₁, then apply A₁⁻¹); (c) 'Commutation and Patches' — each patch type defines its own commutation rules (hunk patches adjust line offsets), and some pairs CANNOT commute (e.g., a file-add and a content-add into that same file).

“The commutation of patches X and Y is represented by XY ↔ Y₁X₁, where X₁ and Y₁ are intended to perform the same change as X and Y”
“Some patches cannot commute (e.g., file addition with content addition to that file).”

§ 8. Merging. Builds MERGE entirely out of inverse + commutation. PARALLEL patches A∨B diverge from a common context o (Arjan's A reaches a, Ganesh's B reaches b). To let Ganesh pull Arjan's change, the algorithm: (1) start at °B^b; (2) prepend B⁻¹ to return to o; (3) append A; (4) commute B⁻¹A into A₁B₁⁻¹ so A₁ now lives in Ganesh's context; (5) discard B₁⁻¹, leaving °B^b A₁^c. MERGE IS SYMMETRIC: the merge of A and B yields A₁,B₁ with A∨B ⇒ BA₁ ↔ AB₁, so if Ganesh can pull Arjan, Arjan can equally pull Ganesh. A supporting 'commutation-with-inverse' property (BA₁ ↔ AB₁ iff B₁A₁⁻¹ ↔ A⁻¹B, when both succeed) underwrites the algorithm's correctness. The whole construction handles only clean, non-conflicting merges — conflict is out of scope here.

“The result of a merge of two patches A and B is one of two patches A₁ and B₁, which satisfy the relationship A ∨ B ⇒ BA₁ ↔ AB₁”
“If you can commute the inverse of a patch A⁻¹ with some other patch B, then you can also commute the patch itself against B₁.”

§ 9. Definitions and Properties. A summary reference table consolidating the algebra: inverse (AA⁻¹ has no effect), inverse-of-inverse ((A⁻¹)⁻¹ = A), inverse-of-composition ((AB)⁻¹ = B⁻¹A⁻¹), commutation (AB ↔ B₁A₁), merge (A∨B ⇒ BA₁ ↔ AB₁), and commutation-with-inverse. There is no reflective conclusion; the page ends by pointing to the next page, 'More patch theory,' where conflicts/conflictors are taken up.

“(AB)⁻¹ = B⁻¹ A⁻¹”
“A ∨ B ⇒ BA₁ ↔ AB₁”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (when-it-arrived vs when-it-was-committed/true; partial vs full persistence) — Darcs patch theory gives a name and an algebra to the distinction we care about: a patch is not a snapshot but a context-dependent transformation (°A^a means 'A taken FROM context o TO context a'). Identity of a change is the change-plus-its-context, not a fixed line-number diff. This warns us that our 'committed/minted version' layer must carry context, not just a value: the same logical edit re-expressed against a different base (A vs A1) is a different patch object even though it 'performs the same change.' Commutation is exactly the machinery that lets you re-base a true/committed change onto a different arrival history without losing identity.
  • The code-identity question (is identity the file/path/AST/hash, atomic unit file vs function) — Darcs answers a sibling question and the answer is instructive: the atomic unit of identity is the PATCH (the change), not the file state. A repository IS a sequence of patches, and any file-state is a derived projection. This supports treating our object-container's REVISIONS as the values and the EDIT OPERATIONS (in trails) as first-class identity-bearing things — but it also warns: Darcs identity is purely operational/syntactic (hunks at line offsets), it has NO semantic/AST notion of identity, so commutation can fail or mangle when changes touch the same region. If we want function-level identity that survives moves, we cannot get it for free from a patch algebra.
  • The trail-to-code join (DECLARED change vs INFERRED change) — This is the strongest mapping. A Darcs patch is a DECLARED change carrying both content and location ('records both the content (beer) and location (line 3)') — it is the agent saying what it did, with its context, not a reconstructed diff. That is exactly our DECLARED-change pole. The paper shows the payoff: because the change is declared WITH its context, you can mechanically commute/re-base it to resolve a trail's edit onto a different code state, rather than re-diffing. It validates storing trail edits as context-tagged operations so the path+timestamp resolution is an algebraic re-contextualization, not a guess.
  • Object-container kernel hard rule: 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — Darcs is built on the same instinct, restated: it never trusts a positional diff as ground truth across divergence. The 'Why Not Keep Our Old Patches?' subsection is a concrete failure mode — applying a patch made in context o directly onto context b inserts at the WRONG line because the inferred line number is stale. The discipline is: only the declared change-in-its-context is trusted; any cross-context application MUST go through commutation (an explicit, lawful transformation), never naive reapplication. This is a precise mechanism for our 'don't accept inferred structure at ingest' rule.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; conflicts late-bound synthesis — Darcs's design philosophy directly supports late-bound synthesis: 'getting merging right does not necessarily consist of having clever behaviour with respect to conflicts' — it separates clean parallel composition (A∨B ⇒ BA1 ↔ AB1, fully symmetric) from conflict handling, which it explicitly DEFERS to a later treatment (conflictors). The lesson for us: a merge of two divergent local worlds should first be modeled as symmetric re-contextualization where it commutes cleanly, and conflict should be a separate, named, deferred construct — not silently resolved at merge time. WARNING: this page does NOT actually deliver the conflictor model, so we cannot cite a concrete preserved-disagreement mechanism from it, only the architectural stance.
  • Groupings as versioned objects; Rama event-sourced substrate — A Darcs repo = an ordered, append-style sequence of context-linked patches, which is structurally our event log: the patch sequence is primary and any state is a fold over it. This supports our Rama 'event log is primary, rendered state is projection' stance. But the merge algorithm reveals a cost relevant to groupings: to undo a buried patch you must (1) commute it to the surface, (2) apply its inverse — i.e., reordering is a real algebraic operation with offset bookkeeping, not free. A versioned grouping whose members are patch-like changes inherits this: pinned vs floating member binding is essentially whether you re-commute (re-base) a member onto the grouping's new context or freeze its original context.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the reframing of a version as a CONTEXT-DEPENDENT, INVERTIBLE TRANSFORMATION rather than a snapshot or a context-free diff: a patch is a typed arrow °A^a between named states, and reordering/re-basing it onto a different history is a lawful algebraic operation (commutation), not a re-diff or a guess. For Softland this directly underwrites the DECLARED-change pole of the trail-to-code join — store each agent edit as content+location tagged with its context, and resolve it onto code-at-a-moment by commutation rather than inference, satisfying our 'inferred structure is never truth at ingest' rule. The cautionary footnote is that Darcs's identity is purely syntactic (line/hunk offsets) and the page DEFERS conflicts entirely — so it gives us the algebra of clean re-contextualization and a model for late-bound conflict, but not a semantic/AST identity nor an actual preserved-disagreement mechanism.

What we can gather — Angle 2

This angle is the formal floor, and it returns three results that change how confidently we can build.

First, our design is a known framework, not an invention. Conradi & Westfechtel's 1998 survey is our architecture under older names: container = versioned item / OID, revision = VID, our two times = extensional vs intensional versioning — and their emphatic ruling is that these "are by no means mutually exclusive, but can (and SHOULD) be combined." They name our grouping hazard precisely — version proliferation, where naive version-first grouping forces a new version of every ancestor — and its fix is exactly our instinct: give configurations their own version history, distinct from their members. The cheapest high-value move available is to adopt this vocabulary wholesale; it makes every future contract and Codex gate cheaper to argue.

Second, the two times are formally distinct objects, and one of them is genuinely hard. DSST (1989) proves partial persistence (read any version, extend only the tip — our Rama arrival log) and full persistence (modify/branch from any version — our minted committed layer) are different mechanisms: full persistence needs a version tree, not a line, plus a total-order version list to navigate it. Both cost O(1) amortized via structural sharing — but confluent merge (synthesizing branches back together) was explicitly left open in 1989, and it is still the hard part. This is the formal warning against promising clean automatic merge.

Third, the patch theories give us our deepest principles. Across Pijul, the categorical theory (Mimram-Di Giusto), the homotopical theory (Angiuli et al.), and Darcs, two ideas recur with proof behind them: (a) identity and change are declared, never inferred from content — a line's identity is the change that introduced it, and if you only store snapshots you must infer the morphism and inference is lossy; and (b) a conflict is a first-class value, not an error — formally the free cocompletion, concretely "a finite set of lines with a transitive relation, not a linear order." That last result is a literal schema instruction for us: a conflicted/plural code-unit in a PState must store a relation, not a list. And the homotopical result — identity = history modulo commutation of independent edits — is the formal statement of two things we already believe: the event log is primary, and semantic time is a DAG (independent edits commute, so they are unordered).

What we gather: build the two times explicitly and combined; encode "conflict is a stored relation" into the plurality layer; and treat synthesis/merge as the late-bound, still-open frontier rather than a feature we can promise total.

Angle 3 — Versioned Databases & Content-Addressed Stores

11. Mergeable Persistent Data Structures

Benjamin Farinier, Thomas Gazagnaire, Anil Madhavapeddy · 2015 · JFLA 2015 (26es Journées Francophones des Langages Applicatifs); system = Irmin / MirageOS

In one line. Irmin gives any OCaml data structure git-style persistence — an immutable content-addressed block store plus a tiny mutable tag store — and extends queues and ropes with a recursive 3-way merge whose only constant cost is a ~5x overhead.

Source access: full. Read the original full PDF (266KB, extracted via pdftotext) from the author's site anil.recoil.org/papers/2015-jfla-irmin.pdf — abstract, all 5 sections, type signatures (Figs 1,2,4,5,8), the queue worked example (push=6/pop=2), the rope merge_branch/merge_node functions (Figs 9,10), complexity tables (Figs 3,7), and benchmarks (Figs 12,14). Also cross-read Adrian Colyer's The Morning Paper summary at the requested blog URL for framing. The blog post is the requested URL; the primary technical source quoted here is the JFLA PDF itself.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States Irmin is an OCaml library for purely functional data structures that 'can be persisted on disk and be merged and synchronized efficiently.' The paper's focus is the merge aspect, demonstrated on two structures — queues and ropes — that 'extend the corresponding purely functional data structures with a 3-way merge operation.' Promises early theoretical and practical complexity results, and notes Irmin is open-source within MirageOS.

“Irmin is an OCaml library to design purely functional data structures that can be persisted on disk and be merged and synchronized efficiently.”
“queues and (ii) ropes that extend the corresponding purely functional data structures with a 3-way merge operation.”

§ 1. Introduction. Frames the thesis: take DVCS workflows (used to manage source code, track provenance, relate bugs to the change that caused them) and apply them to application DATA. The motivating question is 'What if you could version-control a (mutable) persistent data structure, inspect its history, clone a remote state and revert it?' Rather than a conventional database, the functionality is delivered as a LIBRARY linked into application logic. Irmin reuses git's concepts (clone/push/pull/branch/rebase) and maintains a bidirectional mapping with the git on-disk format so standard tools (tig, gitk) work. Positions the work as a generalization of the Concurrent Revisions framework to arbitrary history-graph shapes and an extension of CRDTs that uses 3-way merge instead of encoding operation sequences into state. Crucially observes that while any data structure can be made persistent, the authors 'do not believe such a technique exists for mergeable data structures' — hence Irmin provides an immutable key-value store over which users build their own mergeable structures. Lists the two conflict situations (concurrent local edits; divergent remote edits) and three resolution techniques: CRDTs, a custom merge operator, or explicit first-class conflict handling.

“What if you could version-control a (mutable) persistent data structure, inspect its history, clone a remote state and revert it to a previous state?”
“Our work is a generalization of the Concurrent Revisions framework to handle arbitrary shape of history graphs.”
“we use 3-way merge instead of encoding the sequences of operations in the state itself.”
“While it has been shown that is possible to make any data structure persistent, we do not believe such a technique exists for mergeable data structures.”

§ 2. Irmin Architecture. Describes a mutable high-level interface built on two backend stores. (1) The BLOCK STORE: a low-level, append-only key/value store where keys are deterministically computed from values (e.g. SHA). Two consequences are spelled out: modifying a value creates a new key/value pair (the store is immutable), and two stores sharing the same values get the same keys (the store is consistent; structure depends only on data). It holds serialized application content AND structured data (prefix-tree nodes, history metadata); being append-only it has no remove, grows forever, and relies on GC/compression. (2) The TAG STORE: 'the only mutable part of the system,' a key/value store whose keys are user-created names and whose values are block-store keys — 'a set of named pointers,' expected to be local and very small, and central to synchronization and GC. The high-level interface lifts immutable block operations into a mutable prefix-tree (Fig 1: read/update/remove over a path = list of strings, with mergeable contents at nodes). Figure 2 gives the merge contract: result is ['Ok of t | 'Conflict of string] and merge: old:t -> t -> t -> result. The section closes with the GOOD-MERGE definition that serves as the paper's correctness spec.

“the block store: this is a low-level key/value append-only store, where values are a sequence of bytes and keys are deterministically computed from the values (for instance using SHA algorithms).”
“The tag store: this is the only mutable part of the system... a set of named pointers to keys in the block store... expected to be local to each replica and very small.”
“Given two data structures to be merged, the result of every operation applied on these structures since their common ancestor has to be present in the result of the merge operation.”
“type result = [ 'Ok of t | 'Conflict of string ] val merge: old:t -> t -> t -> result”

§ 3. Mergeable Queues (incl. 3.1 Implementation, 3.2 Merging). Queue chosen for ubiquity and for the simplicity of stating merge semantics: every element popped in either queue must be ABSENT from the result, and all elements pushed in either queue must be PRESENT in the same order. Implemented as a functional queue = two linked lists (a push list and a pop list); when the pop list empties, the push list is flushed into it ('normalization'), giving amortized O(1) push/pop (Fig 3: push = 2 writes; pop ≈ 2 reads/1 write; merge = O(n) reads, 1 write). The Irmin store holds three element types (Fig 5): Index (fields push, pop, top, bottom — the push/pop COUNTS are what merge uses), Node (next/previous/elt/branch pointers, where branch holds a concatenated queue's index), and Elt (the values). Merge takes q1, q2, and common ancestor old, and proceeds in steps: using only the push/pop counts (no element access), remove from q1 the old-elements that q2 popped, and likewise drop old-elements still shared, then concatenate q2 onto q1 by adding a node whose branch field is q2's index. Worked example: old={push 6,pop 2}, q1={push 8,pop 2}, q2={push 7,pop 4} normalizes to q1'={push 8,pop 4}, q2'={push 7,pop 6}, and the final concatenation is O(1). Worst-case merge reads are linear in |old| but reducible to logarithmic via binary random-access lists.

“A functional queue is composed of two simple linked lists... When the pop list is empty, the push list is flushed into the pop one. This operation is called normalization.”
“The two first fields, push and pop, are the number of pushes and pops applied to the queue since its creation. They are useful for the merge operation.”
“elements of old which have been removed from q2 are removed from q1. This is done without accessing these elements by using the push and pop values.”
“using binary random-access lists instead of classical chained lists will reduce the overall number of reads to the logarithm of the size of the old.”

§ 4. Mergeable Ropes (incl. 4.1 Implementation, 4.2 Merging). A rope is a self-balancing binary tree whose leaves hold short strings; each node indexes by the total length in its left subtree, giving O(log n) set/get/split/concat/insert/delete vs O(1)/O(n) for raw strings (Fig 7). The design is generic: 'give me a container with a set of operations, and we will return you a rope on this container... achieving a better complexity' — so the user must supply a merge on the leaf container because a general merge over arbitrary containers is impossible. A key application: in MirageOS the rope is instantiated as a FILE SYSTEM with pages as atomic leaves, yielding 'a Git-like persistent file system that can be distributed over several machines.' The store again holds Index/Node/Leaf. Merge (4.2) uses the block KEYS of the two trees and their common ancestor to locate the smallest changed subtrees, via two recursive functions: merge_branch (Fig 9) does key comparison across three cases (both branches equal → return either; one side unchanged vs old → take the other; modifications in different branches → take the modified one; else None), and merge_node (Fig 10) pattern-matches on (left_option, right_option) into a GOOD case (deduce immediately), a RECURSIVE case (recurse into the None subtree), and a BAD case (no automatic deduction → linearise both subtrees into containers and call the user merge). When changes are in 'differentiable places' the result is deduced conflict-free. Rebalancing is the efficiency enemy — rotations deeply reshuffle structure and defeat key comparison — so rotation is applied as little as possible and only to the smallest unbalanced subtree, making merge cost proportional to the VOLUME of modification.

“the rope can be instantiated as a file system, using pages as rope atomic elements. In combination with Irmin, we obtain a Git-like persistent file system that can be distributed over several machines.”
“their keys in the Irmin store are used to determine the smallest subtrees where modifications occurred.”
“if these subtrees are separated... we can therefore include all of them being sure there is no conflicts. If it is not the case, these subtrees are linearised and flushed into containers on which the user-defined merge operation is applied.”
“In order to minimize the scope of rebalancing operations, the rotation function is applied as less as possible, and only on the smallest unbalanced subtree.”

§ 5. Analysis (5.1 Automatic checking, 5.2 Benchmarking). Correctness (5.1) uses an oracle: a sequence of classical operations is run on the implementation and on a witness from another library, requiring exact match at every step. This cannot validate MERGE because no witness implementation exists, so they instead pick results with an easily-checkable invariant preserved by merge (e.g. a queue of increasing numbers, a rope of nondecreasing characters), decompose them, and re-merge to recover the original — confirming merge correctness at each use. Tests passed but cannot confirm asymptotic complexity, motivating benchmarks (5.2). Benchmarks confirm the theoretical complexities are reached and reveal a 'stair' pattern (each new tree level) and spikes (balancing vs leaf-length interactions). Backends compared: Memory (RAM), GitMem and GitDsk (git format in memory / on disk), Obj (an Obj.magic heap backend using memory addresses as block addresses, for raw comparison only), and Core (the Core library's functional queue, used as the optimal baseline).
