Headline result: 'there is a factor five between Core and the implementation of our queue on the Obj backend' — deemed 'an acceptable price to pay for maintaining a mergeable data structure.'

“it does not exist at our knowledge another data structure to be used as a witness.”
“we chose a result with a easily verifiable property which is preserved after each merge operation... a queue containing an increasing sequence of numbers, or a rope composed by a nondecreasing sequence of characters.”
“there is a factor five between Core and the implementation of our queue on the Obj backend. It is an acceptable price to pay for maintaining a mergeable data structure.”

§ Backends & Git format (within Analysis / 5.2). The bidirectional git-format mapping is concrete, not aspirational: GitMem and GitDsk backends store Irmin's content-addressed blocks in actual git object format so that application commits show up in the git repository and manual git changes flow back through Irmin. The append-only, content-addressed block model maps cleanly onto git's object store (blobs/trees keyed by hash), and the small mutable tag store maps onto git refs/branches. The Obj backend (Fig 13) deliberately bypasses hashing — 'maps Irmin blocks to the OCaml heap and use the memory address as block address (instead of its hash)' via Obj.magic — purely to measure raw overhead against Core, and is explicitly not advised for real use.

“any changes committed by an application are reflected in the associated Git repository, and any manual change in the Git repository is reflected back in the application via Irmin.”
“the OCaml heap, which maps Irmin blocks to the OCaml heap and use the memory address as block address (instead of its hash)... We do not advise to use it in other contexts.”

§ Conclusion / positioning. Irmin demonstrates that DVCS workflow — branchable history, clone/merge/revert, provenance — can be delivered as a library over purely functional, hash-consed, content-addressed data, at a modest (~5x) constant cost. The two showcase structures (queue, rope) prove the 3-way merge approach generalizes beyond text, with the rope-as-filesystem result directly enabling MirageOS's git-like distributed file system. The authors flag future work: formalizing the merge algorithms in a proof assistant such as Coq to strengthen the informal correctness argument, and reducing merge read-cost via binary random-access lists.

“We demonstrate this increased flexibility power comes with a very reasonable cost, thanks to the use of purely-functional data structures and (implicit) hash-consing.”
“We are planning to translate these algorithms into a proof assistant such as Coq in order to assert them more strongly.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (stable container = identity thread; revisions = immutable values; 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest') — Irmin's two-store split is a direct architectural template. The content-addressed block store is the immutable-revisions layer: every value is keyed by its own hash, so a modification creates a new key/value pair and 'the resulting data-store is immutable.' The mutable tag store ('named pointers to keys in the block store') is the container/current-revision layer: a stable user-chosen name whose value (a block-hash) moves over time. This validates our split of stable identity thread (tag) over immutable values (blocks). It also supports the hard rule: blocks are truth-by-content (hash = what it is), and only the tiny mutable tag layer asserts 'this is current' — structure is content-addressed, identity assignment is explicit and minimal.
  • The code-identity question (file vs path vs name/FQN vs AST vs git hash; atomic unit file or function) — Irmin answers the analogous question for data: the atomic unit is the smallest content-addressed block, and identity-over-time is NOT the hash (which changes on every edit) but the externally-minted tag name. Mapped to code: the git-hash IS the revision identity (immutable value), while the container name (path/FQN) is the identity thread. The rope-as-filesystem instantiation ('pages as rope atomic elements') shows the same content can be sub-divided into finer content-addressed atoms (pages/functions) under one identity (file) — supporting 'atomic unit = function inside a file-container' rather than forcing file-granularity.
  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit committed/minted git-style versions (full persistence) — two distinct times — Irmin is the canonical full-persistence system: 'clone, push, pull, branch, rebase' over an arbitrary history DAG ('a generalization of the Concurrent Revisions framework to handle arbitrary shape of history graphs'). This is exactly our committed/minted-version layer — you can branch from any committed node. It warns, though, that full persistence of MERGEABLE structures is not free: 'while it has been shown that is possible to make any data structure persistent, we do not believe such a technique exists for mergeable data structures.' So our two-time design (Rama arrival-time log + explicit git-style commits) cannot assume merge comes for free at the committed layer; merge must be defined per data-structure.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (a curated local world has its own version history, partly independent of members; pinned/floating bindings) — Irmin's prefix-tree (the high-level mutable interface 'generated over the block store, the tag store') is exactly a grouping that has its own commit identity while its leaf contents have theirs. Because every node — including interior structure/'history meta-data' — is itself a content-addressed block, a grouping's version is a hash over its members' hashes: change a member, the grouping gets a new version automatically (floating binding). To PIN a member you reference its specific block-hash; to FLOAT you reference through the tag/tree. This gives a concrete mechanism for member-binding modes.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data must compose) — Irmin's hash-consed Merkle tree is the composition mechanism we need: 'if two data-stores share the same values, they will have the same keys; the overall structure only depends on the stored data.' A change at node-data level bubbles a new hash up through node, relation, and graph levels deterministically, so each level is independently versioned yet provably composed (a graph-hash uniquely determines all sub-level hashes). This is direct evidence that content-addressing makes multi-level versioning compose for free — the same property git relies on.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; synthesis is late-bound — This is the paper's core contribution and its main caution. Irmin makes divergence first-class (two replicas, arbitrary history DAG) and offers THREE conflict strategies: CRDTs, a custom 3-way merge operator, or 'explicit handling of conflicts as first-class citizen in the API.' The recursive merge only auto-resolves when changes are in 'differentiable places'; otherwise it returns ''Conflict of string' or flushes subtrees to a user-defined merge — it never silently overwrites. This validates our late-bound synthesis stance and supplies the exact signature: merge: old:t -> t -> t -> result. The merge correctness law ('the result of every operation applied since their common ancestor has to be present in the result') is a usable specification for our synthesis layer.
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; declared vs inferred change) — Irmin's 3-way merge depends critically on a COMMON ANCESTOR ('old') being known, not inferred: the queue merge uses old's push/pop counts to compute the diff without reading elements, and the rope merge compares block-keys against old.left/old.right to locate changed subtrees. Mapped to trails: a stored commit DAG lets us resolve a trail's path+timestamp to an exact ancestor revision (declared change against a known base), rather than reconstructing change by diffing two states blind. The Acolyer summary flags the gap — the paper 'assumes knowledge of a common ancestor state but the discovery method remains unaddressed' — warning us that finding the LCA is our responsibility, not a freebie.
  • Substrate choices (Rama event-sourced; cost of the versioning machinery) — Irmin gives an honest cost number for git-format versioned data: 'there is a factor five between Core and the implementation of our queue on the Obj backend... an acceptable price to pay for maintaining a mergeable data structure,' with backends ranging from in-RAM to GitMem/GitDsk. This calibrates expectations for layering explicit git-style commit/merge on top of Rama: a ~5x overhead on the optimal in-memory case is the order of magnitude to budget. The append-only block store ('there is no remove function; the store is expected to grow forever') matches Rama's append-only log substrate exactly — same growth assumption, same reliance on GC/compaction.

Takeaway. Irmin gives our versioning/identity layer its strongest existence proof and its exact shape: separate immutable, content-addressed REVISIONS (the hash-keyed append-only block store) from a tiny mutable IDENTITY layer of named pointers (the tag store), and you get git-style full persistence — branchable history, provenance, clone/merge/revert — over arbitrary data, with multi-level versioning composing for free because every level is a hash over its children. The critical, citable warning for us is that merge is NOT free at the committed layer: persistence is automatic but mergeability is per-structure, requires a KNOWN common ancestor (LCA discovery is the caller's problem), and resolves conflicts only by an explicit user merge (old:t->t->t->result) that must never silently overwrite — exactly our preserved-disagreement/late-bound-synthesis stance — all at a calibrated ~5x overhead.

12. Prolly Trees (Probabilistic B-trees) — Dolt Storage Engine Architecture

DoltHub (Dolt documentation team) · 2023 · DoltHub official documentation (docs.dolthub.com / www.dolthub.com)

In one line. A B-tree variant with content-defined, rolling-hash chunk boundaries and content-addressed storage that gives B-tree read/write performance plus O(change) diffs and automatic structural sharing across versions — the storage engine under the version-controlled SQL database Dolt.

Source access: full. Read the full official Dolt documentation page. Original URL https://docs.dolthub.com/architecture/storage-engine/prolly-tree 301-redirects to https://www.dolthub.com/docs/architecture/storage-engine/prolly-tree, which I fetched in full (three passes: full text, then two targeted verbatim-quote passes). This is a docs page, not a peer-reviewed paper; section names below are its real structural headings. The data structure was originally invented by the Noms team; this page is Dolt's description of its own implementation.

The paper, section by section

§ Introduction & Motivation. Defines a Prolly Tree as a 'Probabilistic B-tree' that is 'particularly effective as the basis of the storage engine for version controlled databases.' It frames three simultaneous goals that conventional structures cannot all meet: B-tree-class random read/write performance, fast diff computation, and structural sharing across versions. A motivation table claims prolly trees match B-trees on random ops at logk(n) but compute diffs in time proportional to the difference size d rather than total data n. The defining version-control advantage is content addressing: portions of the tree are shared between versions because intermediary storage is content-addressed.

“"Prolly Trees can also structurally share portions of the tree between versions due to the content-addressed nature of their intermediary storage."”
“Prolly trees are "particularly effective as the basis of the storage engine for version controlled databases."”

§ How Prolly Trees Work — B-tree Review. Reviews ordinary B-trees as sorted key->value maps with pairs in leaf nodes, then names their two disqualifying limitations for versioning. First, finding differences between two B-trees requires scanning the entire structures (O(n)). Second, and central to the whole design, B-tree shape is order-dependent: the insert/update/delete sequence changes internal structure, so two trees with identical contents but different write histories can differ structurally. This non-determinism is exactly what defeats cheap diffing and structural sharing, motivating the history-independent construction that follows.

“"writes to B-trees are not history independent, the order of the writes internally changes the structure of the tree."”

§ How Prolly Trees Work — Construction Algorithm. Gives the six-step build: (1) sort the key-value map; (2) determine chunk boundaries with a rolling/strong hash over keys, using a seed, target chunk size, and the key value — a boundary is declared when the hash falls below a target threshold; (3) hash each resulting chunk and store it content-addressed; (4) if a single content address remains, done; (5) otherwise build the next layer up as a map of each chunk's highest key to its content address; (6) recurse until one root remains. Because boundaries are content-defined (a function of the data itself, not of write order), the resulting tree is deterministic for a given dataset — the property the B-tree review identified as missing.

“"You store the contents in a content addressed block store."”
“"Prolly trees are stored in a content addressed block store where the content address forms the lookup key for the block."”

§ Modification Operations — Updates, Insertions, Deletions. Describes copy-on-write modification. A fixed-size value update rewrites the value in a copied chunk, recomputes that chunk's content address, then walks upward recomputing each parent's address up to a new root; only the path from the changed leaf to the root is rewritten, everything else is shared. Variable-length changes may force local re-chunking. Insertions behave by position: at the beginning they perturb the left edge, at the end the right edge, in the middle a 'diagonal spline' of affected chunks; deletions mirror insertions. Crucially the page quantifies boundary stability: with average 4KB chunks the chance that a single-byte change shifts a chunk boundary is about 1/4096 (~0.02%), so almost all edits are local.

“"In Dolt's Prolly Tree implementation, chunks are set to be on average 4 kilobytes. This means that you can imagine a probability of 1/4096 or 0.02% of triggering a chunk boundary shift when changing a single byte."”

§ Key Properties — History Independence. States the foundational invariant: the tree is a pure function of its contents, independent of operation order. Any sequence of inserts/updates/deletes that yields the same key-value set yields the same tree, the same chunk boundaries, and the same root hash. This determinism is what makes the two downstream features (fast diff, structural sharing) possible — equal contents are guaranteed to produce equal, shareable subtrees, and two databases that converged to the same state are byte-for-byte identical in storage.

“"No matter which order you insert, update, or delete values, the Prolly tree is the same."”

§ Key Properties — Fast Diff Algorithm. Explains the diff as a parallel descent over two trees comparing content addresses level by level: if two nodes share a hash, their entire subtrees are identical and are skipped wholesale; the algorithm recurses only into branches whose hashes differ, bottoming out at the actually-changed key-value pairs. Cost is therefore set by the number of differing chunks, not tree size. This is the structural payoff of history independence plus content addressing, and it is the property that makes Dolt's commit-to-commit diffs fast at any database scale.

“"If the hash of the root chunk is the same the entire subtree is the same."”
“"This algorithm scales with the size of the differences, not the size of the tree."”

§ Key Properties — Structural Sharing. Because each block's lookup key IS its content address, any two versions that contain a byte-identical chunk physically store it only once; unchanged subtrees across commits/branches are automatically deduplicated. A new version after an edit costs only the rewritten path from changed leaf to new root (logk(n) chunks), with the rest of the tree referenced by shared hashes. This is the storage basis for keeping many committed versions and branches cheaply — the cost of a version is proportional to its change, not the database size.

“"any blocks that share the same content address are only stored in the block store once."”

§ Implementation Details — Chunk Size Control. Documents Dolt's improvement over the original Noms design, which produced geometrically/exponentially distributed chunk sizes (many tiny chunks, a few huge ones). Dolt instead targets a normal distribution around 4KB by adjusting the boundary probability with a cumulative-distribution-function formula (CDF(end) - CDF(start)) / (1 - CDF(start)), which raises the boundary probability as a chunk grows. The result is more uniform chunk sizes, improving cache behavior and predictability without sacrificing content-defined boundaries.

“"we use the formula (CDF(end) - CDF(start)) / (1 - CDF(start)) to calculate the target probability."”

§ Implementation Details — Key-Only Hashing. A pivotal design decision: where Noms hashed both keys and values to place boundaries, Dolt hashes ONLY keys. Two consequences. First, value edits cannot move chunk boundaries, so an update is guaranteed local (rewrite one chunk, no re-chunking) — maximizing structural sharing and diff locality. Second, it sidesteps a low-entropy failure mode where ordered/sequential data hashed by value would chunk poorly. This separates the identity/ordering axis (keys) from the content axis (values), a clean engineering lesson for any identity-keyed versioned store.

“"any updates to values is guaranteed not to shift the chunk boundary"”

§ Implementation Details — Type-Less Block Storage. Notes that Dolt's block store, unlike Noms, does not encode type information inline within blocks; type metadata is kept out of band. This 'type-less' on-disk layout reduces per-block overhead and improves read and write performance. It is a comparatively minor optimization relative to the chunking and key-hashing decisions, but reinforces the theme that Dolt re-engineered Noms's prolly-tree storage for performance and uniformity.

“type information is stored "out of band"; the "type-less layout improves Dolt read and write performance."”

§ Performance Analysis. Tabulates prolly trees against B-trees: random read logk(n) for both; random write logk(n) for B-trees versus (1 + k/w)*logk(n) for prolly trees — a small penalty from the occasional chunk split; diff calculation n for B-trees versus d for prolly trees. The narrative concedes a slight write cost from the low-probability boundary shift but emphasizes the asymptotic diff win. This is the quantitative case that prolly trees buy version-control superpowers (O(change) diff, sharing) at near-B-tree operational cost.

“"Prolly Trees can produce differences in time proportional to the size of the differences."”
“writes incur "a slight performance penalty on writes due to the small probability of a chunk split."”

§ Real-World Usage in Dolt. Grounds the structure in the shipping product: every piece of Dolt's data is a prolly tree — a table is a map of primary key to data columns; a secondary index is a map of index values to primary key; schemas and keyless tables also use prolly maps, all over one shared content-addressed block store. Benchmarks put Dolt roughly comparable to MySQL, with gaps attributed to implementation language (Go vs C), SQL-analyzer maturity, and data-transformation overhead — NOT to the prolly tree itself. The closing claim restates the payoff: Dolt computes diffs proportional to change size and structurally shares data across versions.

“"a map of primary key to data columns is stored in a Prolly Tree. Similarly, secondary indexes are maps of index values to the primary key."”
“Dolt "can compute diffs in time proportional to the size of the differences" and "structurally shares data across versions."”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit committed/minted versions (full persistence) — Prolly trees are the mechanism Dolt uses to make EVERY committed version cheap to keep: structural sharing means a new commit stores only changed chunks (~the d in the diff), not a full copy. This is a concrete proof-of-feasibility for our 'full persistence' / git-style minted-version layer: branching and retaining many committed snapshots is affordable when unchanged subtrees are shared by content address. It does NOT address arrival-time history (that is Rama's log); it addresses the OTHER time axis — when-it-was-committed — and shows the storage cost of keeping all those committed states is proportional to total change, not number-of-versions x size.
  • Object-container kernel: container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, current-revision = state — The prolly tree gives a name to the substrate beneath our revision model: revisions are immutable content-addressed values; the container's history is a chain of root hashes over a shared block store. The hard rule 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' is REINFORCED here — prolly tree identity is the content hash of the actual bytes/keys, fully determined by data (history-independent), never by inferred or order-dependent structure. A revision's identity = its root content address; equality of two revisions is an O(1) hash compare, exactly the 'if the root hash is the same the subtree is the same' property.
  • The trail-to-code join (first consumer): resolve a trail's path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; declared vs inferred change — Prolly-tree diff (proportional to d, descend only into differing branches) is the mechanism for cheaply reconstructing INFERRED change between two committed code states. But the doc's strength is structural sharing + content addressing, which lets us pin a trail to an exact root hash (code-at-that-moment) and resolve a path against an immutable snapshot. Declared change (agent says what it edited) maps to a known key-range; the prolly diff between adjacent roots is the inferred-change cross-check. Caveat: prolly diff is key-level, not semantic — it tells you WHICH keys changed, not whether the change is meaningful, so it informs but does not replace declared change.
  • The code-identity question: file vs path vs FQN vs AST vs git hash; atomic unit = file or function — Dolt's design choice — hash KEYS ONLY, not values, so 'any updates to values is guaranteed not to shift the chunk boundary' — is a direct lesson for our identity question. It separates the IDENTITY/ordering dimension (keys = stable address: path or FQN) from the CONTENT dimension (value = the code bytes/AST). If we make our atomic unit's key its stable identity (e.g. FQN or container-id) and its value the code text, edits to a function body change one chunk without re-chunking neighbors. This warns against keying by content hash (git-hash-as-identity), which would re-shuffle boundaries on every edit and destroy structural sharing.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data), must compose — Prolly trees compose hierarchically by construction (leaf chunks -> parent map of highest-key+chunk-address -> recurse to single root), and Dolt stores tables, secondary indexes, schemas all as nested prolly maps sharing one block store. This is a working example of MULTI-LEVEL versioning that composes through a single content-addressed root: a change at node-data level bubbles new hashes up to the graph root, while untouched levels keep their old hashes and are shared. It supports our requirement that the four levels compose into one version identity, and gives the mechanism (one root hash over nested maps) for how lower-level changes roll up without recopying unchanged levels.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with their own history, partly independent of members — A grouping = a prolly map whose keys are member-ids and whose root hash is the grouping's version. Structural sharing means the grouping's identity changes ONLY when membership/binding changes, independent of churn inside members that are referenced by stable key. This supports pinned bindings (key -> specific member root hash) vs floating bindings (key -> member container, latest). Warning: prolly trees are history-independent and have no native notion of effectivity/condition-scoped binding — that temporal/conditional logic must live in OUR layer above the tree, not be expected from it.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; late-bound synthesis — Prolly trees give cheap, persistent BRANCHES (any root hash is a fork point, unchanged subtrees shared across branches), which is the storage substrate for holding conflicting versions side-by-side without overwrite. But the doc offers NO merge/synthesis semantics — content-addressed diff tells you keys differ, not how to reconcile. So this maps as: prolly trees make plurality cheap to STORE and cheap to DIFF, but late-bound synthesis (our merge policy) is entirely our responsibility above it.
  • Substrate choices: Rama (event-sourced log) as our chosen substrate — This is a fork-comparison warning, not an endorsement. Dolt's two strengths (diff proportional to change, structural sharing across versions) are delivered by content-addressed prolly trees, a DIFFERENT substrate than Rama's append-only log + PStates. Rama gives arrival-time time-travel for free but does not natively give git-style branchable committed versions with O(d) diff and structural sharing. The takeaway for us: if we want Dolt-like committed-version semantics on top of Rama, we must BUILD a content-addressed, history-independent revision store (the prolly-tree role) as a layer; Rama alone provides the log, not the full-persistence committed-version structure.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the proof that you can keep an unbounded number of committed, branchable versions cheaply by combining content-addressed storage with a history-independent (order-independent) tree: storage cost and diff cost both scale with the SIZE OF CHANGE (d), not the number or size of versions (n). For our versioning/identity question this gives a concrete mechanism for the 'full-persistence / git-style minted version' layer and a sharp design rule — key by stable identity, hash keys not values, so edits touch one chunk and unchanged subtrees are shared by content address. It also marks the boundary: prolly trees make plurality, branching, and diff cheap to STORE and DETECT, but carry no semantic merge, no effectivity-scoped binding, and no arrival-time history — those remain our layer's job on top of Rama.

13. Version Controlled Databases: Defining a Category

Oscar Batori (DoltHub) · 2020 · DoltHub Engineering Blog

In one line. Coins 'version controlled database' as a product category — an append-only DB with native branch/merge/diff over both data and schema — and positions Dolt against Noms, Irmin, and TerminusDB by query interface.

Source access: full. Read the full blog post (a ~6-minute engineering post, not a formal paper) directly via WebFetch at the canonical DoltHub URL; no paywall. Confirmed it is a short category-definition piece with no deep technical sections on the storage structure.

The paper, section by section

§ Opening: the two-concepts distinction. The post's central move is to separate two phrases that are routinely conflated. 'Database version control' means storing your schema (and schema migrations) in a Git-like system external to the DB — 'the database itself knows nothing of its version history.' A 'version controlled database' is the opposite: a class of append-only databases that natively offer branch, diff, and merge across BOTH schema and data. The author's stated goal is reclaiming the term from schema-migration tooling for products that are actually databases with version control built in.

“"'Database version control' and 'version controlled database' are not the same thing."”
“"a 'version controlled database' represents a class of append-only databases that offer traditional version control features like branch, diff, and merge across both schema and data"”
“"reclaim the term 'version control database' for products that actually are databases with version control features"”

§ Version Controlled DB vs Database Version Control (the definition). This is where the category is formally defined. A version controlled database 'stores a full history of its own state, both data and schema.' The defining feature set is: the ability to store a history of state, PLUS some form of branching, merging, and diffing across that stored history. Crucially the history lives inside the database, not in an external VCS that is blind to the DB's contents. This is the criteria list the rest of the post measures candidate systems against.

“"Is a database that stores a full history of its own state, both data and schema."”
“"the ability to store a history of its state, along with some version of branching, merging, and diffing across the stored history"”
“"The database itself knows nothing of its version history."”

§ Why Now? (the enabling structure). Argues the category is newly feasible due to three converging factors: the rising value of data in production systems (invoking Karpathy's 'Software 2.0' — behavior determined by data, not code), a long-run decline in storage costs, and 'the advent of Merkle tree.' This is the ONLY place the enabling data structure is named: Merkle trees make it 'tractable to expose a full data history to a query interface.' The post gives no further technical depth — no prolly tree, no content-addressing internals, no structural-sharing explanation — so the Merkle reference is asserted as the unlock, not elaborated.

“"computer behavior is increasingly determined by data rather than code"”
“"the advent of Merkle tree make it tractable to expose a full data history to a query interface, and therefore to build true version controlled databases"”

§ Why Adopt — Branching/Merging. Frames branch and merge as the core justification: they are 'fundamental primitives for collaboration,' letting many users work concurrently against the same base dataset and then 'resolve their differences in a principled and robust way.' The contrast is with conventional databases that assume a single concept of state, forcing any versioning/reconciliation logic to be hand-built in the application layer — which the author treats as a fragility this category removes.

“"Branching and merging are fundamental primitives for collaboration."”
“"work concurrently against the same base dataset, and then resolve their differences in a principled and robust way"”
“"any tools that permit versioning and reconciliation [must be] built into the application layer"”

§ Why Adopt — Diffing & distribution. Elevates diff from a review convenience to an infrastructural primitive: robust diffing is 'what enables version control systems to effectively implement operations like clone and pull.' This makes a version controlled DB an 'excellent data distribution format' — history and semantics are native, so consumers do not reverse-engineer the producer's conventions (e.g. which string means NULL). Summarized benefits: collaboration as first-class (hundreds of concurrent editors), full reproducibility by tagging runs with the input/result commit hash, and distribution reduced to clone/pull instead of parse-and-load.

“"not just useful for review, it is what enables version control systems to effectively implement operations like clone and pull"”
“"It shouldn't be necessary to figure out what strings a data vendor uses to signify NULL, it should be native to the distribution format."”
“"associating runs with commit hashes"”

§ Examples — TerminusDB, Noms, Irmin, Dolt. Surveys four members of the category along the implicit dimension of QUERY INTERFACE and host language. TerminusDB: a graph database with its own query language, Git-inspired branch/merge/diff. Noms: a Git-like distributed database storing structured objects defined by Go structs, versioning both the object DEFINITIONS and the VALUES they take — and the substrate Dolt is built on top of. Irmin: a storage-layer-agnostic, OCaml database 'quite similar in spirit to Noms' but objects are described/updated in OCaml. Dolt: the author's entry, a SQL database implementing the MySQL standard with Git primitives. The differentiator across all four is what language/interface the user must adopt.

“"Dolt is built on top of Noms."”
“"provides versioning and synchronization primitives for both the object definitions themselves, and values those objects take on"”
“"Irmin is a storage layer agnostic database inspired by Git, and written in O'Caml."”
“"TerminusDB is a graph database... inspired by Git, and supports branching, merging and diffing in a Git-like interface."”

§ Why Dolt. Argues Dolt's strategic bet is the query interface: by implementing the MySQL standard it lets users keep SQL and the existing connector ecosystem ('nine programming languages with tested MySQL connectors') rather than adopting a bespoke language as Noms/Irmin/TerminusDB require. The honest tradeoff is stated plainly: the version-control storage (a commit graph) is a poor natural fit for a SQL engine, so building a MySQL superset over it is 'a huge technical commitment.' This is the post's only acknowledgment that marrying version-control storage to a familiar query model is hard.

“"We believe that SQL is the language of choice for the vast majority of our potential users."”
“"The underlying commit graph storage is not a natural fit for a SQL query engine, and implementing a super set of MySQL on top of that storage layer is a huge technical commitment."”

§ Conclusion. Restates the category claim: the industry has historically treated version control as something done TO databases, not a feature OF them, and Dolt's category inverts that. It stresses the two ideas are not competitors — version controlled databases do not replace the practice of version-controlling your schema; they serve users who specifically need collaboration, versioning, reproducibility, and distribution at the data layer, without writing application code.

“"The software industry has long considered version control something that is done to databases, not a feature of databases."”
“"not competitive with the practice of version controlling your database schema"”
“"elevate the quality of their data infrastructure without the need to write and maintain application code"”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time vs committed/minted time; full vs partial persistence) — Names and validates our core distinction. Dolt's whole thesis is that a 'version controlled database' STORES its own history with explicit branch/merge/diff as first-class DB features — exactly our 'explicit, git-style COMMITTED/MINTED versions you can branch from' (full persistence) layered ABOVE whatever the substrate does. The post warns of the cost: 'The underlying commit graph storage is not a natural fit for a SQL query engine... a huge technical commitment.' That is the warning for our two-times design: bolting committed-version semantics onto an arrival-time log is real engineering, not free.
  • Object-container kernel (stable container = identity thread; immutable revisions inside; current-revision = state) — Supports it via the Noms model the post cites: Noms 'provides versioning and synchronization primitives for both the object definitions themselves, AND values those objects take on' — i.e. a stable object identity (container) distinct from the immutable values (revisions) it takes over time. Merkle/content-addressing is the mechanism that lets revisions be immutable and shared while the container persists as an identity thread.
  • Substrate choices: Rama (event-sourced) as the versioning enabler — Offers a mechanism + a name. The post pins the enabling condition: 'the advent of Merkle tree make it tractable to expose a full data history to a query interface.' Append-only history + content-addressed structural sharing is what makes time-travel queries affordable. Rama gives us the append-only log; this paper says the missing ingredient for cheap branch/diff is content-addressed (Merkle) structure over that history — a hint for how we'd build the committed-version layer.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change vs INFERRED change — Reinforces the 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth' rule. The post elevates DIFF to a first-class, native, robust operation rather than a reconstruction: 'Robust diffing... is what enables version control systems to effectively implement operations like clone and pull,' and history should be 'native to the distribution format,' not parsed/guessed. This favors DECLARED change captured at commit time over INFERRED change reconstructed by post-hoc diffing — the same instinct behind our hard rule.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, composing) — Partial support + a gap. The post names a graph-shaped version-controlled DB (TerminusDB, 'a graph database... branching, merging and diffing in a Git-like interface') and stresses versioning spans 'both schema and data.' Schema-vs-data ~ our relation-TYPE-level vs node-DATA-level distinction. But the post offers NO mechanism for composing those levels — it's a category-definition piece, not an implementation, so it warns by omission that multi-level composition is unsolved here.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (a curated local world with its own history) — Supports via the collaboration framing: 'Branching and merging are fundamental primitives for collaboration' letting users 'work concurrently against the same base dataset, and then resolve their differences.' A branch is exactly a grouping/local-world with its own independent version history that later merges back — our pinned/floating/merge semantics for grouped members.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (late-bound synthesis) — Directly supports. Dolt's model treats divergence as normal: concurrent branches hold conflicting states co-present and 'resolve their differences in a principled and robust way' at merge time. That is preserved disagreement with late-bound synthesis, implemented at the data layer rather than 'built into the application layer' — the post's explicit critique of single-state databases.

Takeaway. The post's durable contribution is the clean category definition we should adopt verbatim for our versioning layer: a true version-controlled store keeps a FULL history of its own state (data AND schema) inside the substrate and exposes branch/merge/diff as native operations — never bolted on at the application layer and never reconstructed by guessing. For Softland that validates making committed/minted versions a first-class kernel feature over Rama's arrival-time log, and the Noms lineage it cites (versioning object DEFINITIONS separately from the VALUES they take) is precisely our object-container vs immutable-revision split. Its one honest warning: the version-control storage (a content-addressed commit graph) is 'not a natural fit' for a familiar query interface — wiring our committed-version graph to ergonomic queries is the real cost, not the history-keeping itself.

14. So you want Database Version Control?

Tim Sehn (DoltHub) · 2021 · DoltHub Engineering Blog

In one line. A vendor argument that 'true' database version control means full git-style branch/merge/diff of BOTH schema and data, achievable via prolly-tree storage, with Dolt as the only SQL database delivering cell-wise diff and merge on rows.

Source access: full. Full original blog post read directly at dolthub.com (not gated). This is a company engineering blog, not a peer-reviewed paper; it has named structural headings rather than formal academic sections, and it argues a market thesis rather than proving results. Two WebFetch passes: one full-text, one targeted on prolly trees / cell-wise diff / schema-vs-data / SQL system-tables / AS OF.

The paper, section by section

§ Introduction — the search-result framing. Opens with a rhetorical complaint: googling 'database version control' returns schema-migration tools (Liquibase, Redgate), which the author calls 'both too specific and antiquated.' The framing move is to claim the term has been wrongly narrowed to schema migration, and that new storage technology now makes true versioning of schema AND data possible. This is a positioning argument, not a technical contribution — it sets up the schema-vs-data axis the rest of the post organizes around.

“In 2021, this query response to 'database version control' is both too specific and antiquated.”

§ What do you mean by database version control?. Decomposes the term into a set of dimensions before evaluating solutions: database type (relational vs other), versioning target (schema, data, or both), desired capabilities (rollback, time travel, lineage, diff, branch/merge), and conflict detection/resolution. This taxonomy is the post's most useful analytic move — it makes explicit that 'versioning' is a bundle of distinct capabilities, and that most tools deliver only a subset. It maps cleanly onto our own need to separate arrival-time rollback from committed branch/merge.

§ Schema — migrations as the term of art. Defines the conventional approach: 'The term of art for version controlling database schema is database migrations.' You baseline a schema in version control and track changes as patches. The author notes DoltHub itself runs Postgres with schema+patches checked into Git as 'common practice,' and names Liquibase, Redgate, and Planetscale as the representative tools. The implicit critique: migrations version the schema deltas but say nothing about the DATA, leaving half the problem unsolved.

“The term of art for version controlling database schema is database migrations.”

§ Data — four approaches and the prolly-tree breakthrough. The technical heart. Surveys four ways people version data and rejects the first three. (1) Slowly-changing-dimensions / soft-deletes via active-inactive or temporal columns — dismissed because 'the application or an operator can still make a mistake and do really bad things.' (2) Backups + transaction logs — rollback exists but on hours-to-days timescales, too coarse. (3) SQL AS OF temporal tables (SQL:2011, Oracle/SQL Server/MariaDB) — give 'instant rollbacks' but 'You don't get diffs and merges.' (4) The breakthrough: Noms' prolly tree, 'a content addressed binary tree,' which 'allows for fast diff and merge on binary trees, the core data structure in most databases, without compromising too much read or write performance.' TerminusDB (graph/doc) and Dolt (relational) are built on this. Crystallizes the thesis: 'database version control means full Git-style versioning, branch, merge, and diff, of schema and data.'

“You don't get diffs and merges but you do get instant rollbacks.”
“Noms laid the groundwork with a core storage format called a prolly tree, a content addressed binary tree.”
“This data structure allows for fast diff and merge on binary trees ... without compromising too much read or write performance.”
“We think database version control means full Git-style versioning, branch, merge, and diff, of schema and data.”

§ The options — database migration tools. Evaluates three schema-only tools against the data axis. Liquibase (2012): config-language descriptors (XML/SQL/JSON/YAML) that migrate cross-platform (Postgres to MySQL), supports rollback and preview; mature but schema-only. Redgate Deploy (2014): SQL-Server-only, Visual Studio integration, has limited 'static table data' versioning but explicitly 'intended only where the table has fewer than a thousand rows' — a concrete admission that row-level data versioning was an afterthought bolted onto a schema tool. Planetscale (2016, from Vitess creators): adds schema branch/merge plus a modern deployment environment, but is MySQL-only and database-as-a-service. The common verdict: all version schema, none truly version data.

“intended only where the table has fewer than a thousand rows”

§ The options — version controlled databases (Dolt). Presents the two prolly-tree databases. TerminusDB (2019, graph, WOQL query language, git-like branch/push/pull) gets a favorable nod. Dolt (2019, 'It's Git for Data') is the recommended answer. Key mechanisms: Dolt 'implements the Git command line and associated operations on table rows instead of files' — i.e. it re-bases git's unit from the file/blob to the row. Workflow: edit data+schema in a working set via SQL, snapshot via commits. The SQL surface is split by operation kind: 'dolt implements Git read operations (ie. diff, log) as system tables and write operations (ie. commit, merge) as functions.' Granularity: 'Dolt produces cell-wise diffs and merges, making data debugging between versions tractable.' Runs offline (data-as-source-code) or online (like Postgres/MySQL). Closing claim: 'the only SQL database on the market that has branches and merges.'

“Dolt implements the Git command line and associated operations on table rows instead of files.”
“In SQL, dolt implements Git read operations (ie. diff, log) as system tables and write operations (ie. commit, merge) as functions.”
“Dolt produces cell-wise diffs and merges, making data debugging between versions tractable.”
“That makes Dolt the only SQL database on the market that has branches and merges.”

§ Conclusion. Restates the thesis as a recommendation: for a SQL database that needs genuine version control over both schema and data, the author claims exactly one product qualifies. This is an unhedged vendor conclusion — useful as a clear statement of the position (committed branch/merge over rows beats temporal/AS OF rollback) but unsupported by independent evaluation or benchmarks.

“We think if you want Database Version Control for a SQL database, there is only one product that fits that label and that's Dolt.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions (partial vs full persistence) — Gives our two-times split a concrete, named foil. Dolt's AS OF temporal tables = arrival-time/partial-persistence rollback ('You don't get diffs and merges but you do get instant rollbacks'). Dolt's commits = explicit minted full-persistence versions you can branch/merge from. The post's central thesis is that backups/transaction-logs/AS OF are NOT 'true version control' precisely because they lack the committed-branch layer — exactly the argument for why Rama's arrival-time log is insufficient and we must add a git-style committed layer on top.
  • Object-container kernel: stable container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — Dolt's prolly tree (content-addressed binary tree from Noms) is a mechanism for exactly our container/revision split: each commit is an immutable content-addressed value; identity/history is the chain of hashes. Content-addressing means a revision's hash IS derived from its bytes, not declared — which both supports our immutable-revision model and warns about our hard rule: a content hash is computed structure, so equating identity with hash conflates container with value. The post's 'cell-wise' granularity argues identity should be tracked below the file/blob boundary.
  • The code-identity question: is the atomic unit the file or the function; is identity path/name/AST/hash — Dolt's core move — 'implements the Git command line and associated operations on table rows instead of files' — is a direct precedent for rejecting the file as the atomic unit. Git versions files (blobs); Dolt re-bases identity on rows+cells. The analogue for us: don't version the .clj file, version the function/AST-node, with cell-wise (field-wise) diff. This is the strongest single support in the source for 'atomic unit = function, not file.'
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data), must compose — Dolt separates row/cell-level data versioning from schema versioning, and the post repeatedly insists 'true' version control covers BOTH 'schema and data' together. That is a partial precedent for level-composition: schema-version maps to our relation-TYPE level, cell-data maps to our node-DATA level. But it's a WARNING too — the post admits Dolt doesn't cleanly separate them ('doesn't explicitly separate these as distinct features'); schema and data co-version in one commit. Our four-level requirement is more ambitious than anything shipped here.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change vs INFERRED change — Dolt produces 'cell-wise diffs and merges' — i.e. INFERRED change by diffing two committed values. The post never offers a declared-change channel; all change knowledge is reconstructed post-hoc by diff. This both gives us a mechanism (cell-wise structural diff to resolve trail path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment) and marks the gap our design fills: the agent's DECLARED edit operation is information Dolt-style systems throw away and recompute.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality, late-bound synthesis — Branch + cell-wise merge with conflict detection is the mechanism for preserved disagreement: two branches hold conflicting values simultaneously, conflicts are surfaced at merge (late-bound synthesis) rather than silently overwritten. 'The only SQL database that has branches and merges.' Supports our plurality model; the prolly-tree three-way merge is a concrete substrate pattern.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced log as our version substrate — Cautionary mapping. Dolt's whole value rests on the prolly tree giving 'fast diff and merge ... without compromising too much read or write performance' — a purpose-built versioned storage engine. Rama gives us the event log (arrival time) but NOT cell-wise structural diff/merge for free. The post implies that committed-version diff/branch/merge is a storage-engine-level concern, warning that we must build the diff/merge layer atop Rama PStates rather than assume the log provides it.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with independent history — Weak/absent in the source — a noted gap. Dolt versions tables and rows but a Dolt commit versions the whole database snapshot atomically; there is no notion of a curated sub-grouping with its own independent version line and pinned/floating member binding. The post offers no mechanism here, signalling this is novel surface our design must invent rather than borrow.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the sharp distinction between rollback-versioning and branch/merge-versioning, and the claim that only the latter is 'true' version control. Mapped to us: Rama's arrival-time log (≈ AS OF temporal tables — instant rollback, no diff/merge) is necessary but NOT sufficient; we must add an explicit committed/minted layer that supports branch and cell-wise merge, exactly our two-times split. Dolt's concrete mechanism — re-basing git from files to rows/cells via content-addressed prolly trees — is direct precedent for making the function/AST-node (not the file) the atomic versioned unit with field-wise diff. The cautionary note: Dolt versions whole-database snapshots atomically and reconstructs all change by diffing (inferred, never declared), so it offers no model for independently-versioned sub-groupings or for a declared-change channel — both of which our design must invent rather than borrow.

15. XTDB v1 Documentation — Bitemporality

JUXT (XTDB project documentation) · ~2021 (v1 docs, undated page) · XTDB v1 official docs (v1-docs.xtdb.com)

In one line. A documentation page defining XTDB's bitemporal model — independent valid-time and transaction-time axes, append-only transaction time, retroactive/proactive corrections, and a 'partially persistent, fully retroactive' index — illustrated by a border-crossing crime-investigation example.

Source access: full. Read the live page at https://v1-docs.xtdb.com/concepts/bitemporality/ in full via two WebFetch passes (concepts pass + worked-example pass). This is a docs page, not a peer-reviewed paper; the underlying theory citation it leans on is Snodgrass's bitemporal work and the Driscoll et al. 'persistent data structures' terminology, which the page references by name ('partially persistent and fully retroactive data structure') but does not reproduce.

The paper, section by section

§ Overview. Establishes that XTDB is bitemporal: every fact is positioned on two independent timestamp axes, transaction-time and valid-time. These two axes together let the database answer point-in-time queries across both an audit history (what did we know, and when) and domain-specific temporal data (what was actually true in the world, and when). The framing is that these are orthogonal coordinates, not a single timeline — a database state is a function of a (valid-time, tx-time) pair.

“XTDB implements a bitemporal model using two independent timestamps: transaction-time and valid-time.”
“These axes enable point-in-time queries across both audit history and domain-specific temporal data.”

§ Transaction Time vs. Valid Time. Defines the two axes precisely and asymmetrically. Transaction-time is 'the point at which data is transacted into the database' — it is an immutable audit trail, is automatically set to 'now' when unspecified, and crucially CANNOT be written to the past (append-only). Valid-time is arbitrary: it can come from an upstream system or default to the transaction-time, and it is the axis users normally query against to ask 'what is the value of this entity at valid-time?' The asymmetry is the heart of the model: transaction-time is monotonic/append-only (you can never rewrite when you learned something), while valid-time is freely placeable in past or future (you can assert anything about when a fact was/will be true). A table assigns transaction-time to audit/event-sourcing purposes and valid-time to historical analysis and user queries.

“Transaction Time represents 'the point at which data is transacted into the database.' It provides an immutable audit trail and cannot be written to the past.”
“Valid Time is 'an arbitrary time that can originate from an upstream system, or by default is set to transaction-time.'”
“what is the value of this entity at valid-time?”

§ Retroactive and Proactive Operations. Describes the two directions writes can take along the valid-time axis. Retroactive operations write into the PAST of valid-time — they correct historical records when errors surface, asserting that something was true earlier than (or differently from) what was previously recorded. Proactive operations write into the FUTURE of valid-time — scheduling states that will become true later. The key consequence stated is that this decoupling lets data arrive out-of-order without compromising query consistency: because transaction-time independently records arrival, a retroactive correction does not destroy the prior belief, it just adds a later-known fact about an earlier valid-time.

“Writes can be made in the past of valid-time as retroactive operations.”
“Writes can also be made in the future of valid-time as proactive operations.”
“This decoupling allows data to arrive out-of-order without compromising query consistency.”

§ Bitemporal Querying (crime-investigation example). Demonstrates querying by pinning a coordinate on EACH axis simultaneously. The worked example tracks persons p1–p7 crossing US borders; each document carries xt/id, entry-pt (SFO/LA/NY), arrival-time, and departure-time (:na if still present). Facts are ingested over several days: Day 0 records persons 2 and 3 arriving (valid-time Dec 31 2018); Day 2 records person 4 arriving NY; Day 3 person 4 departs; Day 4 (transaction-time) RETROACTIVELY records person 1's arrival back at valid-time Dec 31 2018 plus their departure, and records person 3 departing. The investigative query 'find all persons known present in the U.S. on day 2 (valid-time) as of day 3 (transaction-time)' is expressed by passing both ::xt/valid-time #inst 2019-01-02 ('as at') and ::xt/tx #inst 2019-01-03 ('as of'). Result: persons 2, 3, 4 — i.e., the world as it was BELIEVED on tx-day-3 about valid-day-2, deliberately excluding facts learned later. This shows the database can reconstruct not just past truth but past BELIEF about past truth.

“(xt/q (xt/db node {::xt/valid-time #inst "2019-01-02" ::xt/tx #inst "2019-01-03"})”
“Find all persons known present in the U.S. on day 2 (valid time), as of day 3 (transaction time)”
“corrections to data can flow in from various sources and at various times”

§ Correction Example (Person 3). Shows how a correction is represented WITHOUT destructive overwrite. On Day 7 it emerges that Person 3's recorded departure (valid-time Jan 4) was wrong. The system ingests two documents both at valid-time Jan 4 2019 but at a later transaction-time: first a fact asserting Person 3 did NOT depart on Day 4 (departure-time :na), then a fact that Person 3 actually departed on Day 7. Critically, the original (mistaken) belief remains queryable as-of the earlier transaction-time, while the corrected belief is visible as-of the later transaction-time. The two contradictory states coexist, separated along the audit axis — the correction is additive, not a mutation. This is the concrete demonstration that retroactive writes preserve, rather than erase, prior beliefs.

“Person 3 never departed on Day 4 (departure-time :na)”
“corrections to data can flow in from various sources and at various times”

§ Deletes and Evictions. Distinguishes two erasure semantics. A default DELETE only removes visibility of data from the current perspective — the historical record (both axes) is retained and remains queryable as-of past transaction-times, so the audit trail is intact. EVICTION is the stronger, irreversible operation that erases data completely, provided specifically for cases where the legal status of information changes (e.g., GDPR-style right-to-be-forgotten / compliance erasure). This separation lets the system keep its immutable-audit-trail guarantee by default while still supporting hard erasure as an explicit, exceptional act.

“By default, deleting data only erases visibility of that data from the current perspective.”
“You may of course still evict data completely as the legal status of information changes.”

§ Index Persistence Model. Names the data-structure theory underneath the model: XTDB's bitemporal indexes form 'a partially persistent and fully retroactive data structure.' Partial persistence (from the persistent-data-structures literature) means you can READ any previous version but can only UPDATE the latest version — this maps onto transaction-time's append-only immutability (history is read-only; only the present can be extended). Full retroactivity means you can efficiently make modifications to PAST operations while keeping global consistency — this maps onto valid-time, where retroactive writes edit the past and the whole structure stays coherent. The two properties together are what make bitemporal correction efficient rather than requiring full recomputation.

“a 'partially persistent and fully retroactive data structure'”
“Partially persistent: Transaction-time immutability allows querying any previous version but updating only the latest”
“Fully retroactive: Valid-time representation enables efficient modifications to past operations while maintaining global consistency”

§ Compliance and Auditing. Closes by motivating why bitemporality matters operationally. It enables auditing all data changes and performing data forensics when necessary, and lets organizations respond to new compliance regulations and audit requirements by maintaining complete historical visibility across BOTH temporal dimensions. The argument is that single-timeline databases lose the distinction between 'what was true' and 'what we believed' — bitemporality is what lets you reconstruct prior beliefs for audit and forensic purposes, which a mutate-in-place store cannot.

“Auditing all data changes and performing data forensics when necessary”
“Responding to new compliance regulations and audit requirements”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time vs committed/true time) — Gives our two-times distinction its canonical names and a working implementation: transaction-time = when-it-arrived (append-only, immutable audit log = our Rama default arrival history / partial persistence), valid-time = when-it-was-true (arbitrary, correctable = our git-style committed/minted truth). XTDB proves these are genuinely independent axes you query as a pair, not one derived from the other. Direct support for treating versioning as its own layer with two clocks.
  • Object-container kernel (container=identity thread, revisions=immutable values, current=state) — xt/id is exactly our stable CONTAINER; each transacted document at a (valid-time, tx-time) coordinate is an immutable REVISION; 'what is the value of this entity at valid-time' is our current-revision/state query. XTDB shows the container's state is a FUNCTION of two coordinates, not one — a sharper model than a single current pointer. Validates the kernel shape and warns that 'current revision' is under-specified until you say current-as-of-which-time.
  • Declared vs inferred change (trail-to-code join, the first consumer) — Retroactive writes = an agent DECLARING 'this was actually true back at valid-time T' even though it arrives now; tx-time records when we learned it. This is precisely the DECLARED-change channel: the trail says what changed and when it was effective, separate from when the kernel observed it. Supports resolving a trail's path+timestamp by querying valid-time=edit-moment while keeping tx-time=ingest-moment for provenance. Reinforces the hard rule: inferred structure is never accepted as truth — only declared (transacted) facts enter, and even those are corrigible without destroying prior beliefs.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (late-bound synthesis) — The Person-3 correction shows the WRONG belief is NOT overwritten: the original (departed Day 4) remains queryable as-of the old tx-time, while the corrected fact (departed Day 7) is queryable as-of the new tx-time. This is structurally preserved disagreement across the audit axis — both views coexist, distinguished by transaction-time. A concrete mechanism for never silently overwriting a conflicting view.
  • Substrate choice: Rama as event-sourced log with time-travel — XTDB labels its index 'partially persistent and fully retroactive,' mapping exactly onto our intuition that Rama's arrival-time log gives partial persistence (read any past, write only latest). XTDB warns this alone is insufficient: you ALSO need full retroactivity (efficient edits to the past of valid-time). Tells us Rama's default log gives us only one of the two axes; the valid-time/committed-version axis must be built explicitly on top.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph/node/relation-type/node-data) and groupings as versioned objects — XTDB versions only entity-documents on two time axes; it does NOT natively version graph structure, relation types, or groupings independently. This is a partial-coverage WARNING: bitemporality gives us per-object two-axis history for free but says nothing about composing version histories across the four graph levels or giving a grouping/local-world its own timeline. We must layer that ourselves; bitemporality is the per-node primitive, not the whole answer.
  • Deletes/evictions and the code-identity question — XTDB distinguishes logical delete (hide from current perspective, history retained) from eviction (true erasure for legal/compliance). Bears on our container lifecycle: a code unit going away should default to logical delete (the container's thread continues, just no current revision) with eviction reserved for hard removal. Also: XTDB pins identity to an explicit xt/id rather than inferring it from content — a vote for an assigned-container-id over deriving code identity from AST/path/git-hash.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the rigorous, implemented separation of two clocks — transaction-time (append-only, immutable, 'when we learned it') and valid-time (arbitrary, correctable, 'when it was true') — as INDEPENDENT query coordinates, formalized as a 'partially persistent and fully retroactive' index. This is exactly our versioning-as-its-own-layer distinction (Rama arrival-time = partial persistence; git-style committed/true time = full retroactivity), and XTDB shows that retroactive corrections can be additive rather than destructive, so prior (even wrong) beliefs stay queryable — a ready-made mechanism for both the declared-vs-inferred change channel and preserved disagreement. The caution: bitemporality is a per-OBJECT two-axis primitive; it does not natively compose across our four graph levels or give groupings their own timelines — we must build that layer on top.

What we can gather — Angle 3

The production-database angle does two things the theory cannot: it proves the two-layer pattern ships, and it puts numbers on the cost.

The pattern is convergent and identical everywhere. Irmin states it most cleanly — an immutable, content-addressed block store (key = hash of value) plus a tiny mutable tag store (named pointers into it) — which is our revision/container split exactly, offered as a library over arbitrary data. Dolt, Noms, TerminusDB, git all land on the same shape. The lesson is that we are not betting on an unproven architecture; we are implementing the convergent one.

Structural sharing is what makes keeping all of history affordable. Prolly trees (Dolt's engine) make storage and diff scale with the size of the change, not the number or size of versions — and hand us a sharp design rule: hash the keys, not the values, so an edit touches one chunk and unchanged subtrees are shared by content address. This directly vindicates "function-id is the key, body is the value" over "git-hash is the identity," because content-keyed identity would re-shuffle on every edit and destroy the sharing.

The two times are named and implemented by XTDB. Transaction-time (append-only, immutable, "when we learned it") versus valid-time (arbitrary, correctable, "when it was true") are independent query coordinates — and crucially, retroactive correction is additive: the earlier, wrong belief stays queryable as-of its old transaction-time. That is a ready-made substrate for our exactness flag and for preserved disagreement: a corrected trail resolution does not overwrite the approximate one.

The honest cost and the honest gap: Irmin measures git-format versioning at ~5x overhead and flags that merge needs a known common ancestor (LCA discovery "remains unaddressed" — our problem to solve). The Dolt essays draw the line that rollback (AS OF / temporal tables) is not version control — you need the committed branch/merge layer — and warn that commit-graph storage "is not a natural fit" for a query engine. So: Rama gives us the append-only log (one axis, for free) but not the content-addressed, branchable, O(change)-diff committed-version structure — that is a layer we build on top (the prolly-tree role), at a budgetable ~5x, with LCA discovery as a known cost.

Angle 4 — Graph & RDF Versioning: The Multi-Level Case

16. TerminusDB Explanation (What is TerminusDB? / Graphs Explanation + supporting docs)

TerminusDB / TerminusCC (docs; Big Babushka post by Luke Feeney; succinct-structures material by the TerminusDB team) · 2020-2025 · TerminusDB official documentation (terminusdb.org/docs) + TerminusDB engineering blog/Medium

In one line. TerminusDB is a document-graph database that is 'git for data': it never mutates, appending every change as an immutable, content-addressed delta layer of RDF triples on positive/negative planes, with a separate commit graph providing branch, merge, diff/patch, clone and time-travel.

Source access: partial. The primary URL (terminusdb.org/docs/terminusdb-explanation) is an introductory page that NAMES the mechanisms but does not detail them. I read it in full, then — because the deep technical substance was absent — pulled the open companion docs and posts to recover mechanism: the Graphs Explanation doc (graph hierarchy + commit/branch/layer control graphs), a community 'Storing data like git' write-up and search-surfaced excerpts (the +ve/-ve plane, HEAD-layer query, base vs delta layers), the Wikipedia/Grokipedia summaries (terminus-store Rust backend, WOQL, schema), and the terminus-store README/docs.rs (IdTriple, dictionaries, layers). The Medium 'Big Babushka' post (HTTP 522) and the succinct-data-structures blog (HTTP 522) and tdb-succinct README would not load, so wavelet-tree/rank-select internals are reported only at the level the loadable sources confirm. Numbers (~13 bytes/triple) come from the official intro page.

The paper, section by section

§ What is TerminusDB? (intro page top). Frames TerminusDB as 'an open-source document graph database with built-in version control.' The single architectural commitment stated up front: it 'stores JSON documents in a schema-enforced graph, tracks every change as an immutable delta layer, and supports branch, diff, merge, clone, and time-travel operations.' This is the thesis the rest of the doc justifies — documents on the surface, a versioned triple graph underneath, version control as a built-in property rather than an add-on. The framing is deliberately 'like Git, but for structured data rather than files.'

“open-source document graph database with built-in version control”
“stores JSON documents in a schema-enforced graph, tracks every change as an immutable delta layer”
“like Git, but for structured data rather than files”

§ What Makes TerminusDB Different (the immutability principle). States the core invariant: 'every commit appends a new delta layer — an immutable record of what was added and what was removed.' Four consequences are derived directly from this one rule. (1) Full history: 'every commit is preserved. You can time-travel to any previous state instantly.' (2) Branch and merge: isolated branches edited independently then merged back. (3) Diff and patch: 'compare any two states... to see exactly what changed, at the document or triple level' — note the explicit TWO granularities. (4) Lock-free concurrency: 'because committed layers are immutable, readers never block writers.' The argument is that immutability is not a feature but the generator of all the version-control features.

“every commit appends a new delta layer — an immutable record of what was added and what was removed”
“every commit is preserved. You can time-travel to any previous state instantly”
“compare any two states of a database to see exactly what changed, at the document or triple level”
“because committed layers are immutable, readers never block writers”

§ How It Works (layer stack + content addressing). Describes the read/write mechanics. Documents 'decompose into RDF triples stored in succinct data structures called delta layers. Each layer is content-addressed and immutable.' Reads work by traversal: 'when you query the database, TerminusDB traverses the layer stack to resolve the current state' — i.e. the current value is computed by walking the chain of deltas, not stored as a flat snapshot. Because long chains slow reads, 'periodic delta rollup operations compress the stack to maintain read performance.' Content-addressing means a layer's identity IS its content hash, so identical layers dedupe and a commit can name its parent layer by hash.

“RDF triples stored in succinct data structures called delta layers. Each layer is content-addressed and immutable”
“TerminusDB traverses the layer stack to resolve the current state”
“Periodic delta rollup operations compress the stack to maintain read performance”

§ Storage efficiency (succinct encoding). Quantifies the storage claim that makes keeping all history affordable: succinct encoding yields 'approximately 13 bytes per triple' measured 'on billion-triple datasets.' The deeper sources (and the Wikipedia/Grokipedia summaries) attribute this to the Rust 'terminus-store' backend introduced in v1.1 (Jan 2020), which encodes triples as ID-triples over node/predicate/value dictionaries in compressed (succinct) structures, operating in-memory with ACID persistence to disk. The point being argued: succinctness is what makes 'store every version forever' economically viable rather than a liability.

“approximately 13 bytes per triple”
“benchmarks on billion-triple datasets”

§ Positive / negative planes (the delta mechanism — from companion docs). The intro page hides HOW a delta encodes a change; the companion material reveals it. Each layer (except the base/initial layer, where 'everything was created') stores triples on one of two planes: '+ve means new data is added and -ve means that data is deleted.' A triple is 'the edge and the nodes that it links.' Query resolution walks layers from HEAD backward: 'if it is found in the +ve plane, it exists in the database; if it is found in the -ve plane, it existed at some point in time but was deleted... it does not exist anymore.' Committing builds the +ve and -ve planes of a new layer 'and then commit it to the series of layers, moving the HEAD forward.' This is the actual representation of declared additions and removals — deletion is recorded as a positive fact on the negative plane, never as erasure, which is why prior states remain recoverable.

“data... is stored either on the +ve plane or -ve plane (except the initial layer as everything was created)”
“+ve means new data is added and -ve means that data is deleted”
“if it is found in the +ve plane, it exists in the database; if it is found in the -ve plane, it... does not exist anymore”
“moving the HEAD forward”

§ Graph hierarchy: data graphs vs control graphs (Graphs Explanation doc). TerminusDB is not one graph but a hierarchy: 'a hierarchical collection of data graphs and control graphs.' DATA graphs split into instance, schema, and inference graphs — separating asserted data from the schema constraints that govern it (relevant to our 'inference is not truth at ingest' rule: structure lives in a distinct schema graph, enforced, not folded into instance data). CONTROL graphs 'govern data graphs and database functionality' and come in three kinds: repository, commit, and branch graphs. The lowest level of every graph 'is a single graph composed of a sequence of layers,' and transactions are themselves hierarchical to guarantee ACID. This layered hierarchy is what lets versioning operate uniformly at multiple granularities.

“A TerminusDB database is a hierarchical collection of data graphs and control graphs”
“The lowest level of the hierarchy is a single graph composed of a sequence of layers”
“Layers specify each change to data such as additions and deletions”

§ Commit graph, branch graph, repository graph (the version-control control plane). The version-control machinery lives in dedicated control graphs, separate from the raw layer stack. The COMMIT graph 'provides revision control... contains the information required for time-travel, branch, squash, reset and rebase operations' — each commit object carries a parent reference and is 'associated with layers representing the branch of interest,' linking the commit timeline to the underlying data deltas. BRANCH objects 'point to a commit object,' so a branch is a movable ref into the commit DAG. The REPOSITORY graph tracks other local/remote TerminusDB instances, enabling clone/push/pull across machines. This is the explicit, minted, branchable commit history layered ON TOP of the append-only delta storage — the two-time architecture made concrete.

“Commit graphs provide revision control. They contain the information required for time-travel, branch, squash, reset and rebase operations”
“Branch objects in a commit-graph point to a commit object”
“Repository graphs provide collaboration and communication with other TerminusDB instances”

§ Query interfaces and schema (WOQL / GraphQL / JSON schema — from summaries). Two query surfaces over the same versioned graph: WOQL, a datalog-based language that lets the store 'function as a document store or a graph interchangeably,' and GraphQL for typed link traversal. Schema moved from OWL (early versions) to a JSON-based schema in v10.0, giving 'constraints on the allowable shapes in the graph' without heavyweight ontology languages. The schema is itself a versioned graph, so type-level structure evolves through the same commit/layer mechanism as data. This matters for our relation-TYPE versioning concern: TerminusDB DOES version types, but through the same single mechanism rather than as an independent versioning level.

“as a document store or a graph interchangeably”
“constraints on the allowable shapes in the graph”

§ Git-like operation suite (synthesis across docs). The payoff repeatedly stated: the immutable-layer + succinct-structure combination yields 'the whole suite of revision control features: branch, merge, squash, rollback, blame, and time-travel' and enables 'CI/CD approaches on data.' Because all versions are retained, 'TerminusDB can query any past or present commit.' The explicit claim is that these are not separately engineered features but emergent consequences of two design choices — append-only immutable deltas (giving history and lock-free reads) and content-addressed succinct layers (giving cheap storage and dedup). This is the strongest external evidence that our append-only-substrate + explicit-commit-layer architecture can deliver the full git-for-knowledge feature set.

“the whole suite of revision control features: branch, merge, squash, rollback, blame, and time-travel”
“CI/CD approaches on data”
“TerminusDB can query any past or present commit”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread; REVISIONS = immutable values; current-revision = state. 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest.' — STRONG SUPPORT + a working mechanism. TerminusDB's layer stack IS exactly the container/revision split: the named graph (e.g. a branch's instance graph) is the stable container; each immutable, content-addressed delta layer is a revision; the HEAD layer is current-revision/state. Their hard invariant — 'never mutates existing data; every commit appends a new immutable delta layer' — is the production-proven version of our container-kernel discipline. It also validates our 'inference is not truth' rule from a different angle: TerminusDB separates the SCHEMA graph from the INSTANCE graph and enforces schema as a constraint, so structure is asserted/committed, never silently inferred into the data plane.
  • Versioning as its own layer: Rama gives arrival-time history (partial persistence / time-travel log); we ALSO want explicit git-style COMMITTED/MINTED versions to branch from (full persistence).
