Two times: when-it-arrived vs when-it-was-committed. — DIRECT BLUEPRINT for the two-time distinction. TerminusDB physically separates these into two graph families: the LAYER stack (append-only deltas, the 'log' = arrival/storage time) and the COMMIT GRAPH (explicit minted commits with parent pointers, branch refs = committed/true time). 'The commit graph keeps track of the time at which facts are known... connects it to the parent commit.' This is precisely our Rama-log-vs-minted-version split. Their commit graph is a separate queryable control graph layered over the raw delta storage — exactly the architecture we want: derive cheap arrival-history for free, then mint explicit branchable commits on top.
  • Four-level graph versioning: version at graph level, node level, relation-TYPE level, AND node-DATA level, and these levels must COMPOSE. — PARTIAL SUPPORT + a warning. TerminusDB versions at the GRAPH level (branch/commit granularity) and at the TRIPLE level (additions/removals on the +ve/-ve plane = node-DATA and relation granularity), and 'diff and patch... at the document or triple level' gives us node-level (document) AND edge-level diffs that compose into a graph-level commit. But relation-TYPE versioning lives in the SCHEMA graph, which is versioned the SAME way as instance data (it is also a layered graph). WARNING: TerminusDB unifies all four levels into ONE mechanism (layered triples) rather than giving each level independent version history — so a schema-type change and a data change both become commits in the same graph. Our requirement that the four levels be SEPARATELY versioned yet COMPOSABLE is stronger than what TerminusDB offers out of the box.
  • The trail-to-code join: resolve a trail's path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment. DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing). — MECHANISM + sharp naming. TerminusDB's time-travel ('query any past or present commit') is exactly path+timestamp -> state-at-that-moment resolution. More importantly, the +ve/-ve plane gives us BOTH sides of our declared-vs-inferred axis natively: the layer stores the DECLARED delta (what the committer asserted as added/removed), while diff/patch RECONSTRUCTS change by comparing two states. TerminusDB shows these can coexist: the committed layer is the declared change; diffing two layers is the inferred change. Our trail join should store the agent's declared edit-op as the +ve/-ve delta and reserve diffing for verification.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated 'local world' has its own version history, partly independent of its members; member binding may be pinned, floating, or effectivity-scoped. — PARTIAL SUPPORT + a gap. A TerminusDB BRANCH is a versioned grouping with its own commit history independent of the underlying data layers — and clone/push/pull let groupings be shared and synced. This supports our 'grouping has its own version history' claim. GAP: TerminusDB branches PIN the entire dataset to a commit (one binding mode); it has no first-class notion of FLOATING or effectivity-scoped membership where a grouping tracks the latest revision of some members while pinning others. Our pinned/floating/scoped binding requirement is a generalization beyond TerminusDB's whole-branch pinning.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicting views representable, not silently overwritten; synthesis is late-bound. — SUPPORT via branch/merge model. TerminusDB's git-like 'branch, merge, squash, rollback, blame' lets divergent states coexist as branches without overwrite, with merge as an explicit late-bound reconciliation — structurally identical to our preserved-disagreement-then-synthesis stance. The +ve/-ve plane even preserves DELETED facts (found on the -ve plane) rather than erasing them, so prior assertions remain recoverable. WARNING: git-style merge forces eventual single-resolution; our plurality model may need to keep branches UNMERGED indefinitely as first-class plural truth, which TerminusDB treats as an unfinished merge rather than a stable state.
  • Substrate choices: Rama (event-sourced), content-addressing, succinct/compact storage at scale. — VALIDATION of feasibility + a number. TerminusDB proves the append-only-immutable-delta + content-addressed-layer substrate is production-viable at scale: 'approximately 13 bytes per triple' on billion-triple datasets via succinct data structures, with 'lock-free concurrency... because committed layers are immutable, readers never block writers.' This de-risks our Rama-as-append-only-substrate bet and supplies a concrete cost target. It also flags an operational need we'd otherwise miss: 'periodic delta rollup operations compress the stack to maintain read performance' — long layer chains degrade reads, so we need a compaction story for our own version layer.

Takeaway. TerminusDB is the production existence-proof for our exact two-time architecture: an append-only stack of immutable, content-addressed delta layers (the arrival/storage log, where every change is a +ve/-ve plane delta and deletion is a recorded fact, never erasure) with a SEPARATE commit graph of parent-linked, branchable commits layered on top (the explicit minted-version time). It validates the object-container/revision split, the declared-delta-vs-inferred-diff distinction (diff/patch works at both document and triple level), and the cost story (~13 bytes/triple keeps all history affordable), while warning us where it stops short of our ambitions: it unifies all four versioning levels into one mechanism rather than versioning them independently, and its branch binding is whole-dataset pinning with no floating/effectivity-scoped membership — the two places our design must go beyond git-for-data.

17. Triple Storage for Random-Access Versioned Querying of RDF Archives (OSTRICH)

Ruben Taelman, Miel Vander Sande, Joachim Van Herwegen, Erik Mannens, Ruben Verborgh · 2018 · Journal of Web Semantics (JWS)

In one line. A hybrid IC/CB/TB RDF-archive index using snapshots followed by aggregated delta chains, preprocessing metadata at ingest to serve fast offset-capable VM/DM/VQ versioned queries — at the cost of monotonically growing ingestion time in a single linear history.

Source access: full. Read the full open-access HTML version at the authors' homepage (rdfostrich.github.io/article-jws2018-ostrich/), the canonical author-hosted copy of the JWS 2018 paper. Two WebFetch passes: one for full section content, one for verbatim quotes. Not blocked or paywalled.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. Introduces an RDF-archive indexing technique balancing storage efficiency against versioned-query performance. History is kept as compressed consecutive versions with added metadata to cut lookup time. Presents algorithms for queries AT a version (VM), BETWEEN versions (DM), and FOR versions (VQ). The implementation OSTRICH establishes a new trade-off point: by doing more work at ingestion, it lowers average versioned-query lookup time, supports result offsets for random access, and lowers the barrier to publishing historical datasets.

“By processing and storing more metadata during ingestion time, it significantly lowers the average lookup time for versioning queries.”

§ Introduction. Frames dataset history as essential as Linked Open Datasets evolve at dataset, schema, and instance levels. Identifies the core tension: query-optimized archives need large storage, while space-efficient ones query slowly. Argues for an elementary-but-efficient versioned triple-pattern index as the foundation for richer querying, because combining full SPARQL with archiving does not scale. Lists three needs (scalable storage, compression, expressive versioned indexing) and stream-based results, offsetting, and cardinality estimation as requirements. Contributions: a versioned compressed index with offset support, VM/DM/VQ algorithms, OSTRICH, and BEAR evaluation.

“supporting both RDF archiving and SPARQL at once is difficult to scale due to their combined complexity”

§ Related Work. Surveys non-versioned RDF stores (RDF-3X's lexicographic indexes, Hexastore's six sorted lists, TripleBit, K2-Triples, RDFCSA, and HDT's bitmap-encoded dictionary representation). Then categorizes RDF archiving into three non-orthogonal strategies — Independent Copies (IC, full snapshot per version), Change-Based (CB, store only deltas), Timestamp-Based (TB, store temporal validity) — reviewing SemVersion, R&WBase, R43ples (CB with SPARQL extensions), x-RDF-3X (TB with create/delete timestamps), RDF-TX (B+Trees), v-RDFCSA, Dydra, and TailR (hybrid IC/CB delta chains). Introduces five query atoms (VM, DM, VQ, plus cross-version CV and change CM) and the BEAR benchmark. Notes the known trade-off: IC fast for VM but storage-heavy, CB good for DM but slow for VM/VQ, TB constant for VM but weak for DM/VQ.

“Systems for archiving Linked Open Data are categorized into three non-orthogonal storage strategies: Independent Copies (IC), Change-Based (CB), Timestamp-Based (TB).”

§ Problem Statement and Hypotheses. Poses the central question: how to store RDF archives for efficient VM, DM, and VQ triple-pattern queries WITH offsets. States six hypotheses that frame OSTRICH's expected position between IC and CB: selected version should not affect VM/DM efficiency; storage smaller than IC but larger than CB; VM slower than IC but DM/VQ equal-or-faster; querying equal-or-better than CB; and lower average query time bought at the price of higher ingestion time.

“How can we store RDF archives to enable efficient VM, DM and VQ triple pattern queries with offsets?”

§ Fundamentals / Storage Approach. Core design: combine IC, CB, and TB via intermittent fully-materialized snapshots followed by AGGREGATED delta chains, where each delta is relative to the closest preceding snapshot (not to the previous delta), bounding version reconstruction to snapshot + one delta. Uses three B+Trees (SPO, POS, OSP orders) rather than six, giving more balanced pattern-to-index distribution than HDT-FoQ. Additions and deletions are stored separately, dictionary-encoded and timestamped, each carrying a LOCAL CHANGE flag marking triples that revert an earlier change within the chain. Stores separate addition/deletion counts plus relative deletion positions to enable offsets and cardinality estimation. Snapshot stored via HDT; two dictionaries (snapshot + delta) distinguished by reserved bits.

“Each delta is independent of a preceding delta and relative to the closest preceding snapshot in the chain.”
“Triples that revert a previous addition or deletion within the same delta chain, are called local changes.”

§ Ingestion Algorithms. Transforms incoming CB-style deltas into the aggregated alternative-delta-chain structure. Batch ingestion loads a changeset in memory at O(P + N log N) but is memory-hungry for large versions. Streaming ingestion does a sort-merge join over three SPO-ordered streams (input changeset, existing deletions, existing additions), handling seven merge cases and keeping only stream heads in memory while maintaining relative-position counters. Crucially, ingestion time grows with every added version — a direct consequence of aggregating each delta against the snapshot. Measured: BEAR-A 2,256 s total; BEAR-B-daily 12.36 s; BEAR-B-hourly 4,497 s across 1,299 versions, hitting a swapping wall near version 1,200.

“Within the scope of this article, we only discuss ingestion of deltas in a single delta chain following a snapshot.”
“For each additional version in a dataset, the ingestion time increases. This is a direct consequence of our alternative delta chain method.”

§ Query Algorithms (VM / DM / VQ). VM (state at version i) uses a three-stream sort-merge join over snapshot, deletions, and additions, with an offset-convergence loop: the requested offset is iteratively increased by matched deletion positions to find the right snapshot offset, proven to terminate because offset is a non-decreasing function bounded by the finite deletion list — yielding near-constant lookup independent of version. DM (change set between i and j) either filters the delta directly (when start = snapshot) or iterates addition/deletion trees comparing flags across the two versions. VQ (versions in which a result is valid) joins snapshot and addition trees, querying deletion trees to annotate each triple with the version range it holds in (all versions except those where it was deleted).

“Version materialization (VM) retrieves data using a query Q targeted at a single version Vi.”
“Delta materialization (DM) retrieves query Q's result change sets between two versions Vi and Vj.”
“Version query (VQ) annotates query Q's results with the versions in which they are valid.”

§ Evaluation (BEAR benchmark). Implemented in C/C++ with HDT snapshots, Kyoto Cabinet compressed B+Trees, and gzip delta dictionaries; JS bindings expose a Triple Pattern Fragments API serving 30M triples over 10 versions. Storage: BEAR-A 3,117.64 MB (OSTRICH) vs 2,682.88 MB HDT-CB and 5,335.04 MB HDT-IC — between CB and IC as predicted. VM was ~2x faster than HDT-CB on BEAR-A with constant time as versions grew while CB degraded; DM matched HDT-IC and beat CB from version 7; VQ on BEAR-B-hourly beat all HDT approaches by nearly an order of magnitude. Offsets stayed below 1 ms for VM (offsets 2–4096) and were roughly offset-independent for VQ. Gzip savings reached 99.58% on BEAR-B-hourly. The clear bottleneck was ingestion swapping in long chains.

“outperforming all HDT-based approaches by almost an order of magnitude”

§ Conclusions and Future Work. OSTRICH establishes a new storage/ingestion/query trade-off for RDF archives by front-loading metadata computation at ingest. It confirms most hypotheses: less storage than IC, VM/DM/VQ equal-or-faster than CB, lower average query time at the cost of higher ingestion time. It excels for datasets with many small versions but struggles with few large versions due to materialization overhead, and does not scale past roughly 1,300 versions without dynamic snapshot renewal. Stated limitations: support for only a single snapshot and single delta chain (no branching), and monotonically increasing ingestion cost. Future work points to multiple snapshots and branch/variant support.

“For each additional version in a dataset, the ingestion time increases.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time vs committed-time; partial vs full persistence) — OSTRICH is a pure single-axis versioning store: one linear history of versions V0..Vn over arrival-ordered deltas. It operationalizes 'when-it-arrived' time well (the snapshot+delta-chain IS arrival-time persistence) but has NO branch/commit axis at all ('we only discuss ingestion of deltas in a single delta chain following a snapshot'). This is a sharp warning: a single-time model is cheap and queryable but cannot express our second axis (minted/committed/branchable versions). It validates separating the two times — OSTRICH proves how far the arrival-only design goes and exactly where it dead-ends.
  • Object-container kernel: container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, current = state — OSTRICH's aggregated-delta model gives a concrete mechanism for the container/revision split. A triple-pattern identity is a thread; each version is an immutable value reconstructable as snapshot+one delta. The 'local change flag' (a triple that reverts a previous add/delete within the chain) is a direct mechanism for the kernel problem of a revision that cancels an earlier revision — they pre-compute and store this at ingest rather than re-deriving it, supporting our 'preprocess truth at ingest, not query time' instinct. But note: OSTRICH infers nothing — every delta is DECLARED input, never reconstructed by diffing, matching our hard rule 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest.'
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, composing) — OSTRICH is explicitly a TRIPLE-pattern index — it versions at exactly one granularity (the triple = a node-relation-node statement) and composes nothing. It changes at 'dataset, schema, and instance levels' but stores all of it as undifferentiated triple adds/deletes. This is a failure-mode warning for our four-level model: collapsing all change into one flat statement-delta stream gives great storage/query numbers but loses the level structure entirely. If we want composition across graph/node/relation-type/node-data levels, we cannot adopt OSTRICH's flat single-grain delta as-is — we'd need per-level delta chains that compose.
  • VM/DM/VQ query atoms as the versioned-query vocabulary — OSTRICH gives us a named, proven query vocabulary we can lift directly. VM = 'state at version i' (our current-revision / time-travel-to read), DM = 'what changed between i and j' (our trail-to-code diff / DECLARED-vs-INFERRED change), VQ = 'in which versions is this fact valid' (our effectivity/condition-scoped binding and provenance query). These three plus the offset/cardinality requirements are a ready checklist for what our versioning layer's read API must answer.
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment) — VM is exactly the trail-to-code resolution primitive: given a version (timestamp), materialize the state. DM is the DECLARED-vs-INFERRED distinction made concrete — OSTRICH ingests DECLARED changesets (the agent says what changed) and never reconstructs by diffing, which is precisely our preference. OSTRICH's offset-convergence VM algorithm (constant-time lookup regardless of which version, via stored deletion positions) is a mechanism for making 'code-at-that-moment' lookups not degrade as history grows.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced log + explicit committed versions — OSTRICH's central limitation maps onto Rama's strength. Its ingestion time grows monotonically with chain length ('for each additional version the ingestion time increases') because it eagerly materializes aggregated deltas — it hit a swapping wall around v1200/1300 of BEAR-B-hourly. Rama gives the cheap append-only arrival log for free (no aggregation cost at write). The lesson: keep the raw event log primary and cheap (Rama), and treat OSTRICH-style aggregated-delta materialization as a DERIVED projection (a PState) we build for fast VM/DM/VQ — not as the write path. Aggregated deltas belong on the read side.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with independent history — OSTRICH offers NO support here and thereby names the gap: it versions one flat triple set with a single global version counter. There is no notion of a sub-collection with its own version line, nor pinned/floating member binding. Its single-snapshot/single-chain constraint is the exact opposite of independently-versioned groupings. It tells us groupings-as-versioned-objects is a requirement the RDF-archive literature does not address — we are extending past it, not reusing it.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality, late-bound synthesis — OSTRICH assumes a single authoritative linear truth — every version is one consistent state, deltas are total and authoritative, conflicts are impossible by construction. This is the anti-pattern for plurality: it cannot represent two contradictory views of the same fact at the same version. It warns that the IC/CB/TB archive model is fundamentally single-truth; preserved disagreement needs a branch/variant axis OSTRICH deliberately excludes.

Takeaway. OSTRICH's core lesson for our versioning/identity layer is the explicit IC/CB/TB trade-off triangle plus a clean, named, proven read vocabulary — VM (state-at-version), DM (change-between-versions), VQ (versions-where-valid) — that our versioning layer's API should answer directly. Its central failure mode is the load-bearing warning: aggregating deltas eagerly at INGEST makes versioned reads fast but makes ingestion time grow monotonically with history length and confines you to a SINGLE linear chain with no branching — which is exactly why our design should keep the cheap append-only arrival log primary (Rama) and treat OSTRICH-style aggregated-delta materialization as a derived read-side projection, while adding the branch/commit axis (and per-level, per-grouping version lines) that OSTRICH deliberately excludes.

18. Evaluating Query and Storage Strategies for RDF Archives

Javier D. Fernández, Jürgen Umbrich, Axel Polleres, Magnus Knuth · 2019 · Semantic Web Journal (IOS Press); also SAGE DOI 10.3233/SW-180309

In one line. Foundations + the BEAR benchmark for RDF archiving: formalizes change/dynamicity metrics and a five-atom query taxonomy, then empirically shows that IC, CB, TB, and hybrid storage strategies each win on different change profiles and that version-count scaling breaks TB outright.

Source access: full. SAGE/journals.sagepub.com returned HTTP 403 (gated). Read the FULL open-access Semantic Web Journal preprint PDF instead: https://www.semantic-web-journal.net/system/files/swj1608.pdf (swj1608, 2808 lines extracted via pdftotext). All definitions, tables (Tables 2-8), and figures-as-text were accessible; only the rendered bitmap plots (Figs 2-5) are unreadable, but their findings are described verbatim in the running text I quote below.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the core problem: there is an emerging demand to efficiently archive and temporally query different versions of evolving Semantic Web data, and as archiving systems appear, there is no foundation or standard for benchmarking them on storage-space efficiency and retrieval performance. The paper's three deliverables: (1) theoretical foundations for designing benchmark data and queries for RDF archives, (2) a concrete instantiation of those queries over a real-world evolving dataset, (3) an empirical evaluation of current archiving techniques and querying strategies meant to be a reusable baseline.

“There is an emerging demand on efficiently archiving and (temporal) querying different versions of evolving semantic Web data.”
“we provide theoretical foundations on the design of data and queries to evaluate emerging RDF archiving systems”

§ 1. Introduction. Motivates archiving as a first-class problem: published Linked Data continuously changes at both data and schema level, and evolving vocabularies introduce inconsistencies between data relying on prior ontology versions. Web-of-Data archives must additionally offer time-traversing structured queries that neither SPARQL nor existing temporal SPARQL extensions natively support (e.g. knowing whether a dataset or a particular entity changed). Surveys prior benchmark gaps: EvoGen extends LUBM but is synthetic and single-dataset; existing RDF/SPARQL benchmarks target static, federated, or streaming data, none covering archiving where querying entity changes across time is crucial. Lists four contributions: theoretical foundations, an AnQL-based query instantiation, the BEAR test suite (BEAR-A/B/C from real datasets), and implemented archiving systems evaluated to set an extensible baseline.

“this published data is continuously undergoing changes (on a data and schema level)”
“archives for the Web of Data should additionally offer capabilities for time-traversing structured queries”
“querying entity changes across time is a crucial aspect”

§ 2.1 Retrieval Functionality (query taxonomy). Compiles a 2x3 classification of retrieval needs along two axes: query type (materialisation vs structured query) and focus (version vs delta). The six cells are: Version materialisation (retrieve a full snapshot at time ti, the most common revision-control feature); Single-version structured queries (a query against one specific version); Cross-version / time-traversal structured queries (satisfied across multiple versions, the novel hard case); Delta materialisation (the added/deleted triples between two versions, tied to authoring/merge/conflict-resolution); and Single-delta / Cross-delta structured queries (the delta-focused counterparts). This taxonomy is the conceptual scaffold the rest of the paper builds queries and metrics around.

“This classification distinguishes six different types of retrieval needs, mainly regarding the query type (materialisation or structured queries) and the main focus (version/delta) of the query.”
“Cross-version structured queries, also called time-traversal queries, add a novel complexity since these queries must be satisfied across different versions.”

§ 2.2 Archiving Policies (the three storage strategies + hybrids). Defines the central IC/CB/TB trichotomy. Independent Copies (IC) stores each version as an isolated dataset; simple but duplicates static information across versions, so it is expected to face scalability problems. Change-based (CB) stores only the deltas (added/deleted triples) between versions, addressing IC's duplication but requiring a query mediator to propagate deltas to reconstruct a version, which costs version-focused retrieval. Timestamp-based (TB) annotates each triple with its temporal/version validity locally, a special case of temporal RDF, using compression/self-indexes to avoid repetition. Hybrid (HB) approaches combine these: HB_IC/CB inserts a fresh materialized IC version after some number of CB deltas (governed by a cost model deciding when to re-materialize); HB_TB/CB time-annotates triples only when added or deleted, often managed under named/virtual graphs so the mediator can reuse existing engines. A static core (triples present in all versions) is implicit in CB but explicit in IC.

“Main efforts addressing the challenge of RDF archiving fall in one of the following three storage strategies: independent copies (IC), change-based (CB) and timestamp-based (TB) approaches.”
“It is, however, expected that IC faces scalability problems as static information is duplicated across the versions.”
“CB requires additional computational costs for delta propagation which affects version-focused retrieving operations.”
“each triple locally holds the timestamp of the version. In order to save space avoiding repetitions, compression techniques can be used”

§ 3.1 Dataset Configuration (formal model + dynamicity metrics). Provides the formal data model adapting Gutierrez et al.'s temporal RDF graphs, but with version labels instead of time labels. Def 1: a version-annotated triple (s,p,o):[i] holds in version i; an RDF archive A is a set of these. Def 2: an RDF version A(i)=Vi is the graph of triples labeled i. Then defines the dynamicity/change metrics that are the paper's measurement vocabulary: Def 3 version change ratio delta_ij = |added union deleted| / |Vi union Vj|, split into insertion and deletion ratios; Def 4 version data growth = |Vj|/|Vi|; Def 5 static core = triples in ALL versions; Def 6 version-oblivious triples = the set of distinct triples ignoring timestamps (the size of the annotation-merged store); Defs 7-9 RDF vocabulary per version, per delta, and vocabulary set dynamicity vdyn (symmetric-difference over union of subject/predicate/object sets). Notes IC is sensitive to insertion/deletion counts while CB and TB are sensitive to overall dynamicity delta, and vocabulary evolution matters because stores use term-to-integer dictionaries.

“A version-annotated triple is an RDF triple (s, p, o) with a label i in N representing the version in which this triple holds, denoted by the notation (s, p, o) : [i]. An RDF archive graph A is a set of version-annotated triples.”
“static core CA = {(s,p,o) | for all i in N, (s,p,o):[i] in A}”
“This computes the total number of different triples in an RDF archive independently of the timestamp.”
“the static core can be easily computed taking the first version and applying all the subsequent deletions.”

§ 3.2 Design of Benchmark Queries (five query atoms). Argues there is no standard language for querying RDF archives, then adapts cardinality/selectivity to the archive setting: archive-driven (Def 10) vs version-driven (Def 11) result cardinality and selectivity, plus version-driven result dynamicity (Def 12, how results change across versions). Introduces five foundational query atoms that combine to express richer needs, explicitly aligned with the Memento Framework's datetime-negotiation needs: Version materialisation Mat(Q,Vi); Delta materialisation Diff(Q,Vi,Vj) (the result tuples present in one version but not the other); Version query Ver(Q) (results annotated with the version label where they hold); Cross-version join Join(Q1,Vi,Q2,Vj); and Change materialisation Change(Q) (the consecutive version pairs where Q's results differ, i.e. timemap construction). Emphasizes controlling cardinality/selectivity so that retrieval-time differences can be attributed to the archiving policy rather than query size.

“we introduce five foundational query atoms to cover the broad spectrum of emerging retrieval demands in RDF archiving”
“Change materialisation, Change(Q): it provides those consecutive versions in which the given query Q produces different results.”
“selecting only queries with the same cardinality and selectivity across all version should guarantee that the index performance is always the same... potential retrieval time differences can be attributed to the archiving policy”

§ 3.3 Instantiation in AnQL (and why off-the-shelf SPARQL is hard). Grounds the five atoms in AnQL, a SPARQL extension where a triple pattern is annotated with a version label t:l. Shows naive SPARQL translations of each policy: IC stores each version in a named graph (version name = graph name); TB stores each triple once as a reified triple with valid [start,end] interval metadata (more compact than IC's per-graph repetition); CB stores adds/deletes in separate per-version named graphs and must FILTER-test whether a triple was added in a prior version and not since deleted. The key methodological finding: implementing archives over off-the-shelf RDF stores with pure SPARQL is non-trivial and does not scale; CB's translation works only for single triple patterns, not full BGPs, and Ver/Change break under the TB recipe because the version becomes an unbound FILTER variable. This justifies using tailored optimized stores (HDT, Jena-TDB) in the evaluation rather than generic SPARQL.

“a change-based storage of RDF triples has no trivial implementation in an off-the shelf RDF store”
“an implementation of RDF archives and queries in off-the-shelf RDF stores and using SPARQL is a non-trivial exercise”
“this representation allows for a compact representation of several disjoint (maximal) validity intervals of the same triple, thus causing less overhead than the graph-based representation discussed for IC”

§ 4. BEAR datasets (A: Dynamic Linked Data, B: DBpedia Live, C: Open Data portals). Three real-world corpora with deliberately different change profiles. BEAR-A: 58 weekly snapshots from the Dynamic Linked Data Observatory (650+ domains); grows 30m->66m triples (growth 101% mean per version) yet a mean 31% of data changes between versions (27% deleted, 32% added); only 376m version-oblivious triples and a small 3.5m static core, confirming high churn over a relatively small distinct-triple universe. BEAR-B: DBpedia Live changesets for the 100 most volatile resources at three granularities -- instant (21,046 versions), hour (1,299), day (89) -- showing that aggregation granularity dramatically changes dynamicity (change ratio 0.011% instant to 1.78% daily) and that triples are deleted then re-inserted shortly after (Wikipedia reverts), so the static core stays large relative to version-oblivious triples. BEAR-C: 32 weekly snapshots of the European Open Data portal (~500k triples/snapshot), very high metadata dynamicity (delta 67.6%) but near-zero growth, with complex UNION/OPTIONAL queries deliberately beyond Basic Graph Patterns. Queries are sampled to be epsilon-stable (cardinality near-constant across versions) and entirely dynamic (results always differ between consecutive versions).

“a mean of 31% of the data change between two versions and that each new version deletes a mean of 27% of the previous triples, and adds 32%”
“the average version change ratio increases from very small portions of 0.011% for instant updates to 1.8% at the daily level”
“triples which have been deleted at some point are re-inserted after a short period of time (in the case of DBpedia Live this may happen when changes made to a Wikipedia article are reverted shortly after)”

§ 5.1 Storage Space Results. Implements IC/CB/TB and hybrids on Jena-TDB and HDT (a compressed store). Headline numbers (Table 6): on BEAR-A, raw gzip is 23GB; Jena-IC needs 230GB (~10x raw), HDT-IC 48GB (~2x gzip), Jena-CB 138GB, HDT-CB 28GB. CB space savings track data dynamicity -- diff data saves 40% on BEAR-A and 15% on BEAR-C but on low-change BEAR-B the diff is only 1-3% of original, so CB shrinks dramatically (BEAR-B-day Jena-CB is 10% of Jena-IC; instant is 5%). Critical failure mode: TB does not scale with version count -- Jena-TB introduces large overhead on BEAR-B-hour (1,299 versions) and FAILED to load BEAR-B-instant (21,046 versions) entirely, because the named-graph-per-version notation is inefficient at high version counts; this is the stated motivation for hybrids. On highly dynamic BEAR-C, CB's add+delete indexes make it slightly LARGER than IC. Hybrids interpolate: small IC-gap behaves like IC in size, large gap like CB.

“Jena-TB introduces a large overhead in BEAR-B-hour, with 1,299 versions, and it even failed to load BEAR-B-instant, with 21046 versions. The main reason is that the notation for graphs and versions in TB is clearly inefficient for such large number of versions”
“in BEAR-B-day, Jena-CB only takes 10% the space of IC, and only 5% in BEAR-B-instant”
“The only exception is BEAR-C where data are so dynamic that the additional index overhead in CB (for adds and deletes) produces slightly bigger sizes than IC”

§ 5.2 Retrieval Performance. Maps each policy to each query atom. IC, TB, and CB/TB give constant retrieval time across versions; CB and IC/CB degrade as more deltas must be queried (querying a higher version means replaying more adds/deletes). HDT generally outperforms Jena under the same policy, but an IC policy in Jena can beat a CB policy in HDT (e.g. Jena-IC beats HDT-CB after version 5 in BEAR-A). For Mat and Diff, IC is best/most constant; TB in Jena performs worse than IC because it queries one potentially huge store (catastrophic on data-heavy BEAR-A) yet can beat CB when version count is large. Crucial reversal for Version queries (Ver(Q), Table 8): CB OUTPERFORMS IC because every version must be queried and CB can reuse the already-materialized previous version, while IC must query each full version independently -- Jena-CB 72ms vs Jena-IC 101ms on BEAR-A QSH, and 120ms vs 1189ms on BEAR-B-hour. Hybrid HB_TB/CB emerges as the fastest for version queries (single store query then split by version), whereas pure Jena-TB is catastrophic (56,693ms on BEAR-A, 6,473ms on BEAR-B-hour).

“the CB policy shows a clear trend that the query performance decreases if we query a higher version since more deltas have to be queried”
“an IC policy in Jena can be faster than a CB policy in HDT”
“As opposed to the previous Mat and Diff operations, Jena-CB outperforms Jena-IC in these version queries... the query of a version Vi in CB can leverage the already materialized version for the previous version Vi-1”

§ 6. Conclusions. Concludes RDF archiving is early-stage with no standard corpus or retrieval-functionality definition, which the paper's foundations (dynamicity metrics + five query atoms) and the BEAR suite (three real corpora + query testbeds + reference IC/CB/TB/hybrid implementations on Jena and HDT) are designed to fill as an extensible baseline. Results confirm both the scalability challenges and the strengths of each policy, guiding strategy selection by dataset change profile. Future work: a customizable synthetic generator preserving user-defined dynamicity characteristics while scaling to arbitrary size and version count, and extending to multiple versioned graphs in a federated scenario.

“RDF archiving is still in an early stage of research.”
“there is not a standard way of defining neither a specific data corpus for RDF archiving nor relevant retrieval functionalities”
“Results clearly confirm challenges (in terms of scalability) and strengths of current archiving approaches”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time vs committed-time; partial vs full persistence) — Gives us the canonical names for our two storage axes. IC = full persistence (every committed version materialized, branchable, O(1) read, O(n) space). CB = partial-persistence-style delta log (cheap to store, O(deltas) to reconstruct a version). TB = per-fact validity intervals = exactly our arrival-time annotation model. The paper proves these are a genuine space/time trade-off, not interchangeable. Directly warns: do NOT pick one globally. Our Rama arrival-time log is TB-shaped; our git-style minted versions are IC/CB-shaped; the paper says keep both and choose per change-profile.
  • Object-container kernel (container = identity thread, revisions = immutable values, current = state) — Def 1-2 is our model under another name: a version-annotated triple (s,p,o):[i] is a revision-labeled value; the archive A is the container; A(i)=Vi is current-revision-at-i. Supports the kernel design. The static-core / version-oblivious-triple distinction (Def 5/6) gives us a measurable identity invariant: the static core is what persists unchanged across all revisions of a container -- a natural anchor for stable identity vs churning state.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, must compose) — BEAR versions at the triple level only and the paper is candid that this is a limitation -- it explicitly focuses on low-level deltas and single-graph archiving, deferring multiple/federated versioned graphs to future work. This is a WARNING and a gap our four-level model must close: BEAR's vocabulary-dynamicity metrics (Def 7-9, separate subject/predicate/object evolution) are a partial answer at the relation-type/node-data levels, and we should adopt vdyn as a per-level composable change metric, but BEAR does not give us graph-level-vs-node-level composition -- that is our novel contribution.
  • Groupings as versioned objects with their own history (pinned / floating / effectivity-scoped binding) — The TB validity interval [start,end] per triple is exactly effectivity/condition-scoped binding: a member is bound to a grouping over a version range, not absolutely. The paper's compact 'disjoint maximal validity intervals' representation is a concrete mechanism for our floating/effectivity-scoped membership. The hybrid HB_IC/CB cost-model (re-materialize a grouping snapshot after N member deltas) is a ready mechanism for deciding when to pin (snapshot) a curated local world vs keep it floating (delta-tracked).
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; declared vs inferred change) — Version materialisation Mat(Q,Vi) and the Memento datetime-negotiation framing IS our resolve-trail-timestamp-to-state operation. Change materialisation Change(Q) -- the consecutive versions where results differ -- is precisely how we detect INFERRED change by diffing when the agent did not declare it. The paper's performance data warns us: if we store the code/trail history as pure CB (delta log, like Rama arrival order), point-in-time resolution degrades with distance from the materialized base -- so trail-to-code lookups need either IC anchors or a hybrid, not a raw delta replay.
  • Substrate choice: Rama event-sourced log as default arrival-time history — Offers a direct failure-mode warning. Rama's arrival log is CB/TB-shaped. BEAR shows: (1) pure CB version-materialisation cost grows with version distance, and (2) pure TB with named-graph-per-version CATASTROPHICALLY fails at high version counts (Jena-TB couldn't even load 21,046 versions; 56,693ms queries). If our kernel mints a version per arrival event (DBpedia-Live-instant analogue: 21k versions), we will hit exactly this wall. Mitigation the paper hands us: aggregate updates into coarser granularities (instant/hour/day) and/or insert periodic IC materialized anchors (hybrid).
  • Hard rule: inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest — BEAR operationalizes the declared/inferred split: deltas are low-level computed (inferred) facts, while version labels are asserted (declared) truth. The paper keeps both representable -- version-oblivious triples store every distinct fact with its annotation set rather than collapsing -- which supports our rule that an inferred delta is a derived view, not a new accepted truth. The static core being computable from V0 + subsequent deletions shows derived state stays reconstructable, never overwriting the committed log.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (conflicting views representable, late-bound synthesis) — Weakest mapping -- flag as inference, not something the paper addresses. BEAR assumes a single linear total order over version labels (N with a total order) and one authoritative sequence of snapshots; it has no notion of concurrent/branching versions or conflicting parallel histories. This is a GAP: our plurality requirement (and our git-style branch-from-any-version) needs a partial order / DAG of versions that BEAR explicitly does not model. Useful as a baseline to extend, but it does not support preserved disagreement out of the box.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the proof, on real data, that there is NO universal versioning storage strategy: IC (full copies), CB (delta log), and TB (per-fact validity timestamps) form a hard space/time trade-off whose winner flips by both query type and change profile -- CB beats IC for version-spanning queries (reuse the prior materialization) but loses for point reconstruction, and TB collapses entirely at high version counts (failed to load 21,046 versions). For Softland this validates keeping arrival-time history (TB/CB-shaped Rama log) and committed/minted versions (IC/CB-shaped) as two distinct layers, and it hands us a concrete escape hatch -- aggregation granularity plus periodic IC anchors (hybrids) -- for the version-count explosion our per-event kernel will otherwise hit. The unmodeled gaps (multi-graph/four-level composition and branching/plural histories) are precisely where our design must go beyond BEAR.

19. Towards Fully-fledged Archiving for RDF Datasets

Olivier Pelgrin, Luis Galarraga, Katja Hose · 2021 · Semantic Web Journal (IOS Press), submission swj2538

In one line. A survey-plus-framework paper that defines a 5-tuple RDF archiving model with separate LOCAL (per-graph) and GLOBAL (per-dataset) revision tracks, proposes RDFev metrics for high-level (entity/object) change, empirically studies DBpedia/YAGO/Wikidata evolution, and sketches a modular fully-fledged archiving engine.

Source access: full. Read the full author/publisher PDF from semantic-web-journal.net (swj2538.pdf, 626KB). WebFetch could not parse the PDF binary, so I downloaded it via WebFetch's saved file and extracted text locally with pdftotext (5234 lines). Two-column layout produced interleaved line-numbers but all prose, formulas, definitions, and tables were recoverable. Figures (3-7) are bitmap plots; I read their captions and the surrounding analysis text, not pixel values.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States that the dynamicity of RDF data motivates archiving (storing and querying previous versions). Argues the state of the art is under-developed on three counts: (i) systems are neither scalable nor easy to use, (ii) there is no standard way to query RDF archives, and (iii) solutions ignore the actual evolution patterns of real RDF data. The paper's contributions are a survey, RDFev (a framework to study RDF dynamicity), an empirical study of YAGO/DBpedia/Wikidata evolution, and a sketch of a fully-fledged solution.

“RDF archiving, i.e., the task of storing and querying previous versions of an RDF dataset”
“solutions do not exploit the evolution patterns of real RDF data”

§ 1. Introduction. Frames RDF archiving as storing/querying the entire edition history of a dataset, useful both for producers (fine-grained version control, debugging, evolution study) and consumers (analytics, mining correction patterns, trend analysis). Distinguishes two user camps that drive different designs: maintainer-facing Git-like version control systems (R&Wbase, R43ples, Quit Store) versus consumer-facing time-aware querying (RDF-TX). Attributes the absence of a fully-fledged solution to (i) engine limitations handling metadata, (ii) no query standard, (iii) disregard for real evolution. Lists five contributions: RDFev framework, the DBpedia/YAGO/Wikidata study, the survey, an Ostrich evaluation, and the sketch.

“Storing and querying the entire edition history of an RDF dataset, a task we call RDF archiving”
“RDF archives can serve as a backend for fine-grained version control in collaborative projects”

§ 2. Preliminaries (data model: graphs, graph archives, dataset archives, the 5-tuple). Builds the formal model that is the load-bearing part for our purposes. A graph G is a set of triples; an archive A = {G_s,...,G_{s+n-1}} is an ordered set of revisions where each non-initial revision G_i is obtained by applying a changeset u_i = (added/deleted triples, disjoint) to G_{i-1}, i.e. G_i = (G_{i-1} u+) \ u-. A graph archive is equivalently modeled as 4-tuples where rho is the RDF identifier of a revision, rv(rho) maps it to a natural number, and ts(rho) is its commit time. Crucially the paper then separates LOCAL and GLOBAL history: a dataset is a set of named graphs G^k; a dataset update U_j = {u_hat_j, u^0_j, u^1_j,...} bundles a per-graph changeset PLUS a special GRAPH changeset u_hat (added/deleted graph LABELS). A master graph G_M holds metadata about graphs. The dataset archive is then modeled as 5-tuples : rho identifies the LOCAL revision of the triple within its graph (label g=l(rho)), and zeta identifies the GLOBAL dataset revision rv(zeta), with ts(zeta) its timestamp. Adding a non-empty graph yields TWO revisions (one to create, one to populate); deletion is symmetric. Section 2.5 defines the five canonical archive query types: Version Materialization (VM), Delta Materialization (DM), Version (V), Cross-Version (CV), Cross-Delta (CD).

“we can model a dataset archive as a set of 5-tuples ”
“rho in I is the RDF identifier of the local revision of the triple in an RDF graph with label g = l(rho)... zeta in I identifies a (global) dataset revision j = rv(zeta)”
“the addition of a non-empty graph to a dataset archive generates two revisions: one for creating the graph, and one for populating it”
“we also define the function ts ... that associates a revision identifier rho to its commit time”

§ 3. RDFev: Framework for the Evolution of RDF Data. Proposes RDFev, a set of metrics plus a C++/Python tool (RocksDB-backed) that ingests successive N-triples dumps in chronological order and computes change metrics per consecutive revision pair. Splits metrics into LOW-LEVEL (triple-level: change ratio delta_ij, insertion/deletion ratios, vocabulary dynamicity vdyn over the term set Upsilon, growth ratio) and HIGH-LEVEL (entity- and value-level). The key argument is that triple-counting (as in BEAR) is insufficient for maintainers who need the SEMANTICS of a change. High-level metrics: entity changes ec (an entity = a subject; counts added/deleted subjects), triple-to-entity-change score (avg triples per entity change), object updates (a delete paired with an add , o != o'), and orphan object additions/deletions (triples NOT consumed by any higher-level change). Once a triple is assigned to a high-level change it is 'consumed' and cannot be reused. All metrics are domain-agnostic.

“metrics for low-level changes, and metrics for high-level changes”
“A high-level change confers semantics to a changeset”
“An object update in a changeset u_ij is defined by the deletion of a triple and the addition of a triple with o != o'”
“Once a triple in a changeset has been assigned to a high-level change, the triple is consumed and cannot be assigned to any other high-level change”

§ 4. Evolution Analysis of RDF Datasets. Applies RDFev to YAGO, DBpedia, Wikidata, treating each release as a revision. Finds two distinct evolution regimes. YAGO and DBpedia have a MAJOR/MINOR release cycle: major releases (YAGO 3.0.0 doubled the KB) carry order-of-magnitude larger changes and may not increase size, while minor releases focus on quality/correction with ~10x fewer changes. DBpedia's 2015-04 release is an inflexion point where deletion rate exceeds insertion rate (a design redesign). Wikidata, by contrast, shows a stable release cycle with low, near-constant change. High-level findings: in YAGO/DBpedia object UPDATES dominate (4.8x and 1.8x more frequent than orphan add/delete) meaning edits mostly revise existing values; in Wikidata orphan object additions dominate (19x more common than orphan deletions). The explicit design lesson: an effective archiver must ADAPT to at least these two evolution patterns.

“we have identified two patterns of evolution”
“Major releases are characterized by a large number of updates in the knowledge base and may not necessarily increase its size”
“object updates are 4.8 and 1.8 times more frequent than orphan additions and deletions”
“an effective solution for large-scale RDF archiving should be able to adapt to at least these two patterns of evolution”

§ 5. Survey of RDF Archiving Solutions. Taxonomizes systems along storage paradigm, data model, BGP support, query types, branch/tag, multi-graph, concurrency, source availability (Table 4). Four storage paradigms: Independent Copies (IC, full snapshot per revision, fast VM/CV, disk-prohibitive), Change-Based (CB, snapshots + delta chains, storage-efficient, costly VM/CV on long chains), Timestamp-Based (TB, per-triple time annotations + bit/visibility maps), and Fragment-Based (FB, Quit Store). Discusses SemVersion (IC, 5-tuples with per-graph-local revisions, no global graph-addition tracking), R&WBase/R43ples/Quit Store (Git-like, PROV-O metadata; Quit Store uniquely tracks BOTH local and global history but is Git-bound and memory-heavy), RDF-TX (multiversion B-tree, SPARQL-T), v-RDFCSA (suffix-array, per-triple revision bit-sequence), and Ostrich (hybrid IC+CB+TB; HDT snapshots + redundant delta chains stored against the LATEST snapshot, quads , single snapshot + single delta chain only). Section 5.2 surveys query languages: AnQL (quad patterns), T-SPARQL (interval + timestamp temporal operators), SPARQ-LTL (temporal-logic PAST/FUTURE), RDF/SPARQL. Section 5.3 covers benchmarks BEAR and EvoGen.

“three main storage paradigms ... namely independent copies (IC), change-based (CB), and timestamp-based (TB)”
“Quit Store [4] is the only available solution that supports both archiving of the local and joint (global) history of multiple RDF graphs”
“each revision in the chain is stored as a changeset containing the changes w.r.t. the latest snapshot - and not the previous revision”

§ 6. Evaluation of Related Work (Ostrich). Of all surveyed systems, ONLY Ostrich could ingest the experimental datasets; Quit Store crashed/MemoryError, R43ples could not ingest one DBpedia revision in four days, R&WBase choked on millions of SPARQL update statements, x-RDF-3X would not compile. Ostrich achieves strong compression (87% YAGO, 84% DBpedia, 94% Wikidata via HDT snapshots + delta compression). But its ingestion time grows LINEARLY with history length because it stores revision i as changeset u_{0,i} against revision 0, so changesets only grow: ~22h for DBpedia rev 9 vs ~14h for the larger rev 5. Spearman analysis shows runtime correlates most with delta-chain length, changeset-size variance, and avg deleted triples. Conclusion: massive deletions and long histories degrade Ostrich; the single-snapshot/single-delta-chain design does not scale to long histories.

“only Ostrich can store the release history of very large RDF datasets”
“Ostrich stores the i-th revision of an archive as a changeset of the form u_{0,i} ... can only grow in size. This fact makes it unsuitable for very long histories”
“the most correlated features are the length of the delta chain, the standard deviation of the changeset size, and the average number of deleted triples”

§ 7. Towards Fully-fledged RDF Archiving (the design sketch). The prescriptive core. Derives design lessons. (a) Temporal domain-specific metadata vs revision metadata must be SEPARATED: revision metadata (commit time, revision numbers, branch/tag) is IMMUTABLE and is attached to the revision identifiers rho/zeta, whereas mutable domain-specific time (e.g. validity of a marriage) is attached to graph labels g=l(rho) and can be edited by a later changeset on the master graph. (b) Global AND local history are both vital. (c) Provenance (PROV-O) is workflow history, separate from revision metadata, linkable to either revision ids or graph labels, with a hybrid default-plus-override scheme. (d) Concurrency should be a MODULAR middleware layer separated from the storage backend, so collaborative version control becomes an APPLICATION of archiving rather than the architecture. (e) Storage should hybridize IC/CB/FB/TB and be ADAPTIVE: use RDFev metrics to trigger snapshot creation on large deletions, divide each graph's history into interval BLOCK FILES (snapshot + its interval's changesets), and resize dictionary encodings on vocabulary spikes. (f) Internal serialization of 5-tuples: reification (compatible but quintuples query patterns), singleton properties (encodes rho in predicate, still needs second reification for zeta), named graphs, HDTQ, or binary matrices (triples x revisions, good compression from contiguous runs). RDF / SPARQL (Turtle) is endorsed as the publication/query format because it natively nests multiple metadata levels; the authors propose extending SPARQL with REVISION and DELTA syntactic sugar.

“revision metadata is immutable and should therefore be logically placed at a different level”
“we propose a modular design that separates the concurrency layer from the storage backend”
“collaborative version control systems for RDF become an application of fully-fledged RDF archiving”
“we envision an adaptive data-oriented system that relies on the metrics proposed in Section 3 and adjusts its parameters according to the archive's evolution”

§ 8. Conclusions. Recaps: RDF archiving matters for maintainers and consumers; evolution patterns must drive design; RDFev characterizes three real KBs and exposes design shifts; only Ostrich scales to large datasets but not to long histories and ignores evolution patterns; the field lacks a query standard, for which SPARQL* plus syntactic sugar is the promising path. Reaffirms that collaborative version control is itself an application of archiving rather than the whole of it.

“only a few solutions are available for download and use, and ... only Ostrich can store the release history of very large RDF datasets”
“its design still does not scale to long histories and does not exploit the data evolution patterns”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer: arrival-time history (Rama default, partial persistence) vs explicit committed/minted versions (full persistence) -- 'two distinct times' — Gives our two-times distinction a precise, externally-validated name and structure. The paper's rho = LOCAL revision identifier and zeta = GLOBAL dataset revision are exactly committed/minted versions, and ts(rho)/ts(zeta) are commit timestamps -- separate from arrival order. It even warns of the failure mode where the two diverge: x-RDF-3X 'stores temporal metadata in special indexes that optimize for concurrent updates at the expense of temporal consistency, i.e., revision numbers may not always be in concordance with the timestamps.' That is precisely the arrival-time-vs-commit-time hazard our layer must keep coherent. SUPPORTS + names the mechanism.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated grouping / local world has its OWN version history, partly independent of its members (pinned/floating member binding) — This is the paper's central member-vs-group structure. A graph G^k has its own LOCAL revision track (rho); the dataset/grouping has a GLOBAL revision track (zeta) advanced by a dataset update U_j that bundles per-member changesets PLUS a graph changeset u_hat over member LABELS (membership add/delete). So the group version advances both when members change AND when membership itself changes -- and crucially, 'u^1_1 leaves G1 untouched' shows a global revision can advance while a member's local revision does not. That IS partly-independent group-vs-member history. The master graph G_M holding membership metadata maps onto our grouping's own metadata record. SUPPORTS + gives a concrete mechanism for pinned vs floating binding (the global revision pins a specific tuple of local revisions).
  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread; REVISIONS = immutable values; current-revision = state — Direct structural analogue at the triple/graph level. The graph label g (a stable IRI in I) is the CONTAINER identity that persists across revisions; each G_i is an immutable revision value; current-revision is the latest in the ordered set. The paper's hard insistence that 'revision metadata is immutable and should therefore be logically placed at a different level' echoes our 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' discipline -- immutable committed facts live on one track, mutable/derived annotations on another. SUPPORTS the container/revision split and reinforces the immutability invariant.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data) that must COMPOSE — PARTIALLY SUPPORTS but also WARNS. The paper genuinely composes TWO levels (member-graph local revision composed under dataset global revision) and shows the composition rule (U_j = graph-changeset + per-graph changesets, applied in a defined order). But it operates at triple granularity, not a typed node/relation-type/node-data hierarchy -- 'an entity is a subject for the sake of this analysis,' a flat collapse. So it validates that multi-level versioning must compose and gives the composition operator, but it is a cautionary data point that without typed levels you lose the node-data vs relation-type distinction we want. The 'object update' high-level change (a node-data edit) vs 'orphan addition' (structural add) is the closest the paper gets to separating node-data from structure.
  • Trail-to-code join: DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing) — Names and motivates our declared-vs-inferred split, and supplies RDFev as a concrete inference engine. Low-level changes are RAW diffs (inferred). High-level changes (entity change, object update, orphan add/delete) are the SEMANTIC reconstruction of intent from a diff -- 'A high-level change confers semantics to a changeset.' That is exactly the inference side of our join: given before/after states, reconstruct what operation the author intended. The 'consumed triple' rule (once a delete+add pair is bound to an object-update it cannot be reused) is a reusable disambiguation algorithm for our diff-to-edit-op reconstruction. Critically, the paper shows inference is LOSSY/ambiguous (orphans are leftovers), which is exactly why we prefer the agent's DECLARED edit operation when available. SUPPORTS + offers a mechanism + shows the failure mode of inference-only.
  • The code-identity question: file vs path vs FQN vs normalized-AST vs git-hash; atomic unit = file or function — Offers a transferable answer by analogy: identity is the STABLE LABEL (the IRI/graph-label that threads across revisions), NOT the content. SemVersion's failure is instructive -- it used per-graph-LOCAL revision numbers and thereby 'makes it difficult to track the addition or deletion of named graphs in the history of the dataset.' Translation for us: if a code unit's identity is tied only to a content-derived key (git hash) or a position (path), you cannot track rename/move/delete as identity-preserving events -- you need a stable label decoupled from both content and position, with membership (path) recorded as MUTABLE metadata on a separate track. WARNS against content-hash-as-identity for tracking history; SUPPORTS a stable-label container with path/content as revisable attributes.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced log as truth, derived state as projection; adaptive snapshotting — OFFERS A MECHANISM and a tuning policy. The CB paradigm (snapshots + delta chains) is precisely event-sourcing with periodic materialization. The paper's hardest empirical lesson maps onto our snapshot strategy: Ostrich grew ingestion time LINEARLY with history because it diffed every revision against revision 0 -- a direct warning that an append-only log without periodic re-snapshotting degrades. Their adaptive remedy (use RDFev metrics, e.g. a large-deletion spike, to TRIGGER a fresh snapshot / new block file) is a ready-made policy for when Rama should materialize a checkpoint PState vs keep replaying the log. SUPPORTS event-sourcing + WARNS about unbounded delta chains + gives an adaptive trigger mechanism.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; concurrency and late-bound synthesis — Modestly supports via the modular-concurrency lesson. The paper separates the concurrency/conflict layer as a MIDDLEWARE distinct from storage, and explicitly contrasts manual conflict resolution (R&WBase: users resolve) vs automatic. That maps to our late-bound-synthesis stance: conflicts are not resolved at the storage/ingest layer; they are surfaced to a separable layer where resolution can be deferred or human-arbitrated. Branching/tagging as first-class (which most surveyed systems LACK, a noted gap) is the substrate our preserved-disagreement branches need. SUPPORTS keeping conflict resolution out of the truth-storage layer; the absence of good branching support across systems is a WARNING about how immature this is.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution is the two-track revision identity model: a triple is a 5-tuple where rho is the member's LOCAL revision and zeta is the grouping's GLOBAL revision, advanced by an update that bundles per-member changesets with an explicit membership changeset -- and where IMMUTABLE revision metadata is deliberately placed on a different logical track from MUTABLE domain data. This is a directly portable blueprint for our 'groupings as versioned objects' and 'two times' layers: it shows precisely how a curated local world can carry its own version history partly independent of its members, how membership changes are themselves versioned events, and (via the Ostrich finding that diff-against-origin degrades linearly) why our Rama-backed archive needs adaptive, metric-triggered snapshots rather than an unbounded delta chain.

20. Querying the Edit History of Wikidata

Thomas Pellissier Tanon, Fabian Suchanek · 2019 · ESWC 2019 (Extended Semantic Web Conference) — demo/poster paper

In one line. A SPARQL endpoint over Wikidata's full 700M-revision edit history that stores each triple once with validity-range annotations, exposing per-revision diffs AND global-state-after-revision via plain SPARQL 1.1 named graphs over a RocksDB index.

Source access: full. Read the complete author-hosted PDF at thomas.pellissier-tanon.fr/papers/2019-ESWC-wikidatahistory.pdf. WebFetch returned raw PDF bytes, so I converted it locally with pdftotext -layout and read the full extracted text (all 5 sections + references). It is a short ESWC demo paper (~5 pages); nothing was gated.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States the problem and the contribution in five sentences: Wikidata has accumulated millions of contributions over 7 years, and the paper proposes a system exposing this through a SPARQL endpoint. The key design claim is that they index not only the diffs of each revision but also the GLOBAL STATE of the Wikidata graph after any given revision — this dual capability is the paper's whole point. They motivate it with concrete analytic use-cases that the endpoint enables: tracing human vs. automated (bot) contributions, areas of vandalism, big schema changes, and the adoption of different values for the 'gender' property over time. The deliverable is framed as standard SPARQL 1.1 query access to history, not a new query language.

“We index not just the diffs done by a revision, but also the global state of Wikidata graph after any given revision.”
“This allows users to answer complex SPARQL 1.1 queries on the Wikidata history”

§ 1 Introduction. Sets scale and gap. Wikidata started 2012; as of July 2018 it had 5B statements about 50M entities, 700M+ revisions by 56k contributors. The official SPARQL endpoint (query.wikidata.org) serves ONLY the latest version and is blind to history, so even 'how many people were in Wikidata two years ago?' is unanswerable. The only existing route to full history is a 250GB compressed XML dump of per-revision JSON blobs — hard to manipulate and index. Two naive strategies are rejected up front: materializing one RDF graph per revision is 'prohibitively expensive' (700M revisions × billions of triples), while indexing only inter-revision differences 'loses the global state at each revision.' The contribution is a system that smartly indexes revisions to deliver three query capabilities: (1) retrieve the diff (added/removed triples) for any revision; (2) evaluate any triple pattern against the global state after a given revision; (3) retrieve the revisions that added/removed triples matching a pattern. These answer questions like cities-over-time, entities-modified-exclusively-by-bots, and gender-values-over-time — all in standard SPARQL with no language additions.

“this endpoint emits only the latest version of the data, and is blind to the edit history”
“It would be prohibitively expensive to create one RDF graph for each of the 700 million revisions”
“If one indexes only the differences between the revisions, one loses the global state at each revision.”
“without any additions to the language”

§ 2 Related Work. Surveys versioned-RDF approaches and argues each fails at Wikidata's scale or update model. Quad stores and archives storing each triple annotated per-revision would require storing a triple once for every revision it appears in — 'quadrillions of triples' for Wikidata. tgrin timestamps triples but has no time-range support and no available implementation. R&Wbase ('git for triples') is a SPARQL 1.1 wrapper giving git-like RDF versioning but stores only diffs, so it cannot efficiently query full graph state at a point in time. x-RDF-3X annotates each triple with validity ranges (add/remove timestamps) permitting any-state queries and consistency features, but by design CANNOT load data with already-known timestamps/version IDs — disqualifying for ingesting Wikidata's known history. v-RDFCSA stores versioned triples efficiently but allows no subsequent revision additions and no revision ranges. OSTRICH stores version-annotated triples via an HDT base version plus a changeset, but assumes a non-empty base version; with Wikidata's empty base it would have to store full global state per revision. The recurring distinction is theory vs. a working implementation that survives Wikidata's size and append-with-known-IDs requirement.

“each triple would have to be stored once for every revision in which it appears ... this would amount to quadrillions of triples”
“R&Wbase ... stores only the diff between different revisions. Therefore, it does not allow efficient querying of the full graph state at a given point of time.”
“x-RDF-3X ... annotates triples with validity ranges ... However, by design, it does not allow loading data with already known timestamps or version IDs.”
“This system is not tailored to the case of Wikidata, where the base version is empty.”

§ 3 System Overview (data model + indexes + evaluation). The technical core. The DATA MODEL uses four named-graph families, all expressible in vanilla SPARQL 1.1: a Global State Graph per revision (full state of Wikidata after that revision is saved), an Addition Graph per revision (triples added by it), a Deletion Graph per revision (triples removed), and a single Default Graph holding revision METADATA — author, timestamp, modified entity id, the PREVIOUS REVISION OF THE SAME ENTITY (per-entity chain), the PREVIOUS REVISION IN WIKIDATA (global chain), and the IRIs of the addition/deletion/global-state graphs. They reuse the official Wikidata dump schema. Because materializing those graphs would take exabytes, they do NOT load into a triple store; they build custom indexes on RocksDB ('a scalable key value store, optimized for a mixed workload between query and edits,' chosen to anticipate future live updates). Four index types: (a) Dictionary indexes mapping strings↔integer ids; (b) Content indexes — each triple (s,p,o) stored in three permutation indexes keyed spo/pos/osp, with VALUE = a set of revision ranges [start, end[ where start is the revision that introduced the triple and end the revision that removed it (or +∞ if never removed) — this validity-range encoding stores each triple ONCE; (c) Revision indexes giving the (small) set of triples added/removed by a given revision; (d) Meta indexes for per-revision author, previous revision, etc. Querying the global state = prefix-search a content index then FILTER results by revision range. This is cheap because of near-monotone growth: of 4931M triples that ever existed (as of July 1 2018), only 475M were ever removed, so the largest revision holds ~90% of all triples that ever existed. The QUAD-PATTERN EVALUATOR dispatches: metadata patterns → meta indexes; content patterns on an addition/deletion graph → revision indexes; on a global-state graph or unbound graph → longest-prefix content-index search, then either range-filter (global state) or reconstruct quads by mapping each triple's range to its addition/deletion graph ids (notably NOT returning global-state-graph ids, since one triple pattern could match hundreds of millions of state graphs). This single-quad evaluator is plugged into Eclipse RDF4J, which executes arbitrary SPARQL 1.1 via repeated evaluator calls and applies join reordering with cardinality estimates exploiting structure (one author per revision; few triples per entity).

“The default graph contains triples that encode metadata about each revision: the revision author, the revision timestamp, the id of the modified entity, the previous revision of the same entity, the previous revision in Wikidata”
“Each range is of the form [start, end[, where start is the id of the revision that introduced the triple, and end is the id of the revision that removed it (or +∞ if the triple has not been removed).”
“out of the 4931M triples that have existed in the Wikidata history as of July 1st 2018, only 475M have been removed. Therefore, the largest revision contains 90% of the total number of Wikidata triples that ever existed.”
“we use RocksDB ... a scalable key value store, optimized for a mixed workload between query and edits. This choice anticipates the possibility of live updates from Wikidata in the future.”

§ 3 (deployment numbers). Reports the actual deployed demo instance. Because of storage constraints on the Wikimedia Foundation server, only the direct-claim relations (wdt:) are loaded, covering Wikidata's creation through July 1st 2018. The instance stores 700M+ revisions and 390M content triples about 49M items. The RocksDB indexes occupy 64GB on disk using RocksDB gzip compression after compaction. These numbers are the paper's main empirical evidence that validity-range + permutation-index storage on a KV store makes full edit history tractable — full 5B-statement history is implied to fit the same scheme but was trimmed only for the public demo's disk budget.

“only the direct claim relations are loaded in our demo endpoint”
“stores more than 700M revisions and 390M content triples about 49M items”
“The RocksDB indexes are using 64Go on disk with the RocksDB gzip compression system, after having compacted the database.”

§ 4 Demonstration Setting. Shows the model in action via a worked SPARQL query (Listing 1): the 10 most frequent replacements of the 'gender' (wdt:P21) value. The query pattern is the join the model enables — GRAPH ?addGraph matches added P21 triples, ?revision hist:additions/hist:deletions links to the corresponding deletion graph, and GRAPH ?delGraph matches the same subject's deleted P21 value, then GROUP BY/ORDER BY/LIMIT (plain SPARQL 1.1) compute the ranking. Insights surfaced: users correct erroneous gender values (e.g. 'man' → 'male'); a variant quantifies the gender gap over time (absolute man/woman difference grows but the relative difference shrinks); another variant traces increasing presence of non-traditional sex/gender values like 'trans-male' or 'non-binary.' The point is that historical analytics that previously required custom dump-processing pipelines become one declarative query against added/deleted/global graphs.

“GRAPH ?addGraph { ?s wdt:P21 ?addedGender } ?revision hist:additions ?addGraph ; hist:deletions ?delGraph .”
“users correct erroneous gender values (such as “man”) by their intended variants (“male”)”
“while the absolute difference between the number of men and women in Wikidata keeps increasing over time, the relative difference actually decreases”

§ 5 Conclusion. Restates that the system efficiently indexes the entire Wikidata edit history and answers arbitrary SPARQL 1.1 queries over both revision diffs and global state after each revision, with the live endpoint at wdhqs.wmflabs.org. Future work is to update the store live from the Wikidata edit stream (the same motivation that drove the RocksDB choice). Acknowledges grant ANR-16-CE23-0007-01 ('DICOS').

“Our system allows queries on both the revision diffs and the global state of Wikidata after each revision.”
“we plan to update the content of our system live based on the Wikidata edit stream.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions; partial vs full persistence) — OFFERS A MECHANISM. The content-index entry stores triples once, each annotated with a set of revision ranges [start, end[ — start = revision that introduced it, end = revision that removed it (or +infinity). This is exactly partial persistence done right: one immutable store, time-travel by range-filtering, not by snapshotting. It directly validates our refusal to materialize one graph per version. But note their 'revision' is a single linear ID assigned per edit (arrival/commit fused), so it does NOT split when-it-arrived from when-it-was-committed the way we want — a warning that a single revision counter collapses our two distinct times.
  • Object-container kernel (stable container = identity thread; revisions = immutable values; current = latest) — SUPPORTS + NAMES IT. Each Wikidata entity is a container; the paper threads it via 'the previous revision of the same entity' stored in the default/meta graph — a per-entity revision chain — AND 'the previous revision in Wikidata' — a global revision chain. This is precisely our braided identity thread (per-container chain) co-existing with a global arrival log. Their current-revision = 'the largest revision contains 90% of the total number of Wikidata triples that ever existed' confirms current-state is just the open-ended range subset, computed not stored.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, must compose) — PARTIAL SUPPORT + GAP. They version at the triple grain via validity ranges and expose three composable named-graph views (global-state, addition, deletion) plus a default metadata graph — graph-level and data-level versioning that compose in plain SPARQL 1.1 with no language extension. But they do NOT separately version node identity, relation-TYPE/schema, or grouping objects; schema changes appear only as ordinary triple churn ('the big schema changes' are observed, not modeled). Warns us that triple-grain ranges alone don't give the relation-type level — we must add it.
  • Substrate choices: Rama as event-sourced store with RocksDB-style indexes — STRONG CONVERGENT EVIDENCE. They explicitly reject loading version-annotated quads into a triple store ('would occupy exabytes') and instead build on RocksDB — 'a scalable key value store, optimized for a mixed workload between query and edits' — with dictionary, content (spo/pos/osp permutation), revision, and meta indexes. Rama's PStates are exactly this: hand-rolled, append-friendly, permutation-style indexes over an event log. The whole 390M-triples-in-64GB result is an existence proof that our substrate shape scales to full edit history.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change vs INFERRED change; resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment — OFFERS A MECHANISM. Their query capability #2 ('Evaluate any triple pattern against the global state of Wikidata after a given revision') is the join we need: given a revision (timestamp/version), reconstruct the full state at that moment by range-filtering. Capability #1/#3 (retrieve diff for a revision; retrieve revisions that added/removed a pattern) gives both directions of the trail join. Crucially their diffs are DECLARED — the addition/deletion graphs are stored as recorded by each edit, not reconstructed by diffing snapshots — matching our 'declared change' preference over inferred diffing.
  • Near-monotone growth / cost model for keeping all history — VALIDATES FEASIBILITY. Of 4931M triples that ever existed, only 475M were ever removed; the open (un-removed) set is ~90% of all triples. Growth is near-monotone, so the validity-range representation has almost no end-bounded rows and the 'current state' is cheap. This quantifies why keeping full history costs little more than keeping current state — directly relevant to our claim that Rama arrival-time history is affordable by default.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality and 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — WEAK / CAUTIONARY. The paper has NO conflict model — revisions are a single linear authoritative chain, last-write-wins, vandalism is queryable-after-the-fact but not held as preserved disagreement. It treats every recorded edit as truth (it ingests Wikidata's declared diffs verbatim, which is consistent with our 'declared not inferred' rule). But it offers nothing for late-bound synthesis of conflicting views — a gap we must fill ourselves; their model is the single-authority case of ours.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution: validity-range triple storage as the right shape for keeping full edit history. Store every triple ONCE, annotated with a set of [start, end[ revision ranges (end = +∞ if still live); the current state is just the open-ranged subset, and any past state is a range-filter — no per-version materialization.
