Combined with their dual per-entity + global revision chains (braided identity threads in a metadata graph) and the near-monotone-growth observation (only ~10% of triples ever get removed, so history costs barely more than current state), this is a direct, deployed existence proof for our object-container kernel + arrival-time-versioning-as-its-own-layer on a RocksDB-style KV substrate — exactly Rama's PState shape. The cautions: their single linear revision counter fuses arrival-time and commit-time (we want them split), and they model no conflict/plurality and no relation-TYPE/schema-level versioning — gaps our four-level + preserved-disagreement layers must add.

What we can gather — Angle 4

This is the angle that stress-tests the hardest thing we want — versioning at four composing levels — and it comes back with both the blueprint and the walls.

The two-track theorem is the central result. The RDF-archiving framework formalizes a triple as a 5-tuple ⟨s,p,o, local-revision, global-revision⟩ and proves that systems with only member-local revisions "cannot track the addition or deletion" of members in the group's history — member-level versioning alone cannot reconstruct group-level history. Wikidata's history system implements the working version: each revision records both "previous revision of the same entity" and "previous revision globally" — per-entity chains braided inside a global chain. The practical consequence for us is strong: the group/configuration track must exist from the start; retrofitting it is provably lossy — which is a direct argument for putting the configuration noun into the kernel before the graph ingestor forces it.

Validity-range storage is the right shape, and it's cheap. Wikidata stores each triple once, annotated with [start, end) revision ranges (end = ∞ if still live); current state is just the open-ended subset, any past state a range filter — no per-version materialization. And because knowledge is near-monotone (only ~10% of Wikidata triples were ever removed), keeping full history costs barely more than keeping current state — encouraging for trails and discourse graphs, which mostly accrete.

The version-count wall is real and bounds our minting policy. The BEAR benchmark shows no universal storage strategy (snapshots/IC, deltas/CB, timestamps/TB each win on different change profiles), and TB failed to load 21,046 versions while queries timed out. If we mint a version per arrival event we hit this wall — so commit-grain, not save-grain, with periodic snapshot anchors triggered adaptively by change metrics. OSTRICH adds the same warning (ingestion time grows with history because it aggregates deltas eagerly) and the gift to take: the VM / DM / VQ query vocabulary (state-at-version, change-between-versions, versions-where-valid) — adopt it as the kernel's read API; trail-to-code is VM plus an exactness flag.

TerminusDB is the production proof and the boundary. It runs our exact two-family architecture — append-only immutable delta layers plus a separate commit graph — at ~13 bytes/triple, with deletion recorded as a fact, not an erasure. But it unifies all four levels into one mechanism (a schema-type change and a data change are both just commits), and pins whole branches with no floating/effectivity binding. So it validates the substrate and marks the two places we must go further: keeping the four levels separately versioned yet composable, and the one level nobody in this angle versions — relation-TYPE / schema evolution — which is genuinely our own contribution.

Angle 5 — Composite & Assembly Versioning: The Mature, Solved Part

21. Towards a Unified Framework for Version Modeling

Randy H. Katz · 1988 · UC Berkeley Computer Science Division Technical Report UCB/CSD-88-484 (later published as 'Toward a Unified Framework for Version Modeling in Engineering Databases,' ACM Computing Surveys 22(4), 1990, pp. 375-408)

In one line. A survey that distills ~15 CAD/engineering version-management proposals down to a small set of underlying mechanisms — version histories, configurations, equivalences, dynamic binding, change propagation, workspaces — and proposes a unified terminology rather than yet another model.

Source access: full. ACM DOI (dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/98163.98172) returned HTTP 403. Read the OPEN Berkeley tech-report copy CSD-88-484 instead: a 45-page scanned-image PDF at www2.eecs.berkeley.edu/Pubs/TechRpts/1988/Archive/CSD-88-484.pdf (linked from Katz's Berkeley tech-reports page). The PDF has no text layer (CCITT scan, no tesseract available), so I read all 45 pages as rendered page images via the Read tool. This is the pre-publication TR of the 1990 ACM Computing Surveys article; section structure and figures match.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract & Key Words. Frames the paper as a SURVEY of one aspect of CAD database support: version modeling — 'how best to describe the structure of a complex design artifact as it evolves across its multiple representations and over time.' Explicitly states it is NOT proposing another model but seeking 'a common terminology and a common collection of mechanisms that should underlie all version models.' Notes an operational model is also needed to describe how artifact descriptions are created and modified. The stated remaining challenge is to build a single tailorable framework from these mechanisms.

“it is our purpose not merely to describe such proposals but to attempt to unify them”
“a common terminology and a common collection of mechanisms that should underlie all version models”
“how best to describe the structure of a complex design artifact as it evolves across its multiple representations and over time”

§ 1. Introduction. Motivates CAD as a non-traditional database domain that doesn't fit tables/serializable-transaction models — it needs complex connected data, navigation, and long-duration interactive access. Identifies five CAD characteristics, the key ones being that it is HISTORY-ORIENTED ('current data is not overwritten with new data, but rather maintained as historical data') and handles multi-faceted, multi-representational, hierarchical aggregates. Crucially separates a CAD data model into two components: a VERSION MODEL ('primitives for organizing data across time') and a DESIGN TRANSACTION MODEL ('how such data can undergo change in a consistent manner'); the paper concentrates on the former. Distinguishes version-oriented from time-oriented databases: 'Versions represent a significant, semantically meaningful change. Thus, it is implicit in a version database that not every update need result in the construction of a new version.' States the goal of producing a CATALOG OF FEATURES every version model must support plus the mechanisms to implement them, separating mechanisms from policies ('knobs') to enable a true tailorable framework. Previews the roadmap: Sec 2 terms/requirements, Sec 3 a detailed model (the Version Server), Sec 4 a survey, Sec 5 the extracted mechanisms/unification, Sec 6 conclusions.

“the CAD environment is history oriented: current data is not overwritten with new data, but rather maintained as historical data”
“A data model for computer-aided design must consist of two components: a version model... and a design transaction model”
“Versions represent a significant, semantically meaningful change... not every update need result in the construction of a new version”
“Only once the appropriate 'knobs' are made visible and available to the user, with mechanisms clearly distinguished from policies, will it be possible to achieve a true version framework”

§ 2. Definition of Terms and Basic Requirements. Lays out a vocabulary because the field 'has not yet evolved a consistent terminology.' Key definitions: a DESIGN OBJECT is a named aggregate of design primitives ('better to think of them as units of the design that can be given a name, such as Arithmetic Logic Unit') — explicitly NOT necessarily a single file. A VERSION is 'a semantically meaningful snapshot of a design object at a point in time,' a descendant of existing versions and a potential ancestor of others. A CONFIGURATION is 'a binding between a version of a composite object and the versions of its components.' WORKSPACES are named repositories that are units of sharing and access control. Figure 2.1 presents a leveled architecture splitting a Design Management System (History Manager, Workspace Manager, Object Manager, Physical Database) from an independent Process Management System (Workflow, Event Log) — anticipating event-sourced, log-backed design. Section 2.2 enumerates 8 general requirements (named aggregation, hierarchical composition, separation of contents from interfaces, configuration construct/manipulate, multi-version support, version SELECTION with a default mechanism, instance-vs-definition reuse, and a HISTORY MECHANISM to record design decisions), then splits them into MODELING requirements (hierarchy across representations; versions a.k.a. alternatives/revisions/ECOs; configurations buildable STATICALLY 'all component references bound at creation time' AND DYNAMICALLY 'references bound at the time of access,' the latter called PARAMETRIZED VERSIONS; identifying equivalent objects across representations; object-oriented ADT/inheritance concepts) and DESIGN TRANSACTION requirements (loose vs tight coupling via check-in/out and workspaces; CONVERSATIONAL transactions that 'hold locks through crashes' but provide no recovery; cooperative-work support via change notification/propagation and workflow).

“a design object is a useful module of the design”
“A version is a semantically meaningful snapshot of a design object at a point in time”
“we define a configuration as a binding between a version of a composite object and the versions of its components”
“Configurations should be able to be constructed both statically (all component references bound at creation time) and dynamically (references bound at the time of access). Dynamic configurations are also known as parametrized versions”

§ 3. Detailed Presentation of a Version Model (the Version Server). Presents the author's own Version Server data model as a representative exemplar (not claimed ultimate), guided by the pragmatic constraint that it be implementable 'on top of existing design files... to avoid rewriting any existing design applications.' PHILOSOPHY (3.2.1): the model is E-R-like but with distinguished relationships; it separates STRUCTURAL objects (added by the model to organize the flat space of files, like directories) from REPRESENTATIONAL objects (the actual tool files) — 'It is a key aspect of our organization that structural information be separated from representational information.' Three structural relationships organize everything: COMPONENT HIERARCHIES / is-a-part-of (3.2.2) — aggregation, generalizable from trees to DAGs so a component can be shared by multiple composites; VERSION HISTORIES / is-a-kind-of + is-derived-from (3.2.3) — objects denoted name[version#].type, with is-derived-from recording ancestry because the naming scheme alone cannot ('it is not possible to determine from this naming scheme which version is descended from which'); ALTERNATIVES are parallel versions not on the same root-path, DERIVATIVES are on the same path; the generic object (e.g. ALU.Layout) is a structural object with NO corresponding file. CONFIGURATIONS (3.2.4): combining version history + component hierarchy yields a configuration; mapping info is stored PER NODE ('a composite object version carries with it the version numbers of its components'), enabling the static-vs-dynamic distinction. EQUIVALENCES (3.2.5): a structural object tying heterogeneous representations (layout/transistor/functional) of the same real-world thing via is-equivalent-to; equivalences are 'constraints on the database that can be exploited at design verification time' and can be inherited by new versions. Operations (3.3): CURRENCY (3.3.1) decouples 'current' from 'last created,' constrains branchiness (new versions must be descendants of current), allows multiple parallel currencies (e.g. May Release vs June Release) — 'our first example of an operational mechanism' with associated policy. DYNAMIC CONFIGURATIONS (3.3.2) implemented via PIE-style LAYERS and CONTEXTS (search order over layers), with the noted weakness that layers can be combined in nonsensical ways with no interface constraints. WORKSPACES (3.3.3): archive/group/private repositories with a worked example of a version migrating private->group->archive via check-out/check-in, relationships established automatically on archive check-in. LOGICAL vs PHYSICAL (3.3.4): the web of relationships is implemented as on-disk linked lists of configuration/version/equivalence nodes pointing at UNIX files, keeping per-version storage overhead small. CHANGE/CONSTRAINT PROPAGATION (3.3.5): change propagation 'automatically incorporates new versions into configurations'; constraint propagation enforces directional equivalences by regenerating versions. Two hard problems: LIMITING SCOPE (rarely propagate to the root) and DISAMBIGUATING PATH (DAG propagation order changes the result). Solution = GROUP CHECK-IN/CHECK-OUT making intent unambiguous: 'A configuration node spawns a new version at most once no matter how many times a change path passes through it,' so the result is order-independent; group check-out limits inherited equivalence to the group. INHERITANCE (3.3.6): argues plain type-instance inheritance forces an undesirable proliferation of subtypes to carry instance-specific equivalence constraints, and proposes direct INSTANCE-TO-INSTANCE inheritance along structural relationships (a new version inherits most of its description from its version-history ancestor). Summary (3.4): three orthogonal organizing relationships (version histories, configurations, equivalences); admits the model is weak on separating interface from contents and on the instance/definition distinction.

“It is a key aspect of our organization that structural information be separated from representational information”
“it is not possible to determine from this naming scheme which version is descended from which. Thus, we introduce the concept of a version history and is-derived-from”
“It is frequently the case that the default or current version is not the most recently created version... it is useful to separate 'current' from 'last'”
“A configuration node spawns a new version at most once no matter how many times a change path passes through it... the resulting configuration is independent of the check-in order”

§ 4. Survey of Version Data Models. Traces ~8 years of evolution and reviews ~15 systems. GENERAL TRENDS (4.1): three approaches — extend the relational model (complex objects, conversational/nested transactions, long fields), extend the E-R model (embed is-derived-from/is-a-part-of/is-equivalent-to; Sec-3 model is here), and new object-oriented models (operational with inheritance vs structural with distinguished relationships; combined is best for CAD). Chronology: 1980-81 timestamped records; 1982 complex objects; 1983-84 molecular/composite objects; 1985-86 derivation histories, configurations, equivalences recognized; 1986-87 change notification/propagation; 1988 schema evolution (Banerjee/Kim) and awareness of the need for comprehensive frameworks. PROPOSED MODELS (4.2), each contributing a distinct mechanism: Haskin & Lorie [HASK82] — complex objects in System-R, foreign-key COMP_OF traversal, long transactions (but ignores versions). McLeod/Narayanaswamy/Bapa Rao [MCLE83] — early compositions+versions via AND-OR graphs (AND=composition, OR=alternatives), explicit instance/definition distinction, but no is-derived-from. Dittrich & Lorie [DITT88] — design object = a SET of versions with a distinguished current; GENERIC references + hierarchical ENVIRONMENTS for dynamic binding; logical VERSION CLUSTERS for arbitrary, possibly-overlapping groupings. Klahold/Schlageter/Wilkes [KLAH86] — VERSION GRAPHS (derivation is just one of many threadings), PARTITIONS by consistency level, SUBSET VIEWS. Batory & Kim [BATO85] — MOLECULAR OBJECTS (interface/implementation separation), TYPE-VERSION GENERALIZATION (versions as instances of a type, inheriting the interface), INSTANTIATION (use vs definition), PARAMETRIZED VERSIONS; change notification via two timestamps T_CN (last changed) and T_CA (changes last acknowledged) with implementation/reference/representation consistency conditions. Landis [LAND86] (Ontologic) — non-linear histories, current+default-branch, fixed (historical) vs floating references, change propagation with DELTA SETS and PENDED VERSION CREATION (no object evolves more than one new version per propagation). Chou & Kim [CHOU86] — transient/working/released versions; message-based (immediate) vs flag-based (deferred) notification; single-level (non-recursive) propagation. Ketabchi & Berzins [KETA87] — REFINEMENT (template/explosion/instance); a Refinement Graph (DAG) plus an Incremental Refinement Graph recording DELTAS; dependent/independent/alternative refinements; Parts/Projects/Private databases. Eckland et al. [ECKL87] (DVSS) — FEDERATIONS (site-distributed workspaces with associate/participating access); version DAGs with a PRINCIPAL PATH; principal-successor/derivation/consolidation relationships; fixed vs floating references. Beech & Mahbod [BEEC88] (IRIS) — version is an object with its own OID, organized in version SETS around a generic instance; version graph via functions; new versions by MUTATION (copy-on-write) or PROPAGATION; specific vs generic references resolved by a rule-based trigger CONTEXT. Rumbaugh [RUMB88] — PROPAGATION ATTRIBUTES on relationships (none/propagate/shallow/inhibit) to control operation/change spread. Vines/Vines/King [VINE88] (GAIA) — timestamp versions; version-sensitive and change-sensitive relationships; explicit CHANGE REQUEST and CHANGE NOTIFICATION objects providing an audit trail; configuration objects grouping changes. SUN NSE [SUN88] — File/Target/Component/Linkdb objects, interleaved-delta storage, acquire/modify/reconcile, hierarchical environments (release/integration/development), variants for target architectures, email notification. Apollo DSEE [LEBL84] — History Manager (SCCS-like reserve/replace), Configuration Manager with system models and CONFIGURATION THREADS (component+version tabulations, dynamically bindable via VERSION MAPS; a bound thread + keyword = a RELEASE), Task/Monitor/Advice Managers.

“The model distinguishes among versions representing alternative implementations, revisions, and different representations”
“all versions of an object are constrained to implement the same interface... the type system's inheritance mechanisms can be leveraged”
“An object is implementation consistent if its T_CA exceeds T_CN... reference consistent if its T_CA exceeds the T_CN of all of its components”
“References among objects can be fixed to a specific version or left floating... floating references are resolved to the current version”

§ 5. Version Modeling Mechanisms and Policies (the unification). Re-cuts all the surveyed models by MECHANISM rather than by system, arguing 'much of the variation across models is due not so much to different concepts, but to different terminology for the same concept.' Reduces everything to SEVEN classes of mechanism (5.1): (1) ORGANIZING THE VERSION SET — version set/history/graph/refinement graph are all the same thing arranging versions by ancestor/descendant, with branches=alternatives, a distinguished current, and a generic object; identifiers are version numbers, timestamps, or OIDs. (2) DYNAMIC CONFIGURATION DEREFERENCING — static/specific/fixed vs dynamic/generic/floating/parametrized references; resolved either by layers+contexts/environments/version-maps OR by 'current on a distinguished path,' with Beech-Mahbod's rule-based approach the most general. (3) HIERARCHICAL COMPOSITIONS — flat configurations (a table mapping objects to versions, e.g. VINE88) vs hierarchical configurations (composite/molecular objects, explosion refinements, configuration threads). (4) VERSION GROUPING — Dittrich's logical version clusters (general, overlapping) vs Klahold's single-level partitions. (5) INSTANCES vs DEFINITIONS — only MCLE83 and BATO85 cleanly separate a use (instance, a copy) from a definition (version). (6) CHANGE NOTIFICATION & PROPAGATION — notification via timestamps/messages/flags; propagation = incorporating new versions up the configuration hierarchy, needing disambiguation (group check-in, pended version creation) and scope-limiting (propagation attributes, change-sensitive relationships). (7) OBJECT SHARING — workspaces moved through via check-in/out (a.k.a. acquire/modify/reconcile, reserve/replace), in repositories (workspaces/databases/federations/environments) that most agree need at least THREE levels (archive/group/private; parts/projects/private; release/integration/development). THE GRAND UNIFICATION (5.2) proposes consensus terminology, mostly adopting Section 3: version history with ancestor/descendant links, generic object, main derivation (= principal path = default branch), branches=alternatives, current decoupled from last-created, merged descent (= reconciliation = consolidation). It prefers DITT88's hierarchical ENVIRONMENTS for dynamic binding (subsumes layers/contexts and current-on-path), the composite-object terminology for configurations (plus a flattened-configuration form for archives), Dittrich's version clusters for grouping (orthogonal to ancestry), an explicit instance-hierarchy-referencing-definition-hierarchy for instance-specific attributes, and RUMB88's propagation attributes as the most fundamental scope-limiting mechanism (subsuming timestamp and change-sensitive methods).

“there are really only a small number of notions underlying the various proposals... seven classes of mechanisms”
“We feel it is important to decouple the notion of current from last created”
“[KATZ85] distinguishes between static and dynamic configuration binding... [ECKL87] calls these fixed and floating... [BEEC88] call them specific and generic”
“the propagation attribute approach of [RUMB88] can be made to cover the timestamp methods of [BATO85] and [CHOU86], as well as the change sensitive relationships of [VINE88], and is more general”

§ 6. Summary and Conclusions. Restates that despite many proposals 'with its own idiosyncratic terminology... most proposals were really minor variations on a small number of themes,' and that unification largely succeeded for version histories, dynamic configurations, and workspaces — apparent differences being terminological. Identifies mechanisms that GENERALIZE others (notably propagation attributes subsuming timestamp methods). Honestly concedes no ultimate model exists or is likely soon; the author's PREFERRED model = the Section-3 Version Server extended with Dittrich's logical version clusters + hierarchical environments, Eckland's federation concept, Rumbaugh's propagation attributes, and a merged-path version history. Closes by stating the framework-construction challenge — a single tailorable framework — 'is something that is still left to be accomplished. This paper represents a first step in that direction.'

“most proposals were really minor variations on a small number of themes”
“While we would have liked to have been able to present the ultimate version management model, we do not feel that such a model is likely to exist for some time”
“The final challenge, that of creating a single framework... that can be tailored for the needs of a given version environment, is something that is still left to be accomplished”

§ 7. Bibliography. ~30 references spanning 1982-1989, the foundational corpus of CAD/engineering version management: Haskin & Lorie 1982, Lorie & Plouffe 1983, McLeod et al. 1983, Batory & Buchmann 1984, Leblang & Chase 1984 (DSEE), Katz & Lehman 1984, Batory & Kim 1985 (ACM TODS), Katz 1985 (Springer survey monograph), Chou & Kim 1986, Klahold et al. 1986, Landis 1986, Ketabchi & Berzins 1987, Eckland et al. 1987 (DVSS), Banerjee/Kim/Korth 1987 (schema evolution), Dittrich & Lorie 1988, Beech & Mahbod 1988 (IRIS), Rumbaugh 1988 (OOPSLA, propagation attributes), Vines et al. 1988 (GAIA), SUN NSE 1988, plus the author's own Version Server papers (Katz/Chang/Bhateja 1986-88) and the Bhateja & Katz validation-subsystem paper.

“BANE 87... 'Semantics and Implementation of Schema Evolution in Object-Oriented Databases'”
“RUMB 88 Rumbaugh, J., 'Controlling Propagation of Operations Using Attributes on Relations,' OOPSLA '88”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel: stable CONTAINER = identity thread, REVISIONS = immutable values, current-revision = state — Gives our kernel its exact historical name and validates the shape. Katz's 'generic object' (e.g. ALU.Layout) is a STRUCTURAL object with NO corresponding file — pure identity thread — while each ALU[i].Layout is an immutable versioned instance hanging off it via is-a-kind-of/is-derived-from. That is our container/revision split almost line-for-line. His 'currency' mechanism is precisely our current-revision pointer, and his key insight directly supports our hard rule: he DECOUPLES current from last-created ('It is frequently the case that the default or current version is not the most recently created version'), proving the head pointer must be explicit state, not inferred from arrival order.
  • Versioning as its own layer: Rama arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit git-style committed/minted versions (full persistence) — two distinct times — Strong support, and a warning. Katz separates a 'version model' (organizing data across time) from a 'design transaction model' (how change happens), and insists 'not every update need result in the construction of a new version' — a version is 'a semantically meaningful snapshot.' That is exactly our two-times distinction: Rama's per-update arrival log is the substrate, but a minted/committed version is a deliberate, semantically-significant act layered on top. He also warns the cost of conflating them: uncontrolled version creation causes 'proliferation of versions, many of which are likely to be of little interest,' which his currency mechanism exists to constrain — i.e. minting must be gated, not automatic.
  • Groupings as versioned objects: a curated grouping/local world has its OWN version history, partly independent of members; member binding pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped — Directly mechanized. Katz's configuration = 'a binding between a version of a composite object and the versions of its components,' and crucially a composite is 'a new version in its own right' even when 'its contents have not changed' but a component changed — the grouping versions independently of and because of its members. His pinned/floating/effectivity vocabulary IS our binding spectrum: static/specific/fixed reference (pinned), dynamic/generic/floating reference (floating), and parametrized versions / layer-context resolution (effectivity-scoped). Dittrich's logical 'version clusters' (arbitrary, overlapping member groupings with their own identity) are our 'curated local world' precisely.
  • Four-level graph versioning: graph / node / relation-TYPE / node-DATA, and these must COMPOSE — Partial support + a named gap. Katz delivers node-level (version history), node-DATA-level (revisions of contents), and graph/composition-level (configurations compose component versions hierarchically, and configuration mappings are stored PER NODE so versions compose without re-mapping the whole tree). The relation-TYPE level he does NOT version — his three relation kinds (is-a-part-of, is-derived-from, is-equivalent-to) are fixed schema, not versioned objects. He flags this frontier himself: by 1988 'the field has moved towards extending version concepts to model schema evolution... how the structure of the database changes over time, not just how its contents change' (citing Banerjee/Kim). That is our relation-TYPE-versioning requirement, named as the open edge of the field.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality: conflicting views representable not overwritten; synthesis late-bound — Mechanism + name. Katz's ALTERNATIVES are 'parallel versions... NOT on the same path to the root' — coexisting, non-overwriting branches; merging them is an explicit late act ('merged descent' / 'reconciliation' / 'consolidation'), never automatic. His EQUIVALENCES are an even stronger gift: a structural object that ties heterogeneous representations of the same real-world thing (layout/transistor/functional views) together as a CONSTRAINT 'exploited at design verification time,' not a forced merge. That is our model for holding plural representations in preserved tension with late-bound, checkable synthesis.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing) — Strong support for declared-over-inferred. Katz's is-derived-from is an EXPLICITLY RECORDED ancestor relationship precisely because naming-scheme alone (name[version#].type) cannot recover lineage: 'it is not possible to determine from this naming scheme which version is descended from which.' This is our hard rule that inferred structure is never truth at ingest — derivation must be a declared, first-class edge. Ketabchi/Berzins' Incremental Refinement Graph (recording the actual DELTAS that differentiate a version from its ancestor) and Ontologic's 'delta sets' are the declared-edit-operation analog of our trail's edit operations resolving path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment.
  • The code-identity question: file vs path vs name/FQN vs AST vs git-hash; atomic unit = file or function — Directly addressed, with a stance. Katz separates 'structural objects' (identity/organization, owned by design management) from 'representational objects' (the actual files, owned by tools), and insists 'structural information be separated from representational information... absolutely necessary if the same model is to be applicable to a wide range of design domains.' His unit is the named 'design object' ('better to think of them as units of the design that can be given a name, such as Arithmetic Logic Unit') — NOT the file. The generic object has no file at all. This argues our atomic unit should be a named structural identity (the container), decoupled from any single file/path/hash, which are merely its representational backing.

Takeaway. Katz's deepest contribution to our versioning/identity question is the separation of STRUCTURAL identity from REPRESENTATIONAL backing: a versioned object's identity lives in a 'generic object' that has NO file of its own (our container/identity-thread), while versions (our immutable revisions) and configurations hang off it via explicitly-recorded relationships — and 'current' must be decoupled from 'last-created' as deliberate state, never inferred from arrival order. He proves that derivation, equivalence, and grouping must be DECLARED first-class edges because naming/arrival data cannot reconstruct them — directly underwriting our hard rule that inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest. He also hands us the precise vocabulary for the whole problem (version history, configuration, alternatives vs derivatives, static/dynamic binding, equivalences, change propagation, version clusters) and names our one genuine frontier beyond his 1988 scope: versioning the relation-TYPE level (schema evolution), which the field had only just begun to address.

22. Versions of Schema for Object-Oriented Databases

Won Kim and Hong-Tai Chou (MCC, Austin TX) · 1988 · Proceedings of the 14th VLDB Conference, Los Angeles, California

In one line. Extends ORION's single-object version model to version the entire SCHEMA (the class lattice) as one versioned object, with a derivation hierarchy and an inherited 'access scope' that ties each schema version to a non-destructively branchable snapshot of the database.

Source access: full. Read the FULL paper. WebFetch downloaded the 1.3MB PDF from vldb.org but returned only binary; I extracted the complete text locally with pdftotext (805 lines, all 8 sections + references intact). OCR noise present (e.g. 'CHOUSS'=CHOU88, 'arz'=are) but technical content fully legible. Source read: the canonical VLDB-1988 PDF itself, not a secondary copy.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract. States that prior version research versioned only single design objects, never the schema (the definition of those objects). In a multi-user design environment where the schema undergoes dynamic change, you must be able to version the schema too. The paper's contribution: a model of versions of schema obtained by EXTENDING ORION's existing model of versions of single objects — presenting its semantics, implementation issues, and alternatives.

“research in versions has been focused exclusively on versioning single design objects”
“we propose a model of versions of schema by extending our model of versions of single objects”

§ 1. Introduction. Sets the motivation. ORION is a prototype OODB at MCC supporting CAD/CAM/AI/OIS apps, already with object versions, composite objects, and dynamic schema evolution. The key argument: if the schema cannot be versioned, a schema change (e.g. dropping an attribute) IRREVERSIBLY harms pre-existing objects — dropped-attribute values become invisible. Versioning the schema instead lets old objects stay fully visible under the OLD schema version while the new version sees them without the dropped attribute. Second motivation: checkout/checkin in a federated private/public database — a modified schema must be copied to the public DB as a new schema version before a modified object can be checked in. Claims this is largely unexplored (only [SKAR86] touched versioning a single class) and promises two original contributions: a semantic model with a small command interface, and detailed implementation techniques.

“if an attribute of a class is dropped, the values of the attribute in existing objects ... become no longer visible to the application”
“If, however, a new version of the schema is derived in which the attribute ... is dropped, the values ... continue to be visible to the application under the old version of the schema”
“except for [SKAR86] ... the semantics and implementation of versions of schema have not been reported in the literature”

§ 2. Basic Object-Oriented Concepts. Review (extracted from [BANE87a]). Everything is an object with private memory (attribute/instance-variable values) and behavior encapsulated in methods; objects communicate only via messages, the public interface. 'Similar' objects are grouped into a CLASS sharing the same attributes and methods; objects of a class are its instances. The class hierarchy encodes IS-A: a subclass specializes a superclass and inherits its properties (attributes+methods collectively), inheriting transitively up the superclass chain. The domain (type) of an attribute is itself a class. ORION generalizes the hierarchy to a class LATTICE allowing multiple inheritance, which creates name conflicts: conflicts between a class and its superclass are resolved by giving the local definition precedence; conflicts AMONG superclasses are resolved by superclass ORDERING (the first superclass in the application-specified list wins). This lattice + flattening + ordering machinery is what later sections must version.

“A class describes the form (attributes) of its instances, and the operations (methods) applicable to its instances”
“a class can have more than one superclass, generalizing the class hierarchy to a lattice”
“the one chosen by default is that of the first superclass in the list of (immediate) superclasses of C”

§ 3.1 Versions (review of object versions). The foundational two-level model the whole paper builds on. Versions carry CAPABILITIES: a TRANSIENT version can be updated or deleted at will; a WORKING version can be deleted but NOT updated. A transient version is promoted to working explicitly by the user, or IMPLICITLY when a new transient is derived from it. An object is versioned (instance of a class declared versionable) or non-versioned. A versioned object is a hierarchy of versions = a version-derivation hierarchy. Critical terminology: a VERSION INSTANCE is a specific version in that hierarchy; a GENERIC INSTANCE is the abstract versioned object that maintains the derivation history. Both the generic instance AND each version instance get their OWN unique object identifier. A reference to a version instance's OID = STATIC binding; a reference to the generic instance's OID = DYNAMIC binding, resolving to a default version instance (user-specified, else the most-recent timestamp). Dynamic binding is useful because transient/working versions may be deleted and new ones created.

“We use the term version instance to refer to a specific version ... and generic instance to refer to the abstract versioned object”
“both the generic instance and a version instance ... have object identifiers”
“If the reference is to a version instance, we say that the object is statically bound ... If the reference is to a generic instance, the object is said to be dynamically bound to a default version instance”

§ 3.2 Schema Evolution (review). Defines what a schema change IS, since the paper versions schema changes. The schema = the class lattice; two change types are meaningful: changes to a class DEFINITION (node contents: add/drop attributes & methods) and changes to lattice STRUCTURE (edges/nodes: create/drop a class, add/drop a superclass-subclass IS-A edge). It then gives the precise semantics of five operations: (1) Add attribute V to C — inherited by subclasses, overrides conflicting inherited attribute, existing instances get nil for V; (2) Drop attribute V from C — V removed from C and inheriting subclasses, those instances lose V's value, and an alternate same-named attribute from another superclass may be re-inherited; (3) Make S a superclass of C — must not create a cycle; C and subclasses inherit from S; (4) Add class C — inherits from all specified superclasses (or CLASS if none); (5) Drop class C — edges to subclasses dropped (they lose inherited properties), edges from superclasses removed, C deleted but its subclasses survive. These exact semantics are replayed in Section 5's implementation.

“the schema of an object-oriented database is the class lattice”
“Existing instances of the classes to which V is added receive the nil value”
“The addition of a new edge from S to C must not introduce a cycle in the class lattice”

§ 4. Versions of Schema (the model — 7 rules). The core contribution. Three candidate approaches are named — versions-of-SCHEMA (whole lattice = one versioned object), versions-of-CLASS (each class = a versioned object), and VIEWS-of-schema (dynamic views, not versions). They choose versions-of-schema and extend version capabilities, derivation hierarchies, and default versions to it, adding the view that 'a version of schema is associated with a SET OF OBJECTS created under it.' Key definitions: ancestor/descendant, parent/child, CURRENT schema version (where you operate now), CREATOR schema version (where an object was created), DIRECT access scope (objects created under SV), and ACCESS SCOPE (all objects visible to SV). They note only INSTANCE objects matter for versioning — class objects need not be versioned. The model is seven rules: (1) Capability Rule — transient vs working schema versions; working can't be updated but can be demoted if it has no child; (2) Derivation Rule — derive any number of new schema versions from any existing one, forming a derivation hierarchy; deriving from a transient auto-promotes it to working; (3) Deletion Rule — only a LEAF schema version can be deleted, and deleting it also deletes its direct access scope (so 'a schema version OWNS the objects created under it'); (4) Access-Scope Inheritance Rule — SV-j by default inherits SV-i's access scope, but the user can BLOCK inheritance, and once inherited, SV-i's scope by default becomes non-updatable under SV-i (optionally left updatable); (5) Access-Scope Rule — SV's access scope = objects created under SV plus those inherited from ancestor scopes; all are readable AND updatable, nothing else is visible; (6) Direct-Access-Scope Update Rule — SV's direct scope is frozen once any child is derived from it WITHOUT blocking/leaving-updatable; (7) Inherited-Access-Scope Update Rule — updates/deletes of inherited objects under SV are visible ONLY to SV and descendants that inherited from SV (branch-local). Three derivation options follow (inherit+freeze, inherit+keep-updatable, don't-inherit), changeable later. Figure 1 walks SV-0→SV-1 (drop at3) →SV-2 (add at4) showing objects flowing into each scope minus/plus attributes.

“a schema version 'owns' the objects created under it, that is, its direct access scope”
“SV-j by default inherits the access scope of SV-i. However, the user may optionally block the inheritance”
“The access scope of a schema version SV is the set of objects created under SV and those objects in the inherited access scopes of the ancestor schema versions”
“updates and deletes under SV of the objects inherited into SV are only visible to SV and the descendant schema versions of SV which inherited the access scope from SV”

§ 5.1 Implementation — Auxiliary Data Structures for Objects. To make versions-of-schema cheap, every object gets one system-defined CREATOR attribute (its creator schema version) and, when needed, an ANCHOR INSTANCE side-structure that describes a SET OF COPIES of the object — analogous to how a generic instance describes the set of version instances. The trick: copy-on-write across schema versions. An object first exists with no anchor instance, just its creator id. When it is first deleted/replaced under a NON-creator schema version, the system makes a new COPY and an anchor instance. All copies AND the anchor share the SAME object identifier (so existing references stay valid); the CREATOR attribute distinguishes copies. The anchor instance holds two attributes: a list of TERMINATOR schema versions (where the object was deleted — always a descendant of the creator) and a list of copies. Figure 2 traces: copy-0 created under SV-0; updated under SV-4 → anchor instance + copy-1 (creator SV-4); copy-0 deleted under SV-1 → SV-1 recorded as a terminator.

“we need to include in every object one system-defined attribute, called the creator attribute, and to associate a data structure, called an anchor instance, with every object”
“An anchor instance, and each of the copies of the object it describes, are all identified by the same object identifier of the object”
“A terminator schema version is the schema version under which the object was deleted”

§ 5.2 Object Access Algorithms. Precise fetch/insert/delete/replace algorithms using creator/anchor/terminator. Two booleans: Ancestor-Of (true if arg1 is an ancestor whose access scope is inherited by arg2) and Frozen (true if a SV's direct scope is non-updatable). Helper find-closest-copy walks the copy-list returning the closest ancestor copy; copy-blocked checks whether a terminator hides a copy from sv. ALGORITHM-SV-FETCH: locate object; if it's an anchor instance, find the closest copy and error if none visible / blocked by a terminator; else error if the lone copy's creator isn't an ancestor of sv. INSERT errors if frozen, else stamps creator=sv and allocates an id (new objects join the current version's DIRECT scope). DELETE/REPLACE: if creator=sv, physically delete/update in place; else (inherited object) create/extend an anchor instance, adding sv as a terminator (delete) or pushing a new copy with creator=sv (replace) — making the change branch-local. Performance: the Ancestor-Of check dominates, so the ancestor relationship is encoded in per-schema-version BIT VECTORS (root = all zeros; deriving SV-j inheriting from SV-k sets BV-j[k]=1 and copies BV-k), reducing the ancestor test to an O(1) vector access; provably BV-j[i]=1 iff SV-j inherits from SV-i. A second bit vector tracks class-object inheritance since class objects are always inherited at derivation time.

“the system will create a new copy of the object when the object is first deleted or replaced under a schema version which is not its creator schema version”
“the ancestor relationship can be encoded in bit vectors, one for each schema version”
“we have reduced the check for ancestor relationship to a vector access”

§ 5.3 Storage Representation for the Schema. How a single schema is stored, then how versions are stored cheaply. A single schema = a set of CLASS OBJECTS, each represented as instances of system-defined classes Class, Attribute, Method (like system catalogs). Class holds ClassName, Attributes, Superclasses, Subclasses, Methods — and crucially Attributes/Methods hold BOTH locally-defined AND inherited properties ('FLATTENING' of the lattice, [ZARA85], for fast access). Attribute holds Class, VariableName, Domain (the class the value binds to), InheritedFrom. For VERSIONS, the objective is NOT to copy the whole schema per version. Insight: every schema change is either a class-definition change or a class-relationship change, and relationships are encoded inside class objects — so they keep Class/Attribute/Method instances NON-versioned but apply the SAME anchor-instance copy mechanism to CLASS OBJECTS. A class-definition change copies that one class object; a relationship change copies the class object of EACH affected class (both endpoints of an edge). Figure 4 traces five schema changes (define C-a/b/c; add subclass C-d under SV-1 forcing a copy of C-b; add C-e under SV-2 forcing another C-b copy; make C-c a superclass of C-e under SV-3 copying both; delete C-b under SV-4 copying C-a/C-d/C-e). The schema-version derivation hierarchy itself is stored via a system class SCHEMA with a single versionable instance — each schema version is a version instance of that one object; SCHEMA carries Update-Instances (boolean, false when a child froze the scope) and Ancestor-SVs (list of ancestors whose scope is inherited).

“Attributes and Methods attributes for a class hold values for not only the attributes and methods defined for the class, but also those inherited ... This technique is known as 'flattening'”
“we ... apply the anchor instance structure to class objects to support updates to a class object under different schema versions”
“Each schema version is a version instance of this versioned object, and the schema-version derivation hierarchy is maintained in the generic instance”

§ 6. User Interface. A deliberately small command set despite rich semantics. derive-schema-version(parent-SV, highest-ancestor-SV, update-instances) returns a new schema version derived from parent-SV (auto-promoting parent if transient); highest-ancestor-SV sets the highest ancestor whose access scope is inherited (default root = full inheritance; value 'self' = no inheritance; otherwise the union of direct scopes from parent up to that ancestor); update-instances defaults False (inherited scope becomes non-updatable under its creators) — True leaves them updatable. change-inheritance(highest-ancestor-SV, update-instances) lets you change those two parameters AFTER derivation. delete-schema-version(SV) deletes SV plus all version instances created under it, rejected if SV has a child. promote-schema-version(SV) upgrades transient→working. set-current-schema-version(current-SV) switches the active schema version (all ops run under it). current-schema-version() returns it.

“The set of messages is surprisingly small, despite the rich semantics that the model captures”
“If the value of the parameter is self, the access scope of the parent schema version is not inherited into the new schema version”

§ 7. Alternative Approaches (versions-of-class, views-of-schema). Defends the design against the two rejected approaches. 7.1 Versions of Classes ([SKAR86]): treat each class object as versionable and build a 'virtual' schema as a lattice picking one version per class. Problems: the user must MANUALLY track which class-versions form which virtual schema and maintain per-virtual-schema default-version lists for every generic class reference; dynamic binding of inter-class references means the virtual schema CANNOT be FLATTENED (losing fast access). Even with static binding it's worse: because each class version has a different OID and class objects carry Superclasses/Subclasses, deriving a new version of class C forces a new version of EVERY subclass AND superclass — cascading into 'a new version of the entire class lattice for each virtual schema version.' ORION's own approach uses COPIES (not versions) of class objects, allowing only one copy per class within a schema version — so users can't experiment with alternative defs of a class within one schema version, but avoid the cascade and the cross-version surprise-update hazard. 7.2 Views of Schema: provide dynamic views over a single underlying schema with a view-derivation hierarchy, but with NO inheritance of objects between views — every object belongs to the single schema, and a view's scope is just the objects whose attributes the view defines. Consequence: ALL updates under one view become visible to all views sharing those attribute definitions — so a view does NOT capture a point-in-time DB snapshot, sharply contrasting ORION where objects under SV can be preserved against derived versions.

“if we are to use versions of the class objects to support 'virtual' schema versions, we will end up generating a new version of the entire class lattice for each 'virtual' schema version”
“the use of dynamic binding of references to class objects implies that the 'virtual' schema version cannot be flattened for efficient access”
“the schema view approach does not associate with any view a snapshot of the state of the database at some point in time ... This contrasts sharply with our model”

§ 8. Summary. Recaps that versioning both design objects AND their schema matters in multi-user design, that object-version research was extensive but schema versioning had virtually no formal semantics before this paper, and that they built schema versions atop ORION's object-version model. Reasserts the two admitted weaknesses (non-trivial overhead for inherited-object updates, and user confusion when an object deleted in a descendant reappears when operating under the ancestor — 'exactly the desired effect ... however ... somewhat confusing'). Frames the central thesis: each schema version captures the DB state at a point in time, inheritable into derived versions for read and update — and the two alternatives (class versioning, dynamic views) were shown to fall short of this.

“each version of the schema captures the state of the database at some point in time and that the state may be inherited into any derived schema version for read and update”
“there has been virtually no formal proposal to define the semantics of versioning the schema”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (container=identity thread, revisions=immutable values, current-revision=state) — GIVES IT A NAME and a battle-tested mechanism. The paper's generic instance / version instance split is exactly our container/revision split: a 'generic instance' is the abstract versioned object that carries a stable object identifier and the derivation history; a 'version instance' is a specific concrete version with its OWN identifier. ORION even gives BOTH the container AND each revision distinct OIDs, then lets references target either one. This validates our two-level model and warns us we must decide which identity a reference resolves to.
  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions) — SUPPORTS the two-times distinction and shows the cost of the explicit-commit (full-persistence) side. ORION's schema versions are git-style MINTED versions you branch from (derive-schema-version), with transient (mutable) vs working (frozen) capability — directly our 'committed/minted versions you can branch from.' The paper has NO arrival-time layer; everything is explicit derivation. It thus shows the full-persistence half of our design in isolation, and the bookkeeping (anchor instances, terminators, bit-vectors) it costs.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (a curated local world has its OWN version history, partly independent of members; member binding pinned/floating) — OFFERS A DIRECT MECHANISM and a failure-mode warning. ORION treats the ENTIRE schema (the whole class lattice = a grouping of class objects) as a single versioned object with its own derivation hierarchy — independent of any individual class's evolution. Each schema version is itself a version instance of one system SCHEMA object. The rejected 'versions of classes' alternative is our 'floating member binding' failure mode: when members version independently and reference each other, deriving one member forces re-versioning of all neighbors ('a new version of the entire class lattice for each virtual schema version'). Lesson: version the GROUP, snapshot the members.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, composing) — PARTIALLY VALIDATES and shows the composition tax. ORION versions at graph level (the schema/lattice = SchemaVersion), node level (class objects, via copies under each SchemaVersion), relation-TYPE level (IS-A edges encoded inside class objects, so an edge change forces copies of both endpoint class objects), and node-DATA level (instance objects via anchor instances + creator/terminator). It proves these levels CAN compose, but the cost is the warning: an edge change cascades into multiple node copies, and the access-scope rule is the glue that makes node-data inheritance compose with graph versions.
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve a path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; declared vs inferred change) — OFFERS THE RESOLUTION ALGORITHM. ORION's ALGORITHM-SV-FETCH(object-id, sv) is precisely 'resolve identity X as-of version-context sv': find-closest-copy walks ancestor copies, copy-blocked checks terminators. This is the template for resolving a trail's file-identity at its commit/version context. Our 'declared vs inferred' maps to ORION's static vs dynamic binding: a reference to a specific version instance = DECLARED/pinned; a reference to the generic instance resolving to a default = INFERRED/floating.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (conflicting views representable, synthesis late-bound) — SUPPORTS via the access-scope model. ORION lets sibling schema versions hold DIVERGENT updates to inherited objects that are 'visible only to SV-j; viewed from SV-i it is as if the updates never took place' — concurrent, non-destructive, branch-local truth, exactly preserved disagreement. The paper contrasts this with the rejected 'views of schema' approach where 'all updates under one view become visible to all views' — i.e. silent overwrite, the anti-pattern we want to avoid. ORION chose snapshot-isolation-per-branch over shared mutable views.
  • Hard rule: 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' — SUPPORTS through static binding and creator-attribute provenance. ORION records a CREATOR schema version on every object (declared provenance, stamped at write) rather than inferring which version an object belongs to. Static binding pins a reference to an exact version instance so the system never guesses. The dynamic-binding/default-version path is explicitly the convenience/inference path, and the paper flags that 'virtual versions cannot be flattened' and 'may generate surprises' — i.e. inference is second-class, exactly our rule.
  • Substrate choices: Rama event-sourced full vs partial persistence — WARNS about implementation overhead if we hand-roll. ORION must add a CREATOR attribute to every object, an ANCHOR INSTANCE side-structure (terminator list + copy list) for any object touched outside its creator version, and per-version BIT VECTORS to make ancestor-checks O(1). The authors concede 'non-trivial system overhead' and user 'confusion' as the two admitted weaknesses. In Rama these become PState design choices — and the paper argues a log/copy-on-derive substrate is the right shape, which Rama already gives us for free.

Takeaway. The paper's enduring contribution to our versioning/identity question is the generic-instance / version-instance split with static vs dynamic binding — a stable identity-bearing container (its own OID) holding immutably-derived version instances (their own OIDs), where a reference EITHER pins an exact version (static = our 'declared/pinned') OR floats to a default (dynamic = our 'inferred/floating'). Equally important, it proves that versioning a GROUPING (the whole schema lattice) as a single versioned object — and snapshotting members via copy-on-derive plus an inherited 'access scope' — beats versioning each member independently, because independent member-versioning cascades into re-versioning the entire neighborhood. This is direct, 1988-tested guidance for our 'groupings as versioned objects' and 'four-level graph versioning' layers: version the group, snapshot the members, and make inheritance (not shared mutation) the default so branches preserve disagreement non-destructively.

23. Versioning and Configuration Management in an Object-Oriented Data Model

Edward Sciore · 1994 · The VLDB Journal, vol. 3, no. 1, pp. 77–106

In one line. Extends the EXTRA data model / EXCESS query language so that versions form a multidimensional version SET inside the object, and configurations are declarative (intensional) queries over those dimensions — yielding one application-independent versioning model that subsumes CAD, CASE, and temporal databases.

Source access: blocked-used-alternative. The primary article on Springer (link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF01231359) is paywalled — it 303-redirects to an IdP login; no open PDF, tech-report, CiteSeerX, or author-homepage mirror exists (searched extensively; scispace 403s, Google Scholar blocks). I reconstructed the model from (1) the paper's published abstract as quoted across Springer/scispace search results, and (2) the open, full-text Conradi & Westfechtel survey 'Version Models for Software Configuration Management' (ACM Computing Surveys 30(2), 1998, pp.232–282), which describes Sciore 1994/EXTRA-V directly in several passages (its n-dimensional variant grid in Fig.5b is attributed to Sciore 1994; it cites Sciore 1994 on the awkwardness of separate types for versioned objects). The section-by-section walk below is therefore a faithful RECONSTRUCTION of Sciore's argument from its abstract + this authoritative secondary source, NOT a reading of the primary article's own section text. Page-level section titles of the primary paper could not be verified; section names below are reconstructed from the abstract's stated contributions and are flagged as such.

The paper, section by section

§ Abstract (verified — quoted from search-surfaced abstract). States the problem: many database applications (CAD, CASE, temporal/historical DBs) need to store and manipulate multiple versions of data objects, but contemporary DBMSs support versioning only at a low, procedural level. Sciore's thesis is that application-independent versioning can be supported at a SIGNIFICANTLY HIGHER level of abstraction. The mechanism: extend the EXTRA data model and the EXCESS query language so configurations can be specified conceptually and non-procedurally; show that a version SET can be viewed MULTIDIMENSIONALLY, letting configurations be expressed at a higher abstraction level. A version-specification language is given at the conceptual level and a multidimensional specification language at the logical level, encoding an application's logical versioning semantics into orthogonal dimensions — producing a unified, application-independent treatment of versioning.

“application-independent versioning can be supported at a significantly higher level”
“version sets can be viewed multidimensionally, thereby allowing configurations to be expressed at a higher level of abstraction”
“encoding the logical versioning semantics of an application into orthogonal dimensions”

§ Introduction / Motivation (reconstructed from abstract + survey framing). Frames versioning as cross-cutting CAD, CASE, and temporal databases, each of which historically built its own ad-hoc versioning. The argument (corroborated by the survey crediting Sciore with attacking the 'separate types' problem) is that prior models force a distinction between a versioned object and its versions, requiring two object types — which 'is awkward and complicates both schemata and queries.' Sciore positions the contribution as moving versioning UP into the data model and query language so that one mechanism serves all three application domains rather than each reinventing it.

“As argued in Estublier and Casallas [1994] and Sciore [1994], this distinction is awkward and complicates both schemata and queries.”
“DAMOKLES, EXTRA-V [Sciore 1994], and Adele follow this approach [version model built into the data model].”

§ Version sets and the multidimensional model (the core — reconstructed via survey Fig. 5b). The central mechanism. A versioned object owns a SET of versions, and that set is organized not as a linear version graph nor merely as a tree of branches, but as an n-dimensional GRID: each dimension corresponds to a variant attribute, and a specific version is a point in this coordinate space. The survey attributes exactly this grid organization to Sciore 1994 and explains WHY it matters: pure branch-based variant modeling explodes combinatorially — the number of branches is dominated by the product of dimension cardinalities (b ≈ |A1|·…·|An|; their worked example of OS×DB×window = 3×3×3 needs up to 27 branches). The multidimensional version set tames this by making variation orthogonal rather than enumerated. Time/revision can be added as an additional orthogonal dimension (orthogonal version management).

“versions may be arranged in a grid (Figure 5b), that is, an n-dimensional space whose dimensions correspond to variant attributes [Sciore 1994]”
“In the case of multidimensional variation, this approach [branches] breaks down because the number of branches explodes combinatorially.”
“b # |A1|...|An| [the branch count is dominated by the product of domain cardinalities]”

§ Version-model-built-into-the-data-model: EXTRA-V (reconstructed via survey §4). Sciore's architectural choice. The survey's taxonomy distinguishes three options: version model ON TOP OF the data model (ordinary DB application; simple but inefficient — 'no support for storing versions efficiently, the transaction manager does not take versioning into account'), version model BUILT INTO the data model (the DDL gives customized constructs for defining versioned object types and the query language is modified for natural queries over versioned data), and data model on top of version model (orthogonal version engine). Sciore's EXTRA-V is explicitly placed in the BUILT-INTO category alongside DAMOKLES and Adele. The payoff is that versioned and unversioned objects need not be different types and queries stay convenient; the cost is data-model complexity and the lack of a universally accepted uniform version model.

“If the data model is extended with versioning, applications can be supported through a data-definition language that provides customized constructs for defining versioned object types.”
“the query language is modified so that queries against a versioned database can be written in a convenient and natural way”

§ Declarative configurations as intensional selection (reconstructed via survey §3.2 / §5). Sciore's 'conceptual, non-procedural' configuration specification corresponds to the survey's INTENSIONAL versioning: rather than enumerating members extensionally (V = {v1,…,vn}, each immutable and uniquely numbered — retrieval of previously built versions), an intensional configuration defines the set by a predicate (V = {v | c(v)}) and CONSTRUCTS versions on demand. A configuration query is a (partial) function q: A1×…×An → V over the coordinate dimensions — i.e., supplying a tuple of coordinate values selects/constructs the version. This is precisely Sciore's claim that configurations can be 'specified conceptually': you describe WHICH version by its properties/coordinates, and the system selects or builds it, rather than hand-assembling a parts list. The survey notes intensional versioning demands both combinability (any version constructed on demand) and consistency control (constructed versions must satisfy constraints).

“Intensional versioning... the version set is defined by a predicate: V = {v | c(v)}”
“a query corresponds to a (partial or total) function q that creates versions in V based on attributes ranging over the domains: q : A1 x ... x An -> V”
“an SCM system must provide for both combinability... and consistency control—a constructed version must meet certain constraints”

§ Composite/assembly objects (the requested research angle, reconstructed via survey §2.2). Directly relevant to our research angle 5 (composite/assembly). A composite object / configuration is an object o representing a subgraph of the product graph — all objects transitively reachable from o via composition relationships. It can carry structural constraints (tree vs DAG), existence dependencies (a component may depend on its superobject), and acts as a UNIT with respect to structural operations (copy/delete), abstraction (encapsulation), concurrency control (locking the composite locks its components), AND version control. The key composite-versioning insight Sciore operationalizes: the assembly is itself a versioned item with its own history, and its members are bound either extensionally (pinned, specific version IDs) or intensionally (floating, by coordinate predicate / effectivity). Dependency relationships (orthogonal to composition) connect a dependent to a master and require the dependent be kept consistent as the master changes.

“Objects that are decomposed are called composite objects or configurations.”
“a composite object is defined as an object o that represents a subgraph of the product graph. All objects that are transitively reachable from o via composition relationships belong to this subgraph.”
“a composite object may act as a unit with respect to structural operations... concurrency control... and version control”

§ Unification across CAD / CASE / temporal databases (reconstructed from abstract). Sciore's closing claim of generality: by reducing each application's versioning to orthogonal dimensions plus declarative selection, one mechanism subsumes CAD assembly configurations, CASE software configuration management, and temporal/historical databases (where TIME is simply one more orthogonal dimension of the version set). This 'application-independent' framing is the paper's headline contribution — versioning ceases to be a per-domain feature and becomes a data-model-level capability parameterized by which dimensions an application declares.

“a unified, application-independent treatment of versioning”
“Revisions can be represented in the grid by adding a time dimension (orthogonal version management)”

§ Conclusion (reconstructed). The paper concludes that versioning belongs at the data-model and query-language level, expressed as multidimensional version sets with declarative (intensional) configurations, rather than as procedural application code. The dual specification languages — conceptual version-specification and logical multidimensional-specification — let an application encode its versioning semantics as orthogonal dimensions, achieving abstraction, application-independence, and natural querying. The survey's longevity-test corroborates the influence: Sciore 1994 is cited as the canonical source of the n-dimensional grid version model and as an early argument for collapsing the versioned-object/version type distinction.

“a version specification language was developed at the conceptual level, and a multidimensional specification language at the logical level”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (container=identity, revisions=immutable values, current-revision=state) — Strongly supports and names it. Conradi/Westfechtel's framing of Sciore: 'a versioned item is a container for a set V of versions' is exactly our container/revision split. Critically, Sciore (and Estublier/Casallas) ARGUE AGAINST the naive form of our model: requiring different TYPES for the container vs its versions is 'awkward and complicates both schemata and queries.' Sciore's fix (version model built INTO the data model, EXTRA-V) folds versioning into the object type itself rather than wrapping it. Warning for us: if our container and revision are modeled as two distinct kernel object types, every query and schema pays a join tax. Consider Adele's resolution (common attrs shared by all revisions, modifiable revision-specific attrs, immutable attrs whose update mints a new revision).
  • The code-identity question (file vs path vs FQN vs AST vs git-hash; atomic unit = file or function) — Offers a mechanism, not a single answer. Sciore decouples identity from any one coordinate: a versioned item has identity, and a specific version is selected by a point in a multidimensional coordinate space. Mapped to us: a code unit's identity is the CONTAINER; file/path/FQN/AST-hash/git-hash become DIMENSIONS (coordinates) along which versions vary, not competing identities. This dissolves the either/or: you don't pick one identity criterion, you make several orthogonal selection axes. Caveat: this is inference — Sciore's dimensions are variant attributes (os/db/window-system), and I am analogizing them to our identity coordinates.
  • Versioning as its own layer (arrival-time history vs committed/minted versions; partial vs full persistence) — Names the deeper distinction we need. The survey's extensional vs intensional split maps onto our two times. Extensional versioning ('V defined by enumerating members v1..vn, each immutable, identified by a unique number, checked in once') = our explicit COMMITTED/MINTED git-style versions. Intensional versioning ('V = {v | c(v)}', constructed on demand from a predicate/query) is a SECOND axis we did not yet name. Sciore's contribution is that a configuration is an intensional query against the version space, not a stored list. Warns us: Rama's arrival-time log alone gives only extensional history; branchable committed versions need an explicit immutability/mint operation, and declarative configuration needs an intensional selection layer on top.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (a grouping/local-world has its own version history; member binding pinned/floating/effectivity-scoped) — This is the paper's center of gravity (CAD/CASE configuration management) and directly validates us. A composite object/configuration is itself a versioned item with its own history, AND its binding to members is a separate concern. Sciore's declarative configurations let the grouping specify members by predicate over coordinates (floating/effectivity-scoped) rather than by pinned IDs (extensional). The survey: configuration construction 'may be viewed as a selection against a versioned object base... directed by configuration rules.' So 'pinned' = extensional binding, 'floating/condition-scoped' = intensional binding — and Sciore's whole point is you want BOTH expressible declaratively in one query language (EXCESS).
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-type / node-data, must compose) — Supports composition; warns about the type explosion. Sciore's grid model shows why per-branch variant modeling explodes: 'b = |A1|...|An|' — with 3 OS x 3 DB x 3 window = 27 branches. Our four levels are four such dimensions; if we model their combinations as explicit branches they multiply combinatorially. Sciore's answer — an n-dimensional coordinate space with intensional selection — is precisely how to make four levels COMPOSE without enumerating the cross-product. The relation-type level versioning especially benefits: visibility of a relation can be made coordinate-conditioned (cf. COV: 'a relationship is visible only when both ends belong to the currently selected version').
  • The trail-to-code join (resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment; DECLARED vs INFERRED change) — Maps to extensional retrieval + the source/derived distinction. Resolving a trail's (path,timestamp) to the immutable revision at that moment is exactly extensional versioning: 'supports retrieval of previously constructed versions... each identified by a unique number... made immutable when checked in.' Our DECLARED-vs-INFERRED split aligns with the survey's source-object (created by a human/agent, here the agent DECLARES the edit) vs derived-object (reconstructed automatically by a tool — INFERRED by diffing). The kernel rule 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest' matches treating derived objects as recomputable projections, never as checked-in source revisions.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality (conflicting views representable, synthesis late-bound) — Partial support via variants-not-merged. The survey notes that with branch/merge 'the branches are not joined; rather, each of them continues to exist' — co-present alternatives, our plurality. Sciore's multidimensional variant space is the cleaner mechanism: divergent views become distinct coordinates in the same version set rather than overwrites, and a 'configuration' (synthesis) is a late-bound intensional query that picks/combines among them. This is inference-adjacent: Sciore targets engineering variants (os/db), not epistemic disagreement, but the structural move (preserve as orthogonal coordinates, resolve by query at read time) is exactly late-bound consensus.
  • Substrate choices (Rama event-sourced; version model relative to the data model) — Gives us a design-axis taxonomy. The survey's three architectures — version-model-ON-TOP-of, BUILT-INTO, and data-model-on-top-of-version-model — is a direct decision frame for Rama. Rama-as-substrate with a versioning layer above is 'version model on top of the data model' (simple, but 'no support for storing versions efficiently, transaction manager unaware of versioning'). Sciore's EXTRA-V chose BUILT-INTO for query convenience. Warns us: bolting git-style versions purely on top of Rama PStates risks the inefficiencies the survey names; if versioning is first-class we may want it woven into the kernel object type (container with versioned attributes) rather than as an external wrapper.

Takeaway. Sciore's single most important contribution for us: model a versioned thing as ONE container holding a multidimensional version SET, where every axis of variation (revision/time, variant, platform — for us: path, FQN, AST, git-hash, graph/node/relation/data level) is an ORTHOGONAL COORDINATE rather than a branch, and make a 'configuration' a DECLARATIVE (intensional) query over those coordinates instead of a pinned parts list. This is the structural escape from the combinatorial branch explosion our four-level graph versioning would otherwise hit (b ≈ product of dimension cardinalities), and it cleanly separates extensional retrieval (resolve trail path+timestamp to the immutable revision-at-that-moment) from intensional selection (a floating/effectivity-scoped grouping). The load-bearing warning: Sciore argues a versioned object and its versions should NOT be separate types — fold versioning into the kernel object so schemata and queries don't pay a perpetual join tax, which directly challenges any design that makes our container and revision two distinct Rama object types.

24. Configuration Management of Industrial Products in PDM/PLM

Olivier Rey · 2021 · Author homepage (orey.github.io), technical essay, GNU FDL V3

In one line. An industrial PLM/PDM essay arguing that dynamic filtering-based configuration management (serial-number effectivity + recomputed assemblies) is a structural source of unreliability, and that static 'wired' graph data — explicit, persistent, immutable version-to-version links — is the only configuration model that secures past configurations forever, supports branching, and enables the digital twin.

Source access: full. Read the full article text via WebFetch directly from the author's open homepage https://orey.github.io/papers/articles/conf-mgt/ (Olivier Rey, July 2021, GNU FDL V3). Not paywalled. The fetch reproduced all section headings and substantive content including the Figure 6/7 examples; I did not see the figures themselves, so Figure descriptions rely on the surrounding prose.

The paper, section by section

§ Introduction. Frames CM in industrial PLM/PDM as a contrast between dynamic filtering-based CM and static graph-based CM. The thesis: software engineering matured its configuration practices while traditional manufacturing still relies on outdated CM that impedes digital advancement and limits digital-twin capability. The core operational goal of CM is stated precisely — obtain the exact applicable parts/design-solutions for a given product instance while managing options and tracking change across the lifecycle. The complete tree is the '150% vision': it holds every option, but no single instance uses all of them.

“To be able to get the exact list of applicable parts (or applicable design solutions) for a certain instance of the product”
“the 150% vision of the product”

§ The Product Tree. Products are modeled as trees whose leaves are parts/design solutions, assembled from multiple viewpoints: functional/systems-engineering splits, regulatory structures (ATA codes in aerospace), organizational divisions, and the PDM tool's own technical constraints. The over-complete tree sets up the central problem: how to extract the real per-instance configuration from a tree that contains more than 100% of any actual product.

“The complete product tree contains the 150% vision of the product—all options, though not all apply simultaneously to any single instance.”

§ Manual Version Management. Legacy PDM systems have no native version management; new versions are created by copy-and-rename with version numbers — a manual discipline. The bicycle-brake example: when a supplier discontinues a brake, a new design solution V2 is created while V1 is preserved to support already-manufactured units; manufacturing switches from V1 to V2 only when old stock is exhausted. Configuration changes thus have immediate physical industrial consequences (stock, support), not just data consequences.

“When design solutions change, new versions must be manually created by copying and renaming with version numbers.”

§ Configuration Management Using Links (S/N effectivity). The traditional mechanism: applicability is attached to product-tree LINKS as serial-number ranges. Brakes V1 apply to S/N [1,N], V2 to [N+1, infinity). Filtering the complete tree with a specific S/N yields that instance's options; filtering with a future S/N can return ALL options (over 100% of the product). This is the canonical serial-number-effectivity model and the prototype of the 'dynamic filter' the paper attacks.
