The Product Catalog complements it: external compatibility tables encoding non-trivial interdependent option rules, binding S/Ns to option lists.

“Brakes V1 are applicable to bicycle which S/N is in the range [1, N], and brakes V2 are applicable to the products which S/N is in the range [N+1, infinity[.”
“non trivial rules that depend on one another”

§ Managing the Changes / Change tracking. Components are versioned and change-tracking is fundamental because of industrial impact on stock, procurement, manufacturing. Change management justifies modifications and masters their consequences. In aerospace it carries certification weight: change tracking proves certification inheritance, and a significant change triggers re-certification. This establishes change as a first-class, regulated object, setting up the aerospace 'stack of changes' model.

“In aerospace, change tracking proves certification inheritance; significant changes require re-certification.”

§ Applicability Through Changes (stacking) and the no-fork failure mode. In aerospace the product is 'seen through changes' rather than as physical components: Change 1 creates Brakes V1, Change 2 retrofits V1 (applicable only when both Change 1 AND 2 apply), Change 3 replaces with V2. A product becomes 'a set of changes', requiring 'complex calculation' to replay the iterative sequence. Key warning: recomputing a past configuration gives NO guarantee of matching what was actually delivered if business rules changed. The model also establishes lineage that typically PREVENTS forks — a fork from V1 while Change 4 targets V2 'cannot be represented', forcing every later change to be manually undone ('a manual, costly and error prone process'). This is the paper's strongest demonstration of why a linearizing history substrate becomes unusable.

“calculating past configurations offers no guarantee of matching originally-delivered configurations if business rules change—leading to perceived PDM unreliability”
“The model establishes lineage between changes, typically preventing forks.”
“all changes must be manually undone—a manual, costly and error prone process”

§ From PDM to CLM. Stacking-based change management degrades PDM into Change Lifecycle Management (CLM), where the change is central and the product secondary — the inverse of true PLM where 'the product is at the center and the change on a secondary level.' This inversion 'closes the door to static graph data usage' and cascades problems through manufacturing. The product-first vs change-first framing becomes the paper's organizing axis.

“the product is at the center and the change on a secondary level”
“closes the door to static graph data usage in PLM”

§ Limitations of Filtering-Based CM + workarounds. Filtering-based systems (S/N applicability or change-based) are 'structural sources of problems' rooted in 1990s PDM limits; modern graph-data PLM enables 'static configuration management', the only approach supporting digital twins. Central question: how can you work on the digital product if your view of it is a non-persistent dynamic-filtering calculation? Concurrent engineering breaks immediately — a component applicable at T0 but inapplicable at T1 forces finding a replacement and migrating all attached manufacturing/support data, binding downstream teams to engineering's rhythm. The common workaround — caching intermediate configuration snapshots with timestamps in separate databases — is exactly an ad-hoc persistence layer compensating for the missing wired graph.

“How can you work on the digital product if the vision you have of the product is the result of a dynamic filtering calculation that will not be persistent enough to enable the creation of the many links you need?”
“structural sources of problems”

§ The New Way — Reuse, Interfaces, Multi-level Tree. Modern manufacturing shifted from making all components to assembling supplier subsystems, requiring compatibility through well-defined interfaces (mechanical, electrical, fluid) that enable component reuse and specialization; like software, lifecycles carry many component versions in series production. CM must operate at MULTIPLE levels: component (variants/options), product (which components apply), and intermediate 'big components' aggregating smaller ones. Figure 6 distinguishes internally-mastered design solutions (blue), small assemblies SA (yellow, only SA2 internal), and big assemblies BA (purple, BA1/BA2 internal, BA3 straight from suppliers) — making the multi-level hierarchy explicit. Drawback noted: optimizing within an existing supply chain can suppress global innovation.

“mastering supplier component compatibility through defined interfaces—mechanical, electrical, fluid-based”
“big components aggregating smaller ones (cars, ships, aircraft)”

§ Adding Versions to the Product Tree. Component versions are folded into the tree, showing each component's full lifecycle with explicit change linkages. A propagation convention: any change to a lower component version implies a version change of the components above it. Figure 7: DS2 v1.0→v1.1 is tagged by Change 1a2c3; SA2 uses DS2 v1.1 in its v1.18/v2.0 and DS2 v2.0 in its v12.0/v12.1; black arrows are structural dependencies. The product head P points to all component heads (latest), though not all apply to every instance — distinguishing 'head/floating' from 'wired/pinned'.

“any lower component version change implies upper component version changes”
“Product head P points to all component heads (latest versions), though not all apply to every product instance.”

§ Materializing the S/N in the Product Tree. The crux of the static model. Product instance S/N 1212 version v234.3 points to specific BA1 v25.4 and BA2 v56.6, which point to contained small assemblies, which point to design solutions — a fully resolved, wired graph head. There is NO calculation mechanism: the S/N version points to existing, fully-defined component versions. Upgrades mint a NEW S/N version pointing to updated components, and once wired a version never changes — ten years later that subgraph is identical. This is the paper's definition of immutable, content-pinned, time-stable versions.

“There are no more calculation mechanism: The S/N 1212 in version v234.3 is pointing to existing versions of big assemblies that are fully defined.”
“When a version is 'wired' with links, it will never change in the future, and ten years later, this past part of the graph will be exactly the same.”

§ Applicability and Configuration (static). Applicability splits into 'real usage' (this S/N genuinely uses this component version — de facto applicable) and 'future applicability' (components available for future use, an open choice since all releases stay accessible). Configuration needs no algorithm: each S/N version is itself a component-tree head extending to the needed depth, so every configuration is already 'solved'. A study S/N (XXX01) can fork back to a past version (DS2 v1.2) for analysis/innovation. The decisive payoff: all S/N configurations are 'secured forever', enabling true concurrent engineering — manufacturing assembles SA2 v12.0 while engineering develops DS2 v2.1 with no conflict.

“All versions of the S/N configuration are secured forever inside the system.”
“Different departments work on different component versions without conflict.”

§ Change Management (graph model). Changes tag the LINK between two specific component versions; the old aggregate multi-component before/after change 'does not exist anymore' — changes restrict to single-component transitions, while an assembly version may simply embed different sub-components. Computing a configuration delta vs a baseline becomes graph analysis: aggregate all changes that produced all versions of all sub-components (SA2 v11.8→v12.0, change 3df59 = union of 12bb5, aze129, 1a44ho). Comparison tools allow direct version-to-version analysis (DS2 v2.0 vs v1.1). The two structural differences from filtering: configurations are solved/explicit, and change-sets are computed by graph diff rather than rule replay — 'the product comes first and the change comes second'.

“changes tag links between component versions”
“Change 3df59 corresponds to the union of changes 12bb5, aze129, and 1a44ho.”
“The product comes first and the change comes second. A system with this kind of management can be called a product lifecycle management system.”

§ Product Catalog, PLM Features, Digital Twin, Conclusion. A 'solved graph' holding all design solutions, assemblies and serial numbers in all versions trivially generates catalogs (applicable element heads at a chosen time/granularity plus compatibility tables). Baselines + option choices let an S/N be minted directly as a new graph head — 'the direct fruit of the product catalog' — giving a non-ambiguous link across marketing, engineering, manufacturing, support. Graph data unlock software practices: branching/forking (retrofits as parallel branches, merges with defined 3D/BOM/document strategies) and 'studies' (temporary products from future, fake, or old components). The whole PLM graph IS the product line's digital twin; each S/N is one product's twin. Conclusion: filtering brings problems that worsen downstream; static graph-based CM secures all data, stays simple, and enables concurrent engineering — 'the graph data are at the core of the PLM systems.'

“The S/N, in its first version, is the direct fruit of the product catalog.”
“The complete PLM graph represents the product line's digital twin, containing all possible products.”
“The graph data are at the core of the PLM systems.”

How it maps to our parts

  • Object-container kernel (stable container = identity thread; revisions = immutable values; current-revision = state; 'inferred structure is never accepted as truth at ingest') — STRONGLY SUPPORTS and gives a name. The paper's 'wired static graph' IS the container-with-immutable-revisions pattern: an S/N version 'is pointing to existing versions of big assemblies that are fully defined' and 'when a version is wired with links, it will never change in the future.' The S/N (container) threads identity over time; each version points to specific, frozen component versions (immutable revisions). Critically, the paper's central warning — that dynamic filtering 'calculates' configuration and 'offers no guarantee of matching originally-delivered configurations if business rules change' — is the industrial proof of our hard rule: inferred/computed structure must never be treated as committed truth. Wire it explicitly or you cannot trust it ten years later.
  • Versioning as its own layer: Rama arrival-time history (partial persistence) vs explicit git-style COMMITTED/MINTED versions (full persistence); two times = when-it-arrived vs when-it-was-committed — DIRECTLY VALIDATES the two-times distinction and warns against relying only on the derived one. Filtering-based CM = reconstructing-from-a-log-and-rules (arrival-time/derived); graph-based CM = explicitly minted, branchable versions (committed/full persistence). The paper shows a purely recomputed view is unreliable because the recomputation rules drift ('perceived PDM unreliability'). Lesson: Rama's arrival-time log is necessary but NOT sufficient as truth; we need explicitly minted committed versions ('wired' heads) you can branch from. This is the strongest external argument we have for why the committed-version layer must exist on top of Rama's default history.
  • Groupings as versioned objects (a grouping/local-world has its own version history, partly independent of members; member binding pinned vs floating vs effectivity-scoped) — GIVES A MECHANISM and the exact pinned-vs-floating vocabulary. Assemblies (SA/BA) are groupings with their OWN version lifecycle: 'any lower component version change implies upper component version changes' yet an assembly version can 'embed different sub-components than previous versions' and skip member versions (SA2 jumps DS2 versions). 'Pinned' = wired S/N pointing to fully-defined component versions. 'Floating' = product head P 'points to all component heads (latest versions).' 'Effectivity/condition-scoped' = serial-number-range applicability ('S/N in range [1,N]'). The paper proves a grouping's history is partly independent of members and shows how change-aggregation rolls member changes up into the grouping's delta.
  • The code-identity question: is the atomic unit the file or the function; is identity the path, name/FQN, AST, or git hash? — OFFERS AN ANALOGY by structural inversion. PLM resolved 'is the unit the whole product or the component?' by making the COMPONENT the atomic identity-bearing unit with its own lifecycle, and the product an assembly of pinned component versions — 'the product comes first and the change comes second' only once components are first-class. Mapped to us: the FUNCTION (component) is the better atomic identity unit, with the FILE as an assembly/grouping that pins specific function versions. The paper warns the wrong choice (CLM: change-first instead of product-first) cascades problems — analogous to choosing git-hash/diff-first identity over a stable container, forcing 'complex calculation' to recover what a thing actually was.
  • The trail-to-code join: DECLARED change (agent says what it did) vs INFERRED change (reconstructed by diffing); resolve path+timestamp to code-at-that-moment — WARNS AGAINST inferred-by-diff and ENDORSES declared. The paper's whole thesis is that reconstructing 'what was true at a past moment' by recomputation/filtering is unreliable, while explicit declared links are 'secured forever.' Its change model tags an explicit link between two specific component versions ('changes tag links between component versions') — that is DECLARED change as a first-class edge, exactly our preferred trail-to-code join. Recovering configuration by re-diffing baselines is possible but is the costly, drift-prone path. Direct evidence to privilege the agent's declared edit-operation over diff-reconstruction when resolving a trail to code-at-that-moment.
  • Preserved disagreement / plurality; synthesis late-bound; branching/forking from past committed versions — SUPPORTS via forking and the named failure mode of NOT supporting it. Graph-CM enables 'branching/forking,' 'studies' (temporary products with fake/old/future components), and forking a study S/N back to a past version (DS2 v1.2) for innovation. The killer evidence is the failure mode of the filtering/stacking model: it 'typically prevents forks' — a fork from V1 while a later change targets V2 'cannot be represented,' forcing a 'manual, costly and error prone' undo of all changes. Concrete industrial proof that a substrate which silently linearizes history becomes unusable — exactly our case for representing plurality structurally rather than overwriting.
  • Four-level graph versioning (graph / node / relation-TYPE / node-DATA), and these levels must COMPOSE — PARTIAL MECHANISM. The paper composes versioning across structural levels: design-solution (node-data), small/big assemblies (node groupings), product head (graph), and changes that 'tag links' (relation/edge versioning). The change-aggregation rule ('SA2 v11.8 to v12.0 ... Change 3df59 = union of 12bb5, aze129, 1a44ho') is exactly level-composition: an edge/grouping version's delta composes from its members' deltas. It is weaker on relation-TYPE versioning (edges are mostly structural containment), so it confirms node + node-data + grouping + edge-instance composition but leaves relation-TYPE versioning as our own contribution.
  • Substrate choice: Rama (event-sourced) as ground truth, with graph data as the persistent core — SUPPORTS the substrate stance with a caveat. The paper's verdict — 'the graph data are at the core of the PLM systems' and only persistent graph data 'enables the creation of the many links you need' and digital-twin operations — endorses graph-as-truth over recompute-on-demand. The caveat for our Rama choice: an append-only event log is the substrate, but truth must be the explicitly wired graph projected/minted from it, not a query-time filter over the log. Otherwise we rebuild the filtering anti-pattern on a fancier engine.

Takeaway. The single most important contribution: an independent, decades-deep industrial validation of our object-container kernel and committed-version layer. Rey shows that reconstructing 'what a thing was at a past moment' by RECOMPUTING it from a log plus filtering rules (serial-number effectivity / change-stacking) is structurally unreliable — it cannot guarantee a match to what was actually delivered once rules drift, it cannot represent forks, and it forces costly manual undo — whereas explicitly WIRED, immutable version-to-version links ('a version, once wired, will never change; ten years later this subgraph is exactly the same') secure every configuration forever and make branching and concurrent work trivial. This is the strongest external argument that Rama's arrival-time log alone is not truth: we must mint explicit committed versions wired to specific revisions ('the product comes first, the change comes second'), and never accept a filter-/diff-reconstructed structure as truth at ingest. The assembly model also hands us a ready vocabulary for groupings-as-versioned-objects (pinned vs floating member binding, grouping deltas composed by aggregating member changes)."

What we can gather — Angle 5

This is the mature, boring, solved part — forty years of CAD/SCM/PLM that answers "groupings as versioned objects" directly — and it is the angle with the least to invent and the most to simply adopt.

The configuration is a first-class versioned object. Katz names it exactly: a configuration is "a binding between a version of a composite object and the versions of its components," and the composite "is a new version in its own right" even when its own contents did not change but a member did. The grouping has its own identity and its own history, partly independent of its members. Two further gifts from Katz: current ≠ last-created (the head pointer is deliberate minted state, never inferred from arrival order — our hard rule, stated in 1988), and equivalences — a declared relationship tying heterogeneous representations of the same real thing (layout/transistor/functional views), checked at verification time rather than merged. That is precisely our "the same understanding as code, as trail, as discourse node," with a 1988 mechanism.

Version the group, snapshot the members — because the alternative cascades. ORION tried versioning members (classes) independently and found that, because members reference each other, deriving one forces re-versioning the whole neighborhood — the version-proliferation failure mode, demonstrated. Its fix was to version the grouping (the whole schema lattice) as one object and snapshot members via copy-on-derive, with an inherited access scope that makes branch-local divergence visible only to its branch — non-destructive preserved disagreement. Static vs dynamic binding = our pinned vs floating.

Declarative configuration is the escape from combinatorial explosion. Sciore models versions as a multidimensional coordinate space and a configuration as a declarative (intensional) query over coordinates, not a pinned parts list. Our four levels are four such dimensions; intensional selection composes them without enumerating the cross-product (3 OS × 3 DB × 3 window = 27 branches, or one query). His load-bearing warning: do not make the container and the revision two separate object types — fold versioning into the kernel object, or every schema and query pays a perpetual join tax. That is a direct caution about how we model container-vs-revision in Rama.

And industry has already proven the deepest point. The PLM essay shows that reconstructing "what a product was at a past moment" by recomputing it from a log plus filtering rules (serial-number effectivity, change-stacking) is structurally unreliable once the rules drift, and cannot represent forks at all — whereas explicitly wired, immutable version-to-version links are "secured forever; ten years later this subgraph is exactly the same." This is the strongest external argument in the entire research that the arrival-time log alone is not truth: you must mint explicit committed versions wired to specific revisions, and never treat a filter-/diff-reconstructed structure as truth. Build the wired graph from the log; do not rebuild the filtering anti-pattern on a fancier engine.

What we gather: three binding modes (pinned / floating / effectivity-scoped); the configuration as one first-class noun that unifies our manifest, a git commit, a grouping, and a synthesized local world; version-the-group-snapshot-the-members to dodge the cascade; declarative intensional selection as the sophisticated regrouping end-state; and the join-tax warning about how we type container-vs-revision in the kernel.

What we can gather — overall

Reading all twenty-four together, the same conclusions arrive from independent directions — code systems, formal theory, production databases, graph stores, and forty years of mechanical-engineering PLM — which is the strongest kind of evidence we could have. Nine things are now load-bearing.

1. The architecture is convergent, not chosen. Immutable content-addressed values plus a thin mutable naming layer is where git, Irmin, Unison, Dolt, Noms, TerminusDB, Monticello, Molhado, Katz, and Conradi-Westfechtel all independently land. Our container/revision split is that pattern. We are implementing the answer, not gambling on one — and the one thing we add that most predecessors lacked is a natively event-sourced value layer (Rama), so we keep history by default instead of bolting it on.

2. Versioning is two times, formally distinct, and they must be combined. DSST (partial vs full persistence), XTDB (transaction vs valid time), and Conradi-Westfechtel (extensional vs intensional) are three names for one split. Rama gives us the first axis for free — the append-only arrival log. We must mint the second — committed, branchable, semantically-meaningful versions — as a deliberate act, and PLM proves the arrival log alone cannot be trusted as truth. The mint is an event; the object it produces is a configuration.

3. The function is the atomic unit; the file is an assembly. Angle 1 and Angle 5 fuse here. Every system that cared about code rejected file-granularity, and every system that cared about groupings made the member the identity-bearing unit and the grouping a binding over member versions. So a function is a container; a file is a configuration that pins function versions — and identity is a stable opaque handle, with path, name, content, and git-hash demoted to attributes or coordinates, never the identity itself.

4. Groupings are first-class versioned objects — version the group, snapshot the members. This is the most thoroughly solved part. Configuration-as-object (Katz), the cascade/proliferation failure mode of doing otherwise (ORION, Conradi-Westfechtel), three binding modes — pinned / floating / effectivity-scoped (Katz, ORION, PLM, DSST's valid intervals) — and declarative intensional selection as the end-state (Sciore). One noun, configuration, unifies our version-manifest, a git commit, a curated grouping, and a synthesized local world.

5. Declared beats inferred — and Softland uniquely owns the declared channel. This is not merely our safety rule; the patch theories show it is the foundation a correct version model is built on (you cannot recover the morphism from snapshots without lossy inference), MolhadoRef dogfooded it (declared refactorings preserved history that diffing lost), and Katz and PLM both insist derivation must be a declared first-class edge because naming and arrival data cannot reconstruct it. The asymmetry that no prior system had: agent reasoning trails are a declared-operation log — edits with intent narrated in language. MolhadoRef needed to own an IDE to get this; we get it because agents already make and narrate most edits. This is the single biggest structural advantage the research surfaces.

6. Preserved disagreement can be a data structure. Pijul and the categorical theory prove a conflict is a first-class value — a transitive relation, not a linear list — and tell us the PState schema for a conflicted unit must store a relation. XTDB (additive correction), ORION (access-scope branch isolation), and every branch/merge system show divergence held without overwrite. Synthesis is late-bound — and total automatic merge is the explicitly unsolved frontier (DSST's confluent persistence, the homotopical theory's incomplete merge), so we design for held plurality and human-or-late synthesis, and never promise clean auto-merge.

7. Four-level graph versioning has a blueprint and three hard constraints. You provably need both a member track and a group track, linked (the 5-tuple theorem; Wikidata's braid). Store facts once with validity ranges. Mint at commit-grain with adaptive snapshot anchors, or hit the version-count wall (21k versions = system failure). And relation-TYPE / schema versioning is the one level nobody in the literature solved — it is our genuine contribution, and Sciore's coordinate-space + intensional-selection is the most promising tool for making the levels compose without combinatorial explosion.

8. The warnings to encode now, before code. Don't model container and revision as two separate Rama object types (Sciore's join tax) — prefer Adele's shape: one item with common attributes, revision-specific attributes, and immutable attributes whose update mints a revision. Budget ~5x overhead for the committed layer (Irmin). LCA discovery is your problem, not the merge function's (Irmin). Don't rebuild filtering-as-truth on Rama (PLM) — truth is the wired graph minted from the log, not a query over it. And don't let delta chains grow unbounded — re-snapshot on a metric trigger (OSTRICH, BEAR, the RDF framework).

9. The reordering this whole exercise earns. The versioning vocabulary — item/OID/VID, configuration, mint, the two times, bound/generic/effectivity, partial vs full persistence, VM/DM/VQ — is kernel physics, and it belongs in a kernel-physics document above the ingestor contracts. The code ingestor is merely the first place the question got loud; Roam, groupings, and the four-level graph all route through the same nouns, and the 5-tuple theorem proves the group track cannot be safely retrofitted. Settling the vocabulary is cheap and on paper; building the mechanism should still wait for a consumer to pull it.

What this changes for the code-ingestor slice 1

The research validates more than it overturns. Stable container + revisions = OID/VID, confirmed ~ten times. Commit-grain minting = Katz's "semantically meaningful snapshot, not every update," and the version-count wall actively vindicates it over save-grain. Refusing git's rename heuristic is right because the correct answer is declaration, not better inference (MolhadoRef, Pijul, Katz, PLM all agree). Whole-file artifacts now with forms later = MolhadoRef's own deliberate granularity floor. The earned amendments, each now with citations behind it:

  1. Identity = opaque stable handle, not content-hash (Molhado over Unison) — keep a content hash as a derived attribute for dedup and the exactness flag, not as identity. Same bytes at a new path = same value, new occurrence: half of rename continuity as fact, not inference (Irmin/Prolly/Unison dedup).
  2. Promote the version-manifest to a first-class configuration object (Katz) — it is simultaneously the group-version thread and the noun the future Softland-native commit needs; unify them now at zero cost. Version the group, snapshot the members (ORION) to avoid cascade.
  3. Make the two times explicit (XTDB) — commit author-time (valid) vs ingest-time (transaction); the exactness flag becomes a projection of this, and corrected resolutions are additive, not overwriting.
  4. Adopt the VM/DM/VQ read vocabulary (OSTRICH) and validity-range storage (Wikidata) for the code-at-time / trail-resolution projections.
  5. Snapshot the name/path→identity table as-of each trail moment (Unison's namespace-version) so a trail's recorded path resolves later even after renames.
  6. For the regrouping vision: composition model only — pinned / floating / effectivity-scoped selections (Katz/ORION/PLM/Sciore), never total versioning (proliferation), never a single global version space (Molhado's inexpressible cross-version reference, which would kill anchors and trails).
  7. Don't make container and revision separate kernel types (Sciore) — fold versioning into the item; this is a Rama PState modeling decision to make before writing the adapter.

The open frontier the literature itself hands us, unsolved by anyone: semantic merge (declared refactorings × ordinary edits), relation-TYPE versioning (the fourth graph level), confluent/late-bound synthesis of preserved plurality, and history-length-independent performance. These are not gaps in our design; they are gaps in the field — and three of the four are exactly the places where having agents (declared intent) and LLMs (semantic judgment) in the substrate is the new leverage none of these systems had.
